Topic: a story about an unusual holiday trip

Text:
"Last summer, we rented a small house inside a quiet village for our holiday. Normally, we drive our own car, but this time, the owner suggested borrowing his old van—a method he swore was ‘fun.’ On the first day, we climbed a steep hill, and my stomach turned upside down! ‘No problem,’ he laughed. ‘We’ll take the back roads—informal rules only!’ His suggestion worked, but the journey was bumpy. Later, a guest asked, ‘Why this trip?’ The owner grinned: ‘Because normally, holidays are boring!’ We left with funny stories and full hearts."

Questions:
Q1: Why did the group borrow the owner’s van instead of using their own car?
Correct: The owner recommended it as a fun way to experience their holiday.
Distractors:
1. They didn’t want to pay for parking in the village. / 2. Their car couldn’t climb the hill. / 3. They wanted to try something different because the owner insisted.
Explanation:
The correct answer captures the suggestion and informal context. Distractor 1 is irrelevant; distractor 2 contradicts the text (the hill was a result of the van’s route); distractor 3 is partially true but overstates the owner’s insistence.
---
Q2: What did the owner’s phrase ‘No problem’ imply about his attitude?
Correct: He was confident they could solve the issue with a simple fix.
Distractors:
1. He didn’t think the stomach issue was serious. / 2. He planned to cancel the trip. / 3. He had a secret shortcut to avoid the hill.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects the method (back roads) and informal tone. Distractor 1 ignores the solution; distractor 2 contradicts the resolution; distractor 3 is unsupported.
---
Q3: What best describes the guests’ overall impression of the holiday?
Correct: They thought it was unusual and enjoyable.
Distractors:
1. They regretted not bringing their own car. / 2. They were surprised the owner was so relaxed. / 3. They wanted to repeat the trip but with a different owner.
Explanation:
The correct answer matches the conclusion ("funny stories and full hearts"). Distractor 1 contradicts the own car context; distractor 2 is irrelevant; distractor 3 is unsupported.



Topic: a story about an unusual family celebration

Text:
The Smith family had an unusual profile—neither of their celebrations looked like anyone else’s. Instead of gifts, they enjoyed a variety of funny activities. His job was to organize everything, but this year, no one knew the direction of the party. They argued about the decorations until the room was completely dark except for a single light. Then, they remembered their deep family tradition: writing essays about their favorite memories. Laughing, they read them aloud. The experience was so unique that they decided to keep it every year.

Questions:
Q1: What did the Smith family do instead of giving traditional gifts?
Correct: They wrote funny essays about their favorite memories.
Distractors:
1. They played board games all night. / They gave each other money. / They sang songs together.
Explanation:
The correct answer matches the text’s detail about "writing essays." Distractors are wrong because:
- Board games/singing are unrelated activities.
- Money contradicts the "unusual" tradition.
Weak readers might pick these if they skim and assume "funny activities" = games/singing.
---
Q2: Why was the room completely dark during the celebration?
Correct: They turned off all the lights except one as part of their tradition.
Distractors:
1. They forgot to turn on the lights. / It was a power outage. / They wanted to scare each other.
Explanation:
The correct answer explains the intentional darkness ("single light"). Distractors are wrong because:
- Forgot/outage ignores the text’s control over light.
- Scare is unsupported (no fear mentioned).
Weak readers might choose these if they overlook the tradition’s role.
---
Q3: How did the Smiths feel about their unusual tradition at the end?
Correct: They enjoyed it so much they decided to continue it.
Distractors:
1. They were confused and stopped the tradition. / They argued and went home early. / They cried because it was too strange.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects "laughed" and "keep it every year." Distractors are wrong because:
- Confused/stopped contradicts the happy ending.
- Cried is emotionally opposite to the text.
Weak readers might pick these if they focus only on the argument, not the resolution.



Topic: a description of a morning routine that makes me happy

Text:
My morning routine starts with a wow—waking up to the calm lake outside my window. The first step is to step outside, breathe in the fresh air, and feel my heart smile. I always take a short walk to reach the shore, where I pick up trash to protect the water. It’s a small habit, but it makes me happy. After that, I do some light exercise—nothing too general or fancy. The best part? There are no celebrities here, just me and nature. By the time I finish, everything feels all right. This simple routine fills my heart with joy every day.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the writer pick up trash in the morning?
Correct: To keep the lake clean and healthy.
Distractors:
1. To prepare for a celebrity visit. / Wrong: Text says "no celebrities here."
2. Because their doctor told them to exercise more. / Wrong: Text doesn’t mention doctors.
3. To win an environmental award. / Wrong: Text focuses on personal joy, not awards.

Explanation:
The correct answer rephrases "protect the lake." Distractors 1–3 ignore the text’s emphasis on simplicity ("small habit") and lack of external goals.
---
Q2: What makes the writer’s morning routine special?
Correct: It’s peaceful and connected to nature.
Distractors:
1. They meet famous people every morning. / Wrong: Text says "no celebrities."
2. They follow a strict schedule. / Wrong: Text describes it as "simple."
3. They only exercise indoors. / Wrong: Text mentions walking "outside" to the shore.

Explanation:
The correct answer captures "me and nature" and "calm lake." Distractors 1–3 misread the text’s emphasis on simplicity and outdoor activity.
---
Q3: What does the writer NOT do in their morning routine?
Correct: They don’t do complicated workouts.
Distractors:
1. They don’t go outside at all. / Wrong: Text says "step outside."
2. They don’t enjoy the view of the lake. / Wrong: Text says "waking up to the lake."
3. They don’t pick up trash. / Wrong: Text explicitly mentions this habit.

Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases "light exercise—nothing too general." Distractors 1–3 contradict clear text details ("outside," "lake," "pick up trash").



Topic: a funny story that happened to my friends and me

Text:
Last summer, my friends and I went to a camp in the mountains. We packed food, but my twin brother forgot to check the bags—we had double the sandwiches! While cooking, I dropped a plate, and it crashed loudly. My friend Jake laughed and said, "That noise will increase the fun!" Then, we met a stranger who looked like a celebrity, so we joked about it. Later, we had a silly argument about who was the funniest. My friend Lisa said, "You all have such a funny character!" Finally, we sat by the fire, chatted until midnight, and agreed it was the sweetest trip ever.

Questions:
Q1: What happened because of the twin brother’s mistake?
Correct: They had twice as much food as planned.
Distractors:
1. "They forgot to bring drinks." (Wrong—text mentions food only.)
2. "The plate broke into pieces." (Wrong—text says it "crashed loudly," not necessarily broke.)
3. "They argued about the food." (Wrong—argument was later, about funniness.)
Explanation: The text clearly states "double the sandwiches," so the correct answer refers to extra food. Distractors 1–3 misrepresent details (drinks, plate breakage, argument timing).
---
Q2: Why did Jake laugh after the plate crashed?
Correct: He thought the noise would make the trip more enjoyable.
Distractors:
1. "He was scared of the noise." (Wrong—text says he laughed.)
2. "He wanted more food." (Wrong—no mention of hunger.)
3. "He thought it was dangerous." (Wrong—text implies it was funny.)
Explanation: The text says "That noise will increase the fun," so Jake found it amusing. Distractors assume fear, hunger, or danger, which contradict the playful tone.
---
Q3: How did Lisa describe her friends’ personalities?
Correct: She said they all had a humorous nature.
Distractors:
1. "They were very serious." (Wrong—text says "funny character.")
2. "Lisa was the funniest." (Wrong—text describes all friends as funny.)
3. "They had bad temperaments." (Wrong—text says "funny," not angry.)
Explanation: The text uses "funny character," so the correct answer paraphrases this. Distractors 1–3 misinterpret the tone (seriousness, individual focus, anger).



Topic: an adventure story about getting lost in the forest

Text:
Liam loved exploring the forest in the autumn season. One day, he forgot his equipment and got lost. He felt nervous and almost gave up, but then he saw a red sock hanging from a tree. "Maybe this is mine!" he thought. Following the direct path the sock pointed to, he found a small clearing—but no exit. Suddenly, he remembered his dad’s gift: a walkie-talkie. He called for help, and soon, rangers found him. "Next time, I need to check my bag!" Liam laughed. The forest wasn’t so scary after all.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Liam feel nervous in the forest?
Correct: He was lost and had forgotten his flashlight.
Distractors:
1. He saw a wild animal. / Wrong: No animals are mentioned.
2. His friend left him alone. / Wrong: The story is about Liam’s mistake, not friends.
3. It was too hot outside. / Wrong: The text says it was autumn (cold).
---
Q2: What did the red sock help Liam do?
Correct: It showed him a direct path to a clearing.
Distractors:
1. It led him straight to his car. / Wrong: The sock only led to a clearing, not home.
2. He used it to signal for help. / Wrong: He used the walkie-talkie, not the sock.
3. It was a clue to find food. / Wrong: The text doesn’t mention food.
---
Q3: How did Liam finally get home safely?
Correct: He used his walkie-talkie to call for help from the rangers.
Distractors:
1. He followed the sock all the way home. / Wrong: The sock only led to a clearing.
2. A stranger gave him a ride. / Wrong: The text says rangers found him.
3. He walked until he saw a road. / Wrong: He didn’t walk far; he called for help immediately.



Topic: an adventure story about finding a secret door in the house

Text:
Mr. Carter, a businessman, moved into an old house. He was weak and bored, but one day, he found a novel under the floorboards. The book was creative and full of strange stories. He felt lucky and started to recognize clues. Inside, there was a cartoon of a keyhole—just like a mark on the wall! He looked for evidence and saw dust marks near the fireplace. His relationship with the house’s secrets grew stronger. Finally, he pushed the panel—it opened! Behind it was a secret door. The adventure changed himself forever.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Mr. Carter feel lucky after finding the novel?
Correct: He thought the novel was strange and creative, and it gave him clues to explore.
Distractors:
1. He thought the novel was valuable. / Wrong: The text says it was "creative and strange," not valuable.
2. The novel had his name in it. / Wrong: No personal details are mentioned.
3. He saw a map to treasure. / Wrong: Only a cartoon and dust marks were clues.

Explanation:
The correct answer matches the text’s description of the novel as "creative and strange" and its role in "recognize[ing] clues." Distractors 1–3 add details not in the text (value, personal connection, treasure map), which a careless reader might invent.
---
Q2: What evidence did he find to support the idea of a secret door?
Correct: He saw dust marks near the fireplace and a cartoon of a keyhole that matched a mark on the wall.
Distractors:
1. A hidden key under the floor. / Wrong: The text mentions no key.
2. A ghost showed him the door. / Wrong: No supernatural elements are described.
3. The house’s owner left a note. / Wrong: No owner or note is mentioned.

Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases the text’s "dust marks... cartoon... mark on the wall." Distractors 1–3 introduce unrelated ideas (key, ghost, note), which might confuse readers who focus on partial details (e.g., "cartoon" → assume a ghost).
---
Q3: How did the adventure change Mr. Carter?
Correct: It made him braver and more curious.
Distractors:
1. He started collecting old books. / Wrong: No hobby change is mentioned.
2. He became scared of the house. / Wrong: The text implies a positive change ("changed himself forever").
3. He sold the house for a lot of money. / Wrong: No financial outcome is given.

Explanation:
The correct answer infers the text’s "changed himself" as a positive shift (bravery/curiosity). Distractors 1–3 misread the text’s vague ending: a reader might assume negative outcomes (fear) or unrelated actions (collecting books) without close reading.



Topic: an adventure story about finding a treasure

Text:
One day, Leo found an old advertising poster in his grandfather’s attic. It showed a king holding a golden key and suggested a treasure hidden near the sea. In his pocket, Leo discovered a round coin with strange symbols. His friend Mia said, “Maybe the treasure is near the big stone column by the waves!”

They traveled to the coast, where the waves crashed against the rocks. Behind the column, they saw a chest full of old possessions. Leo had to sort through broken cups and rusty swords before finding a map. The final clue led them to a cave—inside, they found the treasure: a chest of gold and jewels, the king’s lost possession!

Questions:
Q1: Why did Leo and Mia think the treasure was near the stone column?
Correct: The advertising poster suggested the treasure was near the sea, and the column was by the waves.
Distractors:
1. The coin in Leo’s pocket had a map showing the column.
2. Mia’s friend told them the treasure was under the column.
3. The advertising poster had a picture of the column.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text states the poster suggested the treasure was near the sea, and the column was by the waves.
- Distractor 1: Wrong because the coin had symbols, not a map.
- Distractor 2: Wrong because the text mentions Mia, not a friend.
- Distractor 3: Wrong because the poster showed a king, not the column.
---
Q2: What did Leo have to do with the old possessions before finding the map?
Correct: He had to sort through broken cups and rusty swords.
Distractors:
1. He had to clean the possessions to find the map.
2. He had to count all the possessions before looking for the map.
3. He had to hide the possessions before the map appeared.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text says Leo had to sort through the possessions.
- Distractor 1: Wrong because the text says sort, not clean.
- Distractor 2: Wrong because there’s no mention of counting.
- Distractor 3: Wrong because the map was found after sorting, not hidden.
---
Q3: How did Leo know the treasure belonged to a king?
Correct: The poster showed a king holding a golden key, and the treasure was described as the king’s lost possession.
Distractors:
1. The coin in Leo’s pocket had the king’s name on it.
2. The chest was labeled with the king’s name.
3. Mia recognized the treasure because she was a historian.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text explains the poster showed a king and the treasure was the king’s possession.
- Distractor 1: Wrong because the coin had symbols, not a name.
- Distractor 2: Wrong because the chest was full of possessions, not labeled.
- Distractor 3: Wrong because Mia is Leo’s friend, not a historian.



Topic: a story about a day when everything went wrong but ended well

Text:
It was autumn, the season of golden leaves, but my day started badly. I had a headache and thought I had an illness. My little brother was active—he ran everywhere! My task was to carry groceries, but the bus moved at speed—too fast! I dropped everything. Actually, I needed help. Just then, a kind soldier stopped and gave us a ride. We laughed about our silly day. Later, we ended up at a cozy bar. The music was lovely, and we stayed until midnight. Everything went wrong, but it ended well!

Questions:
Q1: Why did the narrator think they were sick?
Correct: Because of their headache.
Distractors:
1. Their brother was too noisy. / Wrong: The text says the brother was active, not that noise made them sick.
2. The bus was too fast. / Wrong: The bus speed caused groceries to drop, not illness.
3. They ate bad food. / Wrong: The text doesn’t mention food at all.
---
Q2: How did the soldier help them?
Correct: He took them home in his car.
Distractors:
1. He called an ambulance. / Wrong: The soldier gave them a ride, not medical help.
2. He carried their groceries. / Wrong: The soldier didn’t carry groceries; the narrator dropped them earlier.
3. He gave them money. / Wrong: The text doesn’t say anything about money.
---
Q3: What made the ending of the day happy?
Correct: The friendly bar and fun music.
Distractors:
1. They fixed their groceries. / Wrong: The groceries were dropped but not fixed; the bar visit was the happy part.
2. The soldier paid for everything. / Wrong: The text doesn’t mention payment.
3. They went home early. / Wrong: They stayed until midnight at the bar.



Topic: a story about a time I helped a neighbor

Text:
Recently, I helped my neighbor, Mr. Lee, a busy businessman. His thick pipe in the kitchen started leaking, and time was a big factor—he had an important meeting. One morning, I received an invitation to fix it. At first, I couldn’t figure out the problem, but I decided to act fast. Working at a fast rate, I followed his suggestion to tighten the loose nut. After an hour, the leak stopped! Mr. Lee was very happy and thanked me. Helping him made me feel good, and I realized how small acts can make a difference.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Mr. Lee need help quickly?
Correct: He had an important meeting soon.
Distractors:
1. His pipe was very old. / 2. He was sick and needed medicine. / 3. His kitchen was flooded.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches time was a big factor + important meeting.
- Distractor 1: Thick ≠ old; text doesn’t specify age.
- Distractor 2: No illness mentioned; busy refers to work.
- Distractor 3: Only a leak, not a flood.
---
Q2: What did Mr. Lee suggest to fix the pipe?
Correct: To tighten the loose nut.
Distractors:
1. Call a plumber. / 2. Use a new pipe. / 3. Turn off the water first.
Explanation:
- Correct: Directly quoted (followed his suggestion to tighten the loose nut).
- Distractor 1: Narrator fixed it themselves; no plumber mentioned.
- Distractor 2: No replacement pipe suggested.
- Distractor 3: Common sense but not in text.
---
Q3: How did the narrator feel after helping Mr. Lee?
Correct: Happy/good.
Distractors:
1. Tired but relieved. / 2. Angry because it took too long. / 3. Surprised the pipe was easy to fix.
Explanation:
- Correct: Made me feel good + happy.
- Distractor 1: No mention of tiredness.
- Distractor 2: No anger; fast rate implies efficiency.
- Distractor 3: No surprise expressed.



Topic: a story about my favorite toy from childhood

Text:
"My favorite toy was an incredible wooden train set! Every day, I’d watch the little passenger cars roll along the track. One afternoon, the train suddenly fell off—ah, what a shock! My father, who published books, helped me fix it with a knife to cut new tracks. The process was fun, but the best part was the tiny stamp on each ticket. Immediately, I imagined traveling to faraway places. Now, I smile remembering how that toy made childhood magical."

Questions:
Q1: What did the child use a knife for?
Correct: Cutting new tracks for the train.
Distractors:
1. To open the train’s engine.
2. To sharpen pencils for school.
3. To carve the train’s wheels.
Explanation:
The text says the knife was used "to cut new tracks." Distractors 1–3 are unrelated to the described action.
---
Q2: Why was the train’s stamp special?
Correct: It made the child imagine traveling.
Distractors:
1. It was a real postage stamp.
2. It helped the train move faster.
3. It was a gift from the father.
Explanation:
The stamp was a "tiny stamp on each ticket" that sparked imagination ("imagined traveling"). Distractors misrepresent its purpose.
---
Q3: What happened when the train fell off the track?
Correct: The child was surprised and said "Ah!"
Distractors:
1. The father immediately repaired it.
2. The passenger car broke into pieces.
3. The child stopped playing forever.
Explanation:
The text describes the child’s reaction ("ah, what a shock!"). Distractors misstate the sequence or outcome.



Topic: a story about my first day at school

Text:
"My first day at school was scary. My parents were divorced, so I lived with my mum. That morning, I wrote in my diary about my nerves. Our school was round and big. In science class, we did an experiment with chemicals. I fell over and spilled everything! I tried to hide my fear, but my teacher laughed. At lunch, the cook served us soup, and I washed my hands carefully. After school, my coach for basketball was very kind. I had to make a choice: leave or stay. I stayed, and it was fun!"

Questions:
Q1: Why did the writer feel nervous on their first day?
Correct: They were worried because their parents were divorced, and they were living with their mum.
Distractors:
1. "They didn’t know anyone in the school." (Text doesn’t mention friends.)
2. "They were afraid of the science experiment." (Fear came later, not the cause.)
3. "Their teacher was strict." (Teacher laughed, not strict.)

Explanation:
- Correct: Directly answers why (family context).
- Distractor 1: Assumes social anxiety (common but unsupported).
- Distractor 2: Confuses nerves (initial) with fear (experiment).
- Distractor 3: Misinterprets laughed as sternness.
---
Q2: What happened during the science experiment?
Correct: They accidentally fell and spilled the chemicals.
Distractors:
1. "They broke a beaker on purpose." (Text says "spilled," not intentional.)
2. "The teacher scolded them for being messy." (Teacher laughed, not scolded.)
3. "They hid the chemicals under their desk." (They fell, didn’t hide them.)

Explanation:
- Correct: Matches fell over and spilled.
- Distractor 1: Implies mischief; text describes an accident.
- Distractor 2: Misreads laughed as punishment.
- Distractor 3: Confuses hide (emotion) with hide (object).
---
Q3: How did the writer decide to stay at school?
Correct: Because their basketball coach was kind to them.
Distractors:
1. "They enjoyed the science experiment." (Experiment was messy; no positive link.)
2. "Their mum asked them to stay." (Mum not mentioned post-school.)
3. "They won a prize in class." (No prizes mentioned.)

Explanation:
- Correct: Links choice to coach’s kindness.
- Distractor 1: Overlooks coach’s role; focuses on experiment.
- Distractor 2: Misremembers family context (divorce earlier).
- Distractor 3: Fabricates detail; text has no rewards.



Topic: a story about a best friend I met when I was little

Text:
When I was little, I met my best friend in a small shop near my house. His name was Leo, and we had a chance to play together every day. One afternoon, we raced down the stairs of the old department store just to see who was faster. Leo always seemed to win, but he was never proud—just clever at finding shortcuts!

We loved looking at books with bright prints in the shop’s corner. Leo wanted to be an engineer when he grew up, and he’d explain how things worked, even if I didn’t understand. Once, he gave me some cash he found to buy me a snack, saying, "You’re the best friend ever." According to my mom, we were inseparable, and I still remember how happy those days made me.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Leo give the narrator some cash?
Correct: He gave it to buy the narrator a snack because he wanted to share his found money.
Distractors:
1. He found it and didn’t know what else to do with it.
2. The narrator asked him for money.
3. He wanted to pay for a toy for himself.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches text ("gave me some cash... to buy me a snack").
- Distractor 1: Ignores the purpose (buy snack).
- Distractor 2: Text shows Leo acted independently ("he gave me").
- Distractor 3: Text specifies snack for narrator, not a toy for Leo.
---
Q2: What did Leo and the narrator do in the department store’s corner, and why was it important to Leo?
Correct: They looked at books with bright prints, and it was important because Leo wanted to be an engineer and loved explaining how things worked.
Distractors:
1. They played games with the books.
2. Leo wanted to be a writer because of the prints.
3. They hid the cash there because it was safe.
Explanation:
- Correct: Combines books with prints + Leo’s dream (engineer).
- Distractor 1: "Played" is unsupported; text says looked at.
- Distractor 2: Confuses engineer (science) with writer.
- Distractor 3: Cash was given to narrator, not hidden.
---
Q3: How did Leo’s cleverness show in the race down the stairs?
Correct: Leo was clever because he always found shortcuts to win the race.
Distractors:
1. He ran the fastest because he was strong.
2. He let the narrator win to be kind.
3. He used a trick like pushing the narrator.
Explanation:
- Correct: Directly quotes text ("clever at finding shortcuts").
- Distractor 1: Text mentions shortcuts, not speed/strength.
- Distractor 2: Text says he always seemed to win (no letting win).
- Distractor 3: "Trick" implies cheating; text says shortcuts (fair strategy).



Topic: a story about a birthday party I will never forget

Text:
My tenth birthday party was unforgettable—especially because my dad, a general in the air force, turned it into a surprise! He invited a director from a movie studio, and suddenly, we were flying in a small plane over the city. My pet dog, Max, was asleep in my bag, but he woke up when we landed.

The necessary part? A cake shaped like a record player! The personality of the party came from everyone involving themselves—even my shy cousin sang. My heart still races when I think about it.

Questions:
Q1: Why was the birthday party so special for the narrator?
Correct: Because their dad organized a surprise plane ride and a themed cake.
Distractors:
1. The narrator’s pet performed tricks for the guests.
2. A famous singer attended the party.
3. The party was held in a movie theater.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text mentions a director from a movie studio, a flying plane, and a record-shaped cake—key details.
- Distractor 1: Wrong—Max the dog was asleep, not performing.
- Distractor 2: Wrong—no singer is mentioned.
- Distractor 3: Wrong—the party wasn’t in a theater; it was outdoors.
---
Q2: What was unusual about the cake at the party?
Correct: It was shaped like a record player.
Distractors:
1. It was made of real ice cream.
2. It had a hidden message inside.
3. It was flown in by a helicopter.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text explicitly states the cake was shaped like a record player.
- Distractor 1: Wrong—no mention of ice cream.
- Distractor 2: Wrong—no hidden message is described.
- Distractor 3: Wrong—the cake wasn’t delivered by helicopter; the flying was the plane ride.
---
Q3: How did the narrator’s cousin contribute to the party?
Correct: They sang, even though they were usually shy.
Distractors:
1. They helped decorate the plane.
2. They brought the cake.
3. They gave a speech about the narrator.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text says the cousin’s personality came from singing, despite being shy.
- Distractor 1: Wrong—no decorating is mentioned.
- Distractor 2: Wrong—the cake was prepared separately.
- Distractor 3: Wrong—no speech is described.



Topic: a story about helping a lost pet find its home

Text:
Lost and Found
Last week, Max, my golden retriever, went missing after his training session. I posted flyers in a large quantity, including the park and vet’s office. A nice guy at the café suggested checking under the carpet—silly, but Max was there, sleeping! His funny walk made it look like a comedy show. When I picked him up, it was an incredible feeling. Helping others has its benefits, too—I replaced his old collar with a new one. Now, Max wears a tag with my number. If your pet gets lost, don’t give up! Community help works wonders.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the owner think checking under the carpet was a silly idea at first?
Correct: The owner didn’t believe Max would fit or hide in such a small space.
Distractors:
1. Max had never hidden there before. / Wrong: Text doesn’t mention Max’s past habits.
2. The guy at the café told him it was a waste of time. / Wrong: The guy suggested it, not criticized it.
3. The carpet was too dirty for Max to sleep on. / Wrong: "Silly" refers to Max’s size, not cleanliness.

Explanation:
The correct answer explains the owner’s initial doubt based on Max’s size. Distractor 1 assumes prior behavior (not stated). Distractor 2 misreads "suggested" as "dismissed." Distractor 3 adds irrelevant detail about dirt.
---
Q2: How did the community help Max find his way home?
Correct: They posted flyers in many places and a guy gave a helpful tip.
Distractors:
1. Neighbors searched parks and pet stores with the owner. / Wrong: Text doesn’t mention neighbors helping actively.
2. Max’s training helped him recognize his owner’s voice. / Wrong: Training is mentioned, but not the cause of being found.
3. A vet found Max and called the owner. / Wrong: Max was found under the carpet, not at the vet’s.

Explanation:
The correct answer covers flyers + suggestion. Distractor 1 overstates neighbor involvement. Distractor 2 confuses training (past) with finding (present). Distractor 3 mislocates Max’s discovery.
---
Q3: What lesson did the owner learn from this experience?
Correct: Asking for help and not giving up can solve problems.
Distractors:
1. Pets should always wear GPS trackers. / Wrong: Text doesn’t mention technology.
2. Max needed more training to stay home. / Wrong: Lesson is about help, not training.
3. Lost pets are always found quickly if you shout loudly. / Wrong: Text describes a process, not shouting.

Explanation:
The correct answer summarizes the theme of perseverance and community. Distractor 1 introduces modern solutions. Distractor 2 focuses on one detail (training). Distractor 3 oversimplifies the method.

Topic: a story about a traditional festival in my country

Text:
In my country, the Lantern Festival has been celebrated since ancient times. Every year, people gather to light colorful lanterns. Female dancers carry them in a parade, and each unit weighs no more than 500 grams. There is a variety of games, like guessing riddles. The typical lantern designs are dragons and flowers. To keep everyone safe, we avoid touching fireflies and other insects. Research shows this festival started as a way to ask the gods for good harvests. Lanterns must not exceed 1 kg in weight, so they stay light and easy to carry.

1. Because insects are sacred. / Wrong: Text focuses on safety, not sacredness.

Correct: So they stay light and easy to carry.
Distractors:
1. To prevent fires. / Wrong: Fire risk isn’t mentioned.
2. Because heavier lanterns are more expensive. / Wrong: Cost isn’t discussed.

Correct: They carry lanterns in a parade.
Distractors:
1. They perform acrobatics. / Wrong: No acrobatics are mentioned.
2. They light the lanterns. / Wrong: "People" light them, not dancers.

Topic: a story about a time I had an incredibly lucky day

Text:
Last Saturday, I walked into an ancient store in a noisy environment. I was late because a lorry had blocked the street. Just as I thought my day was ruined, I saw the manager—she smiled and said, “Try this jacket! It fits perfectly.” I laughed when I almost choked on the scarf around my neck, but it was funny (comedy). Then, I found a wallet with £50 inside! I couldn’t believe any of this was happening. The manager even let me count the money in the store. That day, I realized how lucky I was.

Questions:
Q1: Why was the narrator late for their day?
Correct: A lorry blocked the street, so they were delayed.
Distractors:
1. They forgot their keys. / They overslept. / They got lost.
Explanation:
The correct answer matches the text’s cause (“a lorry had blocked the street”). Distractors are common reasons for lateness but aren’t mentioned. A weak reader might pick “overslept” because it’s a typical excuse, ignoring the lorry detail.
---
Q2: What two lucky things happened in the store?
Correct: They found a jacket that fit perfectly and £50 in a wallet.
Distractors:
1. They found a rare ancient artifact. / They won a prize. / They avoided a traffic accident.
Explanation:
The correct answer covers the two specific lucky events (jacket + money). “Won a prize” is plausible but unsupported—the text describes finding items, not winning. A distracted reader might confuse “ancient” with rarity.
---
Q3: How did the manager help the narrator besides giving advice?
Correct: She let them count the money in the store.
Distractors:
1. She gave them a discount. / She called a taxi. / She fixed their scarf.
Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases “let me count the money”. “Called a taxi” is a helpful action but not in the text. A careless reader might assume the manager did more since she was kind, but the text only mentions counting money.



Topic: a story about a friend who has a very different personality from me

Text:
My friend Clara has a particular way of seeing the world. While I dream of space, she loves the earth—her garden has a number of plants! She’s very closed about her feelings, often covering them with work. Yet, she supports me when I’m too wild, like a quiet tie to reality. Clara has a rule: recycle everything. Her calm nature balances my chaos. We’re different, but her steady presence makes life richer.

1. She grows plants to sell them.
2. She collects plants as a hobby.
3. She needs plants for her job.

Correct: She recycles everything.
Distractors:
1. She plants trees in forests.
2. She teaches others about nature.
3. She uses reusable bags.

Correct: It balances the narrator’s chaotic personality.
Distractors:
1. It makes the narrator more organized.
2. It helps the narrator relax.
3. It makes the narrator feel guilty.
Explanation:
The text says Clara’s "calm nature balances my chaos" and her "steady presence makes life richer." A reader might focus on "steady" (1) or "richer" (2) without noting the contrast to chaos.

- C: Wrong because ship is metaphorical, not literal travel.

Topic: a funny story about a kitchen accident

Text:
Last weekend, my friend Alex and I decided to cook dinner together. We invited our neighbors to seat themselves at the table, but first, we had to prepare everything. While cutting vegetables, Alex dropped a plate—clearly, it was an accident! A big chip flew into the air, and we both jumped. Neither of us wanted to admit fault, so we started to fight over who was more careful. The phone rang, but we ignored it, too busy arguing. Soon, flour and oil were everywhere—we almost destroyed the kitchen! When we finally calmed down, we discovered the mess and laughed. We spent an hour cleaning, but in the end, we felt better. Now, we always cook separately—ourselves! The next day, we invited everyone over again, but this time, the kitchen was spotless.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Alex and the narrator stop arguing?
Correct: They laughed and realized how silly they were being.
Distractors:
1. They called the police. (Wrong—they ignored the phone.)
2. They blamed each other and left. (Wrong—they cleaned up together.)
3. The plate magically fixed itself. (Wrong—they had to clean the mess.)
Explanation:
The correct answer matches the text’s resolution ("laughed" and "felt better"). Distractors 1 and 2 misread the phone’s role (ignored) and the fight’s outcome (they stayed). Distractor 3 ignores the cleanup step entirely.
---
Q2: What did they do after they cleaned the kitchen?
Correct: They invited their neighbors over again, but this time the kitchen was clean.
Distractors:
1. They threw away all their cooking tools. (Wrong—they cooked again later.)
2. They never cooked together again. (Wrong—they cooked separately but still cooked.)
3. They moved to a new apartment. (Wrong—the story ends with them cooking again.)
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects the text’s ending ("invited... kitchen was spotless"). Distractors 1 and 2 confuse "separately" with "never," and distractor 3 is an overinterpretation of the mess.
---
Q3: How did the broken plate cause more problems?
Correct: It made them argue, ignore the phone, and create a bigger mess.
Distractors:
1. It burned their food. (Wrong—the mess was from flour/oil, not fire.)
2. Their neighbors got angry. (Wrong—the neighbors were invited after cleanup.)
3. They had to buy a new kitchen. (Wrong—they cleaned it themselves.)
Explanation:
The correct answer covers the text’s chain: fight → ignored phone → mess. Distractors 1 and 2 misattribute causes (fire/neighbors), and distractor 3 exaggerates the "almost destroyed" line as literal.

1. The meal was difficult to eat and left stains on plates.

- Distractor 2 is wrong because no teacher is mentioned.

Correct: He flew out of control and crashed into the ground.

- Correct matches "laughed so hard they almost had a fight" (teasing).

Topic: a story about a bird that visited my window

Text:
One sunny morning, a small bird visited my window. The heat made me open it, and the bird lifted onto the sill, looking hungry. I was cooking breakfast, and its beak moved as if it smelled the food. I felt like a detective, trying to understand its purpose. Maybe its stomach was empty?

I took a step closer, but it flew away. The next day, it returned, perching higher this time. I gave it a lecture about safety, but it ignored me. My successful attempts to feed it made it bolder. Then, I noticed a car outside—its petrol smell might have attracted it too!

Questions:
Q1: Why did the bird keep coming back to the window?
Correct: It was attracted by the smell of food and the petrol from the car.
Distractors:
1. It wanted to see the narrator. / Wrong: The text says the bird was hungry and smelled food, not social.
2. The bird was lost and needed help. / Wrong: No mention of the bird being lost.
3. It liked the heat from the window. / Partially true but incomplete: Heat was a factor, but food/petrol were also reasons.

Explanation:
The correct answer combines two clues: the bird’s beak moving at the smell of cooking (food) and the narrator’s observation about the car’s petrol smell. Distractor 1 ignores the bird’s hunger; distractor 2 adds unsupported detail; distractor 3 is too narrow.
---
Q2: How did the narrator try to help the bird?
Correct: The narrator gave it a lecture about safety and tried to feed it.
Distractors:
1. The narrator chased it away. / Wrong: The bird ignored the lecture, implying no chasing.
2. It built a nest on the windowsill. / Wrong: No nesting is mentioned.
3. The narrator gave it water. / Wrong: Only feeding and a lecture are described.

Explanation:
The text explicitly states the narrator gave a lecture and had successful attempts to feed it. Distractor 1 contradicts the bird’s ignoring the lecture; distractors 2–3 add details not in the text.
---
Q3: What might have attracted the bird to the car outside?
Correct: The smell of petrol from the car.
Distractors:
1. The bird was scared by the car’s noise. / Wrong: The text implies attraction, not fear.
2. The car’s headlights were bright. / Wrong: No mention of light.
3. The bird recognized the narrator’s car. / Wrong: No personal recognition implied.

Explanation:
The text directly links the bird’s interest to the petrol smell. Distractor 1 reverses the implied attraction; distractors 2–3 introduce irrelevant details.



Topic: a story about my grandparents' old house

Text:
My grandparents’ old house had an enormous garden with tall trees. Every morning, my grandmother pet her cat, Miso, while my grandfather carefully fixed the old door. The curtains had a beautiful pattern of flowers, and my grandfather used to pull them open to let in the sunlight. But the lock had a strange code, and it often gave me a headache when I tried to open it!

My grandparents disagreed about selling the house. My grandmother said, “We made soap here—remember?” But my grandfather wanted to move. Sadly, they sold it last year. Now, I miss the smell of fresh bread and the sound of Miso’s purr.

Correct: It was difficult to open because it had a strange code.
Distractors:
1. The lock was too heavy to lift.
2. The lock was broken and fell apart.

Explanation:

- Distractor 1: Heavy is implied by old, but text specifies code.
- Distractor 2: Broken is not mentioned; headache suggests mental effort, not physical damage.
- Distractor 3: Paint is irrelevant; no visual issue is described.
---
Q2: What did the grandparents argue about?
Correct: They argued about whether to sell the house or keep it.
Distractors:
1. They argued about the cat.
2. They argued about the garden.
3. They argued about the soap recipe.
Explanation:
- Correct: “Disagreed about selling” = conflict over selling/keeping.
- Distractor 1: Cat is mentioned but not disputed.
- Distractor 2: Garden is described but not part of the argument.
- Distractor 3: Soap is a memory, not the reason for disagreement.
---
Q3: How did the house make the narrator feel after it was sold?
Correct: The narrator missed the smells and sounds of home.
Distractors:
1. The narrator was happy because they got a new house.
2. The narrator was angry at the grandparents.
3. The narrator forgot about the house.
Explanation:
- Correct: “Miss the smell... purr” = nostalgia.
- Distractor 1: Sadly contradicts happiness.
- Distractor 2: No anger is expressed; tone is reflective.
- Distractor 3: “Miss” implies ongoing attachment, not forgetting.

Topic: a story about a talent show at my school

Later, Leo found the rabbit in his backpack and performed a successful trick. The crowd shouted with joy! The show ended without more trouble, and Leo even won first prize. Maybe next year’s talent show will be usual again!

- Distractor 1 (Wrong): The teacher only scolded him for losing the rabbit, not banned his performance.
- Distractor 2 (Wrong): The reporter’s arrival was a distraction, not the reason.
- Distractor 3 (Wrong): Leo was doing magic, not singing.

Correct: He performed a successful magic trick and won first prize.
Distractors:
1. He gave up and let someone else perform.

Distractors:

Topic: a story about a surprise visit from an old friend

Text:
Last weekend, my old friend Jake arrived at my house without calling. It was noisy outside because neighbors were having a party, but I didn’t care—it was great to see him! Jake was wearing strange green pants and a mismatched sock, and he acted like a nut when he saw me. ‘You look the same!’ he laughed. We sat down for coffee, but we argued about the music—he wanted loud rock, and I preferred less noise. After a few minutes, we had a discussion and started laughing. Jake was crazy, but in a fun way. Later, he said, ‘Let’s go for a drive!’ so we got in his car. He was driving too fast, and we almost had an argument, but we ended up singing songs instead. It was a perfect day!

- Forget is wrong because the text implies intentional silliness.
- Party is wrong because the visit was at the narrator’s house, not a party.
- Broken is wrong because the clothes are described as "strange," not damaged.
---
Q2: What happened when Jake wanted to listen to loud rock music?
Correct: They started arguing because Jake wanted loud music, but his friend preferred quiet.
Distractors:
1. They turned off the music immediately. / Jake’s friend also liked loud music. / They couldn’t agree on any music.
Explanation:
- Correct reflects the text’s detail about preferences and conflict.
- Turned off is wrong because the argument continued (they later discussed it).
- Also liked is wrong because the friend "preferred less noise."
- Couldn’t agree is wrong because they resolved it by singing.

Q3: How did Jake and his friend end their day together?
Correct: They sang songs in the car instead of continuing their argument.
Distractors:
1. They went home separately. / They had a serious discussion about their argument. / Jake apologized for driving too fast.
Explanation:
- Correct matches the text’s ending ("we ended up singing songs").
- Separately is wrong because they drove together.
- Serious discussion is wrong because they sang (lighthearted).
- Apologized is wrong because no apology is mentioned.

Topic: a story about a stranger who helped me when I was lost

Text:
Last week, I got lost in a big city. The streets looked the same, and I couldn’t find my specific destination. A crowd of people rushed past me, but no one stopped. Then, a man in a black coat appeared. He gave me a wooden sign with a gold watch on it and said, “This will help.” I received a gift—a map! Perhaps he was an actor on stage, but his kindness felt real. I’m not very social, but I thanked him. Later, a strong blow of wind blew his hat away, and I saw his tired eyes. Maybe he was just a good person after all.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the narrator think the man perhaps was an actor?
Correct: The man’s politeness seemed too perfect, like someone performing.
Distractors:
1. Because he wore a black coat like a stage costume. / Wrong: The text doesn’t say it was a costume.
2. Because he gave the narrator a gold watch. / Wrong: The watch was on a sign, not given as a gift.
3. Because he was part of a crowd of actors. / Wrong: The crowd was just people in the city.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches the idea that his behavior was "too polished" (implied by stage).
- Distractor 1: Misreads "black coat" as a costume (overgeneralizes).
- Distractor 2: Confuses the gold watch (part of the sign) with the gift (map).
- Distractor 3: Assumes all in the crowd were actors (no evidence).
---
Q2: What gift did the narrator receive from the stranger?
Correct: A map.
Distractors:
1. A wooden sign with a gold watch. / Wrong: The sign wasn’t the gift.
2. A black coat. / Wrong: The coat belonged to the man.
3. A strong blow of wind. / Wrong: The wind was an event, not a gift.
Explanation:
- Correct: Directly states "a gift—a map!"
- Distractor 1: "Gift" is near the sign, causing misreading.
- Distractor 2: "Black coat" is prominent but unrelated.
- Distractor 3: "Blow" is at the end; readers might misconnect it to the help.
---
Q3: How did the wind help the narrator understand the man’s kindness?
Correct: It showed the man was tired, proving he wasn’t pretending.
Distractors:
1. It blew the map away, so the narrator had to ask for help again. / Wrong: The map stayed; the wind blew the hat.
2. It made the man’s voice sound more sincere. / Wrong: No mention of voice after the wind.
3. It revealed the man was actually an actor hiding in the crowd. / Wrong: The wind showed tired eyes, not hiding.
Explanation:
- Correct: The wind blew the hat, revealing tired eyes (proof of sincerity).
- Distractor 1: Confuses "blow" with the map (proximity error).
- Distractor 2: Assumes wind affects voice (logical but unsupported).
- Distractor 3: Misinterprets "crowd" + "actor" as hiding (no text support).



Topic: a fairy tale about a clever princess who solves puzzles to save her kingdom

Text:
Princess Elara was a teenage girl, but she was very clever. One day, she noticed her father, the king, was weak from a curse. A diary in the castle library had clues to break it. She took it and left the castle, traveling a long distance to the forest. An old woman gave her a tip: "Think like the wind—fast and free!" Elara used this method to solve puzzles at a fast rate. She kept small notes in her pocket and found the treasure in a hidden position. With her quick mind, she saved the kingdom!

Questions:
Q1: Why did Princess Elara leave the castle?
Correct: To find a way to break the curse that made her father weak.
Distractors:
1. She wanted to explore the forest.
2. The king asked her to deliver a message.
3. She was bored and wanted adventure.
Explanation: The text states she noticed the king’s weakness and found clues in a diary, so her goal was to solve the curse. Distractors ignore the curse or add irrelevant details.
---
Q2: What did the old woman’s tip help Elara do?
Correct: It helped her solve puzzles quickly and efficiently.
Distractors:
1. It taught her how to hide the treasure.
2. It gave her a magic spell.
3. It showed her the way to the castle.
Explanation: The tip was about thinking fast, which aligns with solving puzzles at a fast rate. Distractors misrepresent the tip’s purpose.
---
Q3: Where did Elara keep her notes?
Correct: She kept them in her pocket.
Distractors:
1. In the diary.
2. Under her pillow.
3. In a secret drawer.
Explanation: The text explicitly says "kept small notes in her pocket." Distractors assume she stored them elsewhere without evidence.



Topic: a fairy tale about tiny people

Text:
One day, a group of tiny people lived deep in the wood. Their goal was to help the king, but they didn’t know how to apply their skills properly. While cooking, one of them burned his finger and cried in pain. The king, passing by, was surprised to see them. He found a diary near the fire, written in tiny letters. It said, “First, we need tools. Secondly, we must find dry leaves to heal our burns.” The king smiled and gave them what they needed. With his help, the tiny people finally achieved their goal and became heroes.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the tiny people need dry leaves?
Correct: They needed dry leaves to treat their burns.
Distractors:
1. They needed dry leaves to make a bed.
2. They used dry leaves to start a fire.
3. The king told them to use dry leaves.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches the diary’s purpose (“heal our burns”).
- Distractor 1: “Bed” is plausible for tiny people but unrelated to the burn.
- Distractor 2: They already had a fire (they burned their finger while cooking).
- Distractor 3: The king gave tools but didn’t specify dry leaves; the plan came from their diary.
---
Q2: What was the king’s reaction when he saw the tiny people?
Correct: He was surprised.
Distractors:
1. The king was worried about them.
2. The king wanted to take them to his castle.
3. The king was scared.
Explanation:
- Correct: Directly stated (“the king... was surprised”).
- Distractor 1: “Worried” is emotionally close to surprised but not the same.
- Distractor 2: The text doesn’t mention moving them; they helped him in the wood.
- Distractor 3: Surprised ≠ scared; no fear is described.
---
Q3: How did the tiny people plan to solve their problems? (Give two steps.)
Correct:
1. First, they needed tools.
2. Secondly, they needed dry leaves.
Distractors:
1. First, they needed a bigger fire. Secondly, they needed water.
2. First, they wanted to hide. Secondly, they wanted to rest.
3. First, they needed a map. Secondly, they needed a key.
Explanation:
- Correct: Exactly matches the diary’s two steps.
- Distractor 1: “Fire/water” are common burn solutions but not in the text.
- Distractor 2: “Hide/rest” are passive; the text describes active problem-solving.
- Distractor 3: “Map/key” are unrelated to their practical needs (tools + healing).



Topic: a fairy tale about a dragon who was evil, but became kind

Text:
Once, a dragon named Ignar lived in a narrow cave. He was shut from the world, and his smell of fire scared everyone. One day, a brave pilot named Leo took a tour of the mountains. He saw Ignar and tried to communicate, but the dragon was independent and refused to talk.

Leo borrowed a book from Ignar to prove he wasn’t afraid. Ignar was surprised and let Leo read it. Leo’s divorced father had taught him kindness, which Ignar had never known. Ignar realized his code of fear was wrong. He helped Leo communicate with the villagers, and soon, he became their protector.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Ignar’s code of fear change?
Correct: He met Leo, who showed him kindness his father had taught him.
Distractors:
1. The villagers forced him to change. / He smelled something better. / Leo gave him a gift.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches the text (Leo’s father taught kindness → Ignar realized fear was wrong).
- Distractor 1: Wrong because Ignar changed due to Leo, not villagers.
- Distractor 2: Smell describes fear, not a positive change.
- Distractor 3: Leo borrowed, not gave, the book.
---
Q2: How did Leo prove to Ignar that he wasn’t afraid?
Correct: He borrowed a book from Ignar without being scared.
Distractors:
1. He flew away from Ignar. / He shouted at Ignar. / Ignar let him into his cave.
Explanation:
- Correct: Text says Leo borrowed a book to show bravery.
- Distractor 1: Wrong—Leo stayed to talk.
- Distractor 2: Text implies calm interaction, not shouting.
- Distractor 3: Leo already saw Ignar; the book was the key action.
---
Q3: What did Ignar do after he realized his fear was wrong?
Correct: He helped Leo talk to the villagers and became their protector.
Distractors:
1. He left the village. / He burned the villagers’ houses. / He shut himself in the cave again.
Explanation:
- Correct: Text states he helped communicate and became their protector.
- Distractor 1: Wrong—he stayed and helped.
- Distractor 2: Opposite of the resolution (he was kind).
- Distractor 3: Wrong—he opened up, not shut himself in.



Topic: a fairy tale about a tree that grows golden coins, but only a kind person can take them

Text:
In a small village, there was a tree that grew golden coins—but only kind people could take them. Professor Green taught the children about learning and kindness. One day, a greedy man tried to steal coins, but the tree gave any more. A little girl saw a bear near the tree and took a photograph. When she helped the bear, the tree gave her coins. The community celebrated, and the population grew happier. That fall, her pants got stuck in the branches, but the bear helped her check the coins safely. Now, everyone shares the tree’s gifts.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the greedy man fail to take coins from the tree?
Correct: Because he wasn’t kind.
Distractors:
1. He didn’t know the rule. (Wrong: The professor taught the rule.)
2. The bear ate his coins. (Wrong: The bear wasn’t greedy.)
3. The tree was broken. (Wrong: The tree worked for kind people.)
Explanation: The text says the tree only gives coins to kind people. The greedy man’s failure is directly linked to his lack of kindness.
---
Q2: How did the little girl help the bear?
Correct: She shared coins with it or freed it from something.
Distractors:
1. She took a photo of it. (Partially correct but not the help.)
2. She gave it food. (Not mentioned.)
3. She shouted at it. (Contradicts kindness.)
Explanation: The text implies she helped the bear (e.g., by giving coins or freeing it), but not through actions like shouting or feeding.
---
Q3: What happened to the girl’s pants in the fall?
Correct: They got stuck in the tree’s branches.
Distractors:
1. They fell off. (Wrong: They got stuck.)
2. The bear tore them. (Wrong: The bear helped.)
3. They were dirty. (Irrelevant.)
Explanation: The text explicitly states her pants got stuck in the branches during fall.



Topic: an adventure story about pirates

Text:
Captain Redbeard’s ship, The Golden Storm, was enormous but old. The crew worked hard to repair the broken sails. One day, a mysterious passenger appeared on deck. He carried a strange box and said, “I can connect this to your ship’s compass.” The pirates tried to identify him, but he only smiled and washed his hands with soap.

Finally, the captain realized the man was a spy for a major enemy. “We must stop him!” he shouted. The crew chased the spy, but he disappeared into the stormy waves.

Questions:
Q1: What did the mysterious passenger claim he could do with the box?
Correct: He said he could attach it to the ship’s compass.
Distractors:
1. He said it would help them find hidden treasure.
2. He said it would make the ship faster.
3. He said it would clean the ship’s sails.

Explanation:
- Correct: The text states he said, “I can connect this to your ship’s compass.”
- Distractor 1: The text never mentions treasure.
- Distractor 2: The text doesn’t say anything about speed.
- Distractor 3: The text says he washed his hands with soap, not that the box cleaned sails.
---
Q2: Why did the captain realize the passenger was dangerous?
Correct: Because the passenger was a spy for a major enemy.
Distractors:
1. Because he refused to tell his name.
2. Because he stole food from the ship.
3. Because he tried to sink the ship.

Explanation:
- Correct: The text says, “the captain realized the man was a spy for a major enemy.”
- Distractor 1: The text doesn’t mention the passenger refusing to tell his name.
- Distractor 2: The text doesn’t say anything about stolen food.
- Distractor 3: The text says he disappeared, not that he tried to sink the ship.
---
Q3: What happened to the spy at the end of the story?
Correct: He disappeared into the stormy waves.
Distractors:
1. He was caught by the pirates.
2. He jumped overboard to escape.
3. He gave the box to the captain.

Explanation:
- Correct: The text states, “he disappeared into the stormy waves.”
- Distractor 1: The text says the crew chased him but didn’t catch him.
- Distractor 2: The text doesn’t say he jumped overboard—only that he disappeared.
- Distractor 3: The text doesn’t mention him giving the box to the captain.



Topic: a story about a school sports day

Text:
Last week, our school had a big sports day! The wind blew hard, and the sun gave everyone headaches. Team Red took the lead in the race, but then rumors spread about a banned drug. I found a strange book near the underground storage room—it had a small cut with blood on the cover! The teachers threw away old furniture, but we still had to pass the baton carefully. Later, we learned the book was rubbish—just an old story about sports heroes. The day ended happily, and we all cheered!

Questions:
Q1: Why did some students think Team Red might have cheated?
Correct: Because there were rumors about a banned drug.
Distractors:
1. They saw Team Red throw away rubbish. / The book had blood on it. / The wind was too strong.
Explanation: The text only mentions drug rumors as the cheating suspicion. Weak readers might link rubbish, blood, or wind to cheating.

Q2: What did the narrator find near the underground storage room?
Correct: A strange book with a small cut and blood on it.
Distractors:
1. A broken chair. / A baton. / A drug.
Explanation: The book is the only object found. Weak readers might confuse furniture (thrown away) or drug rumors (unseen).
---
Q3: How did the sports day end?
Correct: Happily, with everyone cheering.
Distractors:
1. With a headache for everyone. / Team Red won unfairly. / The book was kept as evidence.
Explanation: The text ends positively ("cheered"). Weak readers might focus on headaches or rumors and overlook the happy ending.



Topic: a story about my favorite holiday memory from when I was a child

Text:
When I was a child, my favorite holiday was Thanksgiving. I remember it so well because of a colorful advertisement I saw on TV. It showed a big turkey and happy families, so my parents decided to celebrate it close to home that year.

The day was full of a variety of fun tasks. We decorated the table with leaves, played games, and even made pies. Except for one thing—it was too hot! Fall usually means cool weather, but that year, the heat made us uncomfortable. Still, the noise from laughter and music made it exciting.

The best part was sitting by the fire. My grandma told stories, and I felt so happy. When the fireworks lit up the sky, I shouted, "Wow!" Sadly, the holiday ended too soon, but I’ll never forget it.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the narrator’s family celebrate Thanksgiving that year?
Correct: They were inspired by a TV advertisement.
Distractors:
1. Their grandparents asked them to come over. / They always celebrated it at home. / It was a school assignment.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches "colorful advertisement" in the text.
- Distractor 1: Wrong because the text mentions a TV ad, not grandparents.
- Distractor 2: Wrong because the text says "that year" (special occasion).
- Distractor 3: Wrong because "task" refers to activities, not the reason.
---
Q2: What was one thing that made the holiday less perfect?
Correct: The weather was unusually hot.
Distractors:
1. The fireworks were too loud. / They ran out of food. / The decorations were ugly.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches "it was too hot" (contrasts with fall).
- Distractor 1: Wrong because noise was described as exciting.
- Distractor 2: Wrong because pies were made (no shortage).
- Distractor 3: Wrong because decorations aren’t mentioned as bad.
---
Q3: How did the narrator feel when the fireworks started?
Correct: Excited and amazed.
Distractors:
1. Nervous. / Tired. / Disappointed.
Explanation:
- Correct: "Wow!" and "happy" show excitement.
- Distractor 1: Wrong because "wow" implies joy, not fear.
- Distractor 2: Wrong because the text describes happiness.
- Distractor 3: Wrong because sadness was about the holiday ending, not the fireworks.



Topic: a story about visiting a zoo

Text:
Last weekend, my family visited the zoo. The manager told us that the zoo earns money by showing animals to visitors. We saw extremely big lions that looked alive and moving. Later, we watched monkeys perform on a small stage. They were able to jump carefully and even sing a few notes! My little brother laughed when a bright red sock fell near the penguins. We either took photos or watched the parrots, which were singing loudly. The zoo was fun, and I learned that animals need care to stay healthy.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the zoo manager mention how the zoo earns money?
Correct: To explain that the zoo makes money by showing animals to visitors.
Distractors:
1. To explain that animals need care to stay healthy.
2. Because the zoo was losing money.
3. To ask visitors to buy tickets.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches the text’s first sentence: "The manager told us that the zoo earns money by showing animals to visitors."
- Distractor 1: Wrong because the manager’s comment is about earning money, not care (that’s mentioned later).
- Distractor 2: Wrong because the text doesn’t say the zoo was losing money; it’s open and operational.
- Distractor 3: Wrong because the manager didn’t ask for tickets; he explained how money is earned.
---
Q2: What two things could visitors do at the zoo either of?
Correct: Take photos or watch the parrots.
Distractors:
1. Watch the lions or the penguins.
2. Take photos or feed the animals.
3. Listen to the monkeys or watch the parrots.
Explanation:
- Correct: Directly from the text: "We either took photos or watched the parrots."
- Distractor 1: Wrong because the either refers to photos/parrots, not lions/penguins (though they’re mentioned).
- Distractor 2: Wrong because feeding animals isn’t an option in the text.
- Distractor 3: Wrong because the text doesn’t mention listening to monkeys as an option.
---
Q3: How did the little brother react when the sock fell near the penguins?
Correct: He laughed.
Distractors:
1. He was scared.
2. He threw the sock back.
3. He told the manager about it.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches the text: "My little brother laughed when a bright red sock fell..."
- Distractor 1: Wrong because laughed implies happiness, not fear.
- Distractor 2: Wrong because the text doesn’t describe the brother throwing the sock.
- Distractor 3: Wrong because the text doesn’t mention the brother telling anyone about it.

I held my coffee with both hands, but it was still too hot. Later, the shop provided warm drinks, and I invented an excuse about ‘testing the heater.’ I finished my walk home in shame, learning to check the weather next time!

Topic: a story about a big argument in my family that ended with laughter

Text:
Last weekend, my family had a big argument—until we laughed! My uncle, who works as a dentist and does advertising, brought a huge stone to our house. "This is proof!" he said. Dad got angry, saying, "You’re accusing me of a crime!" I tried to explain, but we were all confused. Then Uncle showed us digital photos from his gallery of the "evidence." Turns out, he bought the stone at a local store as a joke about education being fun. We used a silly method to solve it: we pretended the stone was a "test" for memory. Everyone laughed, and the argument ended happily!

3. The stone was from a crime scene. / Wrong: It was a joke; no real crime.

Explanation: The correct answer matches the text’s exact wording ("accusing me of a crime"). Distractors misrepresent the cause (breaking/expense/crime scene), which aren’t supported by the text.
---

Correct: He showed digital photos from his gallery as "evidence."
Distractors:
1. He showed a written note. / Wrong: Text specifies "digital photos."
2. He used a video recording. / Wrong: Photos, not video.
3. He had a witness. / Wrong: No witness mentioned.

Distractors:
1. They called the police. / Wrong: They laughed and pretended.
2. They threw the stone away. / Wrong: No disposal mentioned.

Text:
Last weekend, I visited an interactive museum with my friend Jake. Our guide had a fun personality and showed us exhibits about pollution. Sadly, I learned how much trash burns in the ocean. Then, we saw a shiny silver army uniform from World War II. "No any rules here!" Jake said. The guide made a deal: "If you touch the volcano button, I’ll tell you why lava is so hot!" I pressed it, and she taught us how volcanoes form. By the end, I forgot about pollution—it was too exciting!

Questions:
Q1: Why did the child feel "sadly" during the visit?
Correct: They were upset about trash burning in the ocean.
Distractors:
1. They missed their friend Jake. / They couldn’t find the exit. / The museum was too crowded.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches pollution + burns in text.

Topic: a story about a time I went fishing with my dad

Text:
When I was young, my dad took me fishing with his old device—a rusty rod he’d used for years. He’d joke about a frog jumping into the water and how we’d ‘download’ fish if we waited long enough. We sat against the current, checking our lines carefully. Dad had a book full of fishing tips, but he mostly talked about mathematics—like how to ‘calculate’ the weight of a fish before catching it! One day, we rowed too far from shore, and my line got stuck. Dad laughed, saying we’d ‘die’ of boredom if we didn’t catch anything. By evening, we had less fish than we hoped, but it didn’t matter. The distance from home made it feel like an adventure, and I’d do it again.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the boy and his dad have to row back to shore?
Correct: Their fishing line got stuck because they were too far from shore.
Distractors:
1. They were scared of a frog in the water. (Frog was a joke; line was the issue.)
2. Dad wanted to check his device again. (Line was checked earlier; distance caused the problem.)
3. They caught less fish than expected. (Result, not cause.)

Explanation:
The correct answer matches the text’s cause (line stuck due to distance). Distractors 1–3 misattribute reasons (frog, device check, or outcome) that appear in the text but aren’t the cause of rowing back.
---
Q2: What did Dad’s fishing book mostly contain?
Correct: It had fishing tips, but Dad mostly joked about silly things (e.g., mathematics).
Distractors:
1. It had mathematics problems. (Math was a joke, not the book’s content.)
2. It was about how to avoid death while fishing. ("Death" was a joke about boredom.)
3. It had pictures of frogs. (Frogs were mentioned in jokes, not in the book.)

Explanation:
The correct answer clarifies the book’s actual content (tips) and Dad’s behavior (jokes). Distractors confuse words (math, death, frog) that appear in the text but aren’t linked to the book’s purpose.
---
Q3: How did Dad make fishing fun for the boy?
Correct: He told jokes (e.g., ‘downloading’ fish, ‘dying’ of boredom).
Distractors:
1. He taught him mathematics while fishing. (Math was a joke, not teaching.)
2. He used a special device to catch fish. (The rod was ordinary.)
3. He read from his book the whole time. (Jokes were the focus, not reading.)

Explanation:
The correct answer highlights Dad’s humor as the fun factor. Distractors 1–3 misrepresent activities (math, device, book) that appear in the text but weren’t the primary source of fun.



Topic: a story about a time I felt shy meeting new friends and how I got over it

Topic: an adventure story about a camping trip

Text:
Liam and his dad went camping with their coach, Mr. Hayes, who knew the forest well. Near their campsite, Liam found a strange gift—a small metal box hidden under a rock. "What’s this?" he asked. Mr. Hayes smiled but didn’t give an explanation yet.

That night, the fire wouldn’t light. A young park assistant, Mia, joined them. "Maybe the wind is wrong," she said. Liam opened the box and found a compass. "Ah!" Mr. Hayes said. "The nut in the box is just for luck." Mia laughed. "It’s likely to rain—we should cover our food!"

Liam accepted the plan. Together, they worked quickly to tie tarps over their supplies. As they sat by the flickering fire, Mia joked, "I hope no squirrels steal my nuts!" Everyone laughed, happy despite the cold.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Liam open the metal box?
Correct: Because he was curious about what was inside it.
Distractors:
1. Mr. Hayes told him to open it.
2. He thought it was a treasure map.
3. Mia asked him to check it.
Explanation:
The correct answer matches the text’s direct action ("Liam opened the box"). Distractor 1 is wrong because Mr. Hayes smiled but didn’t explain—no order was given. Distractor 2 is wrong because the box contained a compass, not a map. Distractor 3 is wrong because Mia joined after the fire problem, not before the box was found.
---
Q2: What did Mia suggest they do about the rain?
Correct: They should cover their food with tarps.
Distractors:
1. Build a bigger fire.
2. Find a drier campsite.
3. Use the compass to avoid rain.
Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases Mia’s exact words ("cover our food"). Distractor 1 is wrong because the fire was the problem, not the solution. Distractor 2 is wrong because the text doesn’t mention moving—they prepared at the site. Distractor 3 is wrong because the compass was for direction, not weather.
---
Q3: How did Liam feel at the end of the story?
Correct: He felt happy, even though it was cold.
Distractors:
1. Excited to explore more.
2. Frustrated because of the rain.
3. Surprised by the compass.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects the text’s tone ("laughed, happy despite the cold"). Distractor 1 is wrong because the story ends with them sitting by the fire—no exploration is planned. Distractor 2 is wrong because they laughed, showing positivity. Distractor 3 is wrong because the compass was explained earlier; surprise wasn’t the final emotion.



Topic: an adventure story about crossing an old bridge

Text:
In a small village, there was a large wooden bridge that no one in society dared to cross. The general rule was to stay away—it might collapse at any time. One day, a couple of adventurers decided to try it. As they ran toward the bridge, dark clouds covered the sky. An old man suggested they go carefully, but they laughed. The bridge creaked under their feet, but it held! Back then, there were no mobile phones, so they had to trust their own courage.

Correct: They thought it might collapse.
Distractors:
1. It was too small for cars. / 2. Society thought it was haunted. / 3. No one had mobile phones to call for help.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches "might collapse" (reason for avoidance).
- Distractor 1: Wrong—text says "large," not small.
- Distractor 2: Wrong—no mention of ghosts.
- Distractor 3: Wrong—phones are mentioned later, not the cause.

Correct: He told them to cross carefully.
Distractors:
1. He told them not to go. / 2. He ran with them. / 3. He said the bridge was mobile.
Explanation:
- Correct: Paraphrases "suggested they go carefully."
- Distractor 1: Wrong—he didn’t say "don’t go."
- Distractor 2: Wrong—he only advised, didn’t act.
- Distractor 3: Wrong—"mobile" refers to phones.

Correct: They felt relieved.
Distractors:

- Distractor 2: Wrong—no phones were used.
- Distractor 3: Wrong—the bridge held, per text.



Topic: a fairy tale about a wizard who turned an evil person into an animal

Text:
Long ago, in a small kingdom, an evil man wanted to destroy the king’s possession—a golden crown. 3. The wizard was the only person who could find the golden crown. (Wrong: The crown was the villain’s target, not the wizard’s goal.)

2. The knight killed him immediately. (Wrong: The text describes a celebration, not death.)
3. The princess forgave him and let him stay at the palace. (Wrong: The text says he was "lonely," implying exile.)

- Distractor 1: Mixes up fantasy creatures (dragon vs. wolf).
- Distractor 2: Assumes violence where the text shows resolution.
- Distractor 3: Contradicts the "alone" detail.

- Distractor 2: Misreads the villain’s fate (he was cursed, not reformed).
- Distractor 3: Adds irrelevant details (castles aren’t mentioned).

Topic: a fairy tale about a rabbit who wanted to learn to fly

Thistle practiced every morning, but his silly flying pants made him trip. His friend Benny chatted with him, "Why marry failure to your dream?" Thistle expressed frustration. "I’ll never be the winner!"

At last, he ignored the pants and soared high. The crowd cheered, "You’re the winner!" His friends hugged him, and Thistle smiled. Flying did matter—now he knew how.

- Distractor 3: Wrong—text doesn’t compare rabbits/birds.

Topic: a fairy tale about a star that fell to Earth and wanted to go home

Text:
One night, a bright star fell from the sky and landed among the trees in a dark wood. It shone weakly, almost invisible against the background of the night. Sadly, the star could not fly home. It sat on a tree root, its light flickering like a candle.

Nearby, ordinary children played, running active games. The star watched them from its place by a thick trunk, near a child’s outstretched knee. It tried to move, but its speed was too slow. Then, it remembered an simple trick: stars return home by drawing a circle in the sky. With a deep breath, it traced a glowing path. Suddenly, it rose higher and higher—back to its place among the other stars.

Distractors:
1. In a city near a busy street.
2. On a mountain peak.

Explanation:
- The text clearly states the star landed among trees in a dark wood (forest). Distractors 1 and 2 contradict the setting; distractor 3 is indoors, which the text doesn’t mention.

Correct: It was too weak and couldn’t move quickly enough.
Distractors:
1. It was scared of the dark forest.
2. The children were holding it down.
3. It forgot the way home.
Explanation:
- The text says the star was sad and tried to move but its speed was too slow. Distractor 1 misinterprets sad as scared; distractors 2 and 3 are unsupported by the text.

Q3: How did the star finally return to the sky?
Correct: It drew a glowing circle in the air to help it rise.
Distractors:
1. It waited for the children to help it.
2. It called out to the other stars for help.
3. It grew brighter until it floated away.
Explanation:
- The text describes the star drawing a circle as the solution. Distractor 1 ignores the star’s independence; distractors 2 and 3 describe actions not mentioned (communication or brightness alone).



Topic: an adventure story about a foggy day

Text:
Leo, an engineer, worked in a factory but dreamed of adventure. One foggy morning, his employer said, "Take this specific map—it’s definitely safe!" Ignoring warnings, Leo left. Leo promised to marry her daughter if he returned safely. Suddenly, his phone rang—his employer demanded he come back. As Leo hesitated, the fog lifted, revealing a desert instead of the mountains. He laughed. Maybe the factory wasn’t so bad after all.

Distractors:
1. To build a tea factory.
2. To teach her the recipe.
3. To stay in the village forever.

2. He felt scared of the desert.
3. He regretted leaving the factory.

Topic: an adventure story about a desert

Text:
Last week, a group of explorers arrived in a huge, flat desert. Their target was a hidden oasis, but the journey would not be easy. To compete for the prize, they had to cross dangerous land. One explorer, Jake, felt nervous—he had never seen such empty space before.

Their vehicle’s board showed a closed canyon ahead. "We must control our speed," said the leader. Jake started a recording of their adventure. Suddenly, the desert became active—sandstorms blew everywhere! But they kept going until they reached the oasis at last.

Questions:
Q1: Why was Jake nervous?
Correct: He was scared because he had never seen such a vast, empty desert before.
Distractors:
1. He was afraid of the sandstorms. / Wrong: Sandstorms happened later.
2. The canyon was too dangerous. / Wrong: The canyon was mentioned after his nervousness.
3. He didn’t like competing with the group. / Wrong: His fear was about the desert, not competition.

Explanation:
Weak readers might pick Distractor 1 because sandstorms are dramatic, but Jake’s fear came before them. Distractor 2 is tempting because the canyon is dangerous, but it’s described after his nervousness. Distractor 3 misinterprets the text’s focus on the desert as the cause.
---
Q2: What did the explorers use to control their route?
Correct: They used the vehicle’s dashboard (board).
Distractors:
1. A map they carried with them. / Wrong: The text specifies the board.
2. The leader’s instructions. / Wrong: Instructions were verbal; the board was the tool.
3. Their recording device. / Wrong: Recordings were for memory, not navigation.

Explanation:
Distractor 1 confuses board (dashboard) with a map. Distractor 2 ignores the physical board mentioned. Distractor 3 mixes up the recording’s purpose.
---
Q3: How did the desert change during their journey?
Correct: It became stormy with sand blowing everywhere.
Distractors:
1. It started raining heavily. / Wrong: Deserts have sandstorms, not rain.
2. The oasis disappeared. / Wrong: The oasis was their target, not something that vanished.
3. The group got lost. / Wrong: They reached the oasis, so they didn’t get lost.

Explanation:
Distractor 1 is a common desert misconception. Distractor 2 misreads target as a dynamic event. Distractor 3 contradicts the ending (reached the oasis).

Explanation:

Topic: a fairy tale about a boy who could talk to the wind

Later, I performed at a small concert in the mall, and though I was nervous, the applause made me feel proud. Now, I’m not a professional, but I’ve learned that persistence turns mistakes into progress.
