2. Outdoor settings like tents are the best places to practice.
3. Talent is more important than hard work.
Explanation:
- Correct summarizes the narrator’s lesson ("turns mistakes into progress").
- Distractor 1 contradicts the imperfect repairs and early struggles.
- Distractor 2 overgeneralizes the tent’s role (it was one helpful moment).
- Distractor 3 ignores the emphasis on effort ("practiced strongly").



Topic: an adventure story about a mountain trip

Text:
Liam and Jake had always dreamed of climbing Mount Everest Peak, a smaller but still dangerous mountain. Jake, a practical survival expert, reminded Liam of the prediction for storms. "We should postpone," he said. But Liam protested—he wanted to prove he wasn’t afraid. "The weather reports are often wrong," he argued.

They set off, but by noon, dark clouds gathered. The wind howled, and their tent’s wooden frame snapped. Trapped inside, they heard the drumming of rain. Jake felt embarrassed when Liam blamed him: "You judged the conditions poorly!" Jake snapped back, "The mountain was our enemy today!"

Desperate, they used an old drum to signal a rescue helicopter. Exhausted but alive, they reached the summit at dawn. "I was wrong," Liam admitted. Jake smiled: "Next time, we’ll listen to the forecast—and each other."

Topic: a story about an unexpected act of kindness

Text:
Lena owned a small printing shop in a quiet town, where the atmosphere was often damp and gloomy. One rainy afternoon, as she arranged orders, a shivering stranger entered. His emotion was hard to define—a mix of exhaustion and hope. Without thinking, Lena handed him her only luxury umbrella, a gift from her late grandmother. His eyes widened with excitement. "You didn’t have to," he stammered.

Following a brief chat, she learned he was a teacher moving to a new school. The atmosphere in the shop shifted; the rain outside no longer felt heavy. That evening, Lena reflected on the moment. What defines kindness? Not grand gestures, but small ones—like sharing an umbrella on a cold day.

The following week, she pinned a board in her shop: "Kindness Notes." Customers could leave messages for strangers. The stranger, whose name was Clara, returned to thank her. "You gave me more than an umbrella," she said. Lena smiled. Sometimes, the simplest acts create the deepest emotions.

3. The stranger looks dangerous. / Wrong: He’s shivering and hopeful.

Explanation:

The text states the atmosphere shifts from gloomy to warm/hopeful. Distractor 1 confuses emotional warmth with sound, 2 misreads atmosphere literally, and 3 reverses the emotional tone.

Back at the studio, my director reminded me that friendship with the cast makes tough scenes easier. Tonight, I’ll rehearse a frightening chase scene. Personally, I love the challenge, but I’ll take deep breaths if I get nervous. Acting isn’t just a job; it’s a journey of growth.

Topic: a story about a volunteer experience

Text:
When I joined the volunteer team after the explosion, I never expected the battle for survival to be so intense. The air was thick with smoke, and I had to focus on my breath to stay calm. A child coughed violently—I checked her pulse and administered a substance to help her breathe again. The team leader warned us to avoid contact with contaminated water and to watch for animal bites, but our priority was saving lives.

As we moved through the ruins, we gathered intelligence on safe routes, sharing information quickly. There was no profit in this work—just the satisfaction of helping. Later, I wondered about the definition of volunteering: Was it the exhaustion, the fear, or the moments when we turned despair into hope? The experience changed me. I realized that true purpose isn’t measured in money, but in the lives we touch.

In a small village, a young blacksmith named Leo faced unemployment after the mine closed. One day, while searching for accommodation in the ruins of a royal castle, he found a normal but solid silver mirror half-buried in the dirt. Curious, he brushed off the dust and noticed strange symbols written on its frame. An old book in the castle’s library described the mirror’s power: it could show the future.

At first, Leo thought it was a trick, but when he gazed into it, he felt a strange wonder. The mirror revealed a deep secret—a war that would destroy the kingdom in ten years. The vision highlighted a single moment: a child holding a key to stopping the disaster. Leo hesitated. If he told anyone, they might not believe him. But if he kept silent, the kingdom would fall.

That night, Leo stared at the mirror again, wondering if he was brave enough to change history.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Leo initially think the mirror was ordinary?
Correct: It looked normal and unremarkable at first glance.
Distractors:
1. "He had seen similar mirrors in the castle before." / Wrong: The text says it was half-buried in dirt—no prior exposure.
2. "The symbols on it were too complicated to read." / Wrong: He noticed the symbols but didn’t focus on them yet.
3. "It was made of cheap metal." / Wrong: The text describes it as solid silver, implying value.
Explanation: The correct answer matches "normal" (text). Distractors 1/2 misread order; 3 contradicts "solid silver."
---
Q2: What did the mirror reveal about the kingdom’s future?
Correct: It showed a future war that would destroy the kingdom in ten years.
Distractors:
1. "The kingdom would become richer due to a hidden treasure." / Wrong: Text mentions destruction, not wealth.
2. "Leo himself would cause the war." / Wrong: The mirror shows a future event, not his actions.
3. "The mirror would break if used too often." / Wrong: No mention of physical damage.
Explanation: Correct answer paraphrases "war... destroy" (text). Distractors 1/2 misread focus; 3 is unsupported.
---
Q3: How did Leo’s unemployment influence his decision to search the castle ruins?
Correct: He was desperate for work/money, so he risked searching abandoned places.
Distractors:
1. "He wanted to prove he was brave." / Wrong: Text implies practical need, not heroism.
2. "He hoped to find a new trade in the castle." / Wrong: No mention of trade; he was a blacksmith.
3. "His family forced him to look for the mirror." / Wrong: Text says he searched alone.
Explanation: Correct answer links unemployment to desperation (text). Distractors 1/2 add unsupported motives; 3 contradicts agency.
---
Q4: What does the phrase "a child holding a key" suggest about the prophecy?
Correct: A young person would play a crucial role in preventing the disaster.
Distractors:
1. "The child was the mirror’s original owner." / Wrong: Text links the child to the future, not the past.
2. "The key unlocked the mirror’s magic." / Wrong: No action described—just a vision.
3. "The child would become the kingdom’s next ruler." / Wrong: Text says key to stopping disaster, not ruler.
Explanation: Correct answer interprets "key" as symbolic role (text). Distractors 1/2 misread timeline/action; 3 misinterprets purpose.



Topic: a story about a magical pencil that brings drawings to life

Text:
Lena, a struggling artist, found a strange pencil in her grandmother’s attic. When she drew a cake, it smelled like freshly baked cookies. Excited, she drew a drum, but it suddenly started beating loudly in the middle of the market, attracting a crowd. Embarrassed, she tried to explain, but the staff laughed. The "performance" went viral, and soon, people paid high taxes to see her drawings come to life. Though she earned money, she couldn’t account for it properly. One day, she drew a bag of flour, and it turned into a whirlwind, scattering dust everywhere. The result? Fame—but also chaos. Finally, she opened a bank account to manage her earnings and decided to use the pencil wisely.

Topic: a fairy tale about a woman who talks to birds and they help her

Text:
In a quiet village near a dark forest, slow-moving clouds hid the sun as Elara wandered, her mood heavy after losing her home. One evening, a sparrow landed on her shoulder and spoke: "The air smells of chemical—danger is near." The birds, her only friends, warned of poisonous water in the nearby port, where the evil mayor plotted to kill her for her land. Though skeptical, their relation to her was deep, and their words mentioned hidden threats. Finally, they convinced her to flee before the mayor’s guards arrived. As she ran, the birds’ breathing grew faster, signaling urgency. By dawn, the mayor’s plan failed, and Elara’s heart lightened.

Correct: She was skeptical because their advice seemed unusual for animals.
Distractors:

Distractors:

Distractors:

Topic: a story about protecting a magical forest from someone who wants to cut it down

Text:
The villagers of Eldermere lived relaxed lives in the heart of a valuable magical forest. Their economy depended on the outdoors, where the trees whispered secrets and the air shimmered with enchantment. When a southern logging company arrived, claiming the land for profit, the villagers were confused—how could anyone not see the forest’s worth?

The loggers, used to clear-cutting ordinary woods, struggled with communication. "This isn’t just timber," the elders insisted, but the company dismissed their warnings. The villagers realized they needed to act fast. They blocked the loggers’ path ahead, standing firm as the machines roared closer. The forest’s magic surged, and the loggers, confused by the sudden resistance, retreated.

By dawn, the villagers had won. The forest remained untouched, its valuable magic intact, and the southern company left empty-handed. The villagers returned to their relaxed lives, grateful for their unity and the outdoors they had protected.

Questions:
Q1: Why were the villagers initially confused when the loggers arrived?
Correct: They couldn’t understand why the loggers ignored the forest’s magical importance.
Distractors:
1. They were confused about how to communicate with the loggers in their language. / Wrong: No language barrier is mentioned.
2. They were confused because the loggers offered them money to leave. / Wrong: No bribes were offered.
3. They were confused because the forest had disappeared overnight. / Wrong: The forest was never missing.

Explanation:
Weak readers might focus on communication (distractor 1) or misread economy as a bribe (distractor 2). Distractor 3 misinterprets magic as physical disappearance.
---
Q2: How did the villagers’ lifestyle depend on the forest’s magic?
Correct: Their economy relied on the forest’s enchantment, which supported their outdoor way of life.
Distractors:
1. They used the forest’s magic to grow crops for trade. / Wrong: No farming is mentioned.
2. The forest provided them with healing herbs. / Wrong: The focus is on lifestyle, not medicine.
3. They relied on the forest to hide from the logging company. / Wrong: The magic was a value, not a hiding tool.

Explanation:
Distractor 1 assumes economy = trade. Distractor 2 overgeneralizes magic. Distractor 3 confuses protection with hiding.
---
Q3: What happened when the loggers tried to cut down the trees?
Correct: The forest’s magic created resistance, forcing the loggers to retreat.
Distractors:
1. The villagers physically attacked the loggers with weapons. / Wrong: They blocked the path, not fought.
2. The trees grew back instantly after each cut. / Wrong: The magic caused resistance, not regrowth.
3. The loggers were arrested by the local authorities. / Wrong: No authorities are mentioned.

Explanation:
Distractor 1 misreads blocked as violence. Distractor 2 exaggerates magic. Distractor 3 invents authorities.
---
Q4: Why did the logging company leave Eldermere without cutting the forest?
Correct: The villagers’ united action and the forest’s magic made logging impossible.
Distractors:
1. The company received a better offer from another forest. / Wrong: No competing offers are mentioned.
2. The loggers realized the forest was cursed and feared it. / Wrong: They were confused, not afraid.
3. The villagers paid the company to leave. / Wrong: No payment is mentioned.

Explanation:
Distractor 1 assumes profit = alternative deals. Distractor 2 misinterprets confused as cursed. Distractor 3 misreads protection as a bribe.



Topic: a story about buying something online that looked completely different from the picture

Text:
Last week, I saw an online quotation for a beautiful ceramic vase in the "home decor" category. The photo looked elegant, so I confirmed the price and clicked "Buy Now." Unfortunately, the item arrived in a damaged package—and it wasn’t even ceramic! It was a cheap plastic copy. My emotion was one of shock; I had my tongue tied when I realized how different it was from the picture.

I tried to contact the seller, but their communication was terrible. They refused to offer a refund, saying, "The item was clearly mislabeled." I felt forced to return it, even though it was unnecessary to begin with. The mismatch was a clear symptom of their poor quality control. Now, I always check reviews before buying online—this experience taught me a lesson!

Questions:
Q1: Why did the buyer initially think the vase was a good purchase?
Correct: The online photo made it look elegant and expensive.
Distractors:
1. The seller gave a discount on the quotation. / The buyer saw positive reviews before ordering. / It was in the correct category on the website.
Explanation:
- Discount: The text mentions quotation but not a deal.
- Reviews: The buyer learned to check reviews after the bad experience, not before.
- Category: The category was irrelevant to the decision; the issue was the misleading photo.
---
Q2: How did the seller respond to the buyer’s complaint about the incorrect item?
Correct: They refused a refund, saying the item was mislabeled.
Distractors:
1. They offered a partial refund immediately. / They sent a replacement vase right away. / They apologized and fixed the mistake.
Explanation:
- Partial refund: The text says they refused entirely.
- Replacement: They provided no solution.
- Apology: They blamed the buyer ("mislabeled" implies their error, but no apology was given).
---
Q3: What lesson did the buyer learn from this experience?
Correct: They now always check reviews before buying online.
Distractors:
1. They will never buy from that store again. / They should always confirm the package size before ordering. / They realized the quotation was too good to be true.
Explanation:
- Never buy again: The lesson is broader (checking reviews).
- Package size: The issue was the product, not shipping.
- Quotation: The problem was the photo, not the price.
---
Q4: Which word from the text describes the buyer’s reaction when they saw the real vase?
Correct: Emotion (or shock/frustration).
Distractors:
1. Relief. / Curiosity. / Excitement.
Explanation:
- Relief: The buyer was disappointed, not relieved.
- Curiosity: The idiom "had my tongue tied" shows shock, not mild interest.
- Excitement: The vase was a letdown, not a positive surprise.



Topic: a story about a new app that made my life easier

Text:
Last month, I downloaded a new app called QuickCart that slightly changed how I shop online. Before, ordering goods was a hassle—I had to pull out my credit card, wait for the package to arrive, and then deal with returns if something was wrong. But now, everything is faster.

The app lets me scan items in stores and pour them into a virtual cart. If I find something cheaper online, I can compare prices slightly better than before. The delivery is also normal—no surprises—but the app sends a note if my order is delayed, which is fair. However, I noticed something unfair: premium members get discounts before others, even if they’re not loyal customers. My skin turned pale when I saw that!

The only downside? The remote checkout option sometimes glitches, but overall, QuickCart has saved me time and stress. I’d recommend it—just don’t expect everything to be perfect.

Questions:
Q1: What was the main problem with the author’s old shopping method before using QuickCart?
Correct: The process was time-consuming and involved physically handling payments and returns.
Distractors:
1. The app didn’t exist before, so shopping was impossible. (Wrong: The text implies shopping existed but was inefficient.)
2. The author had to carry a physical shopping bag everywhere. (Wrong: The text focuses on online/credit card issues, not physical bags.)
3. Stores charged unfairly high prices for goods. (Wrong: The "unfair" part refers to app discounts, not store prices.)
Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases the text’s description of "hassle" (time + payments/returns). Distractor 1 is absurd; 2 misreads the context (no mention of bags); 3 confuses the app’s discount policy with store pricing.
---
Q2: How does the app’s "virtual cart" feature work?
Correct: Users scan items in stores and add them digitally to a cart within the app.
Distractors:
1. It automatically orders items for you without scanning. (Wrong: The text says users must "scan" items.)
2. You can only use it for online purchases, not in physical stores. (Wrong: The text mentions "scan items in stores.")
3. The cart fills up with physical goods delivered instantly. (Wrong: It’s a "virtual" cart, not physical.)
Explanation:
The correct answer captures "scan" + "virtual cart." Distractor 1 omits the scanning step; 2 contradicts the in-store use; 3 misinterprets "virtual" as physical.
---
Q3: Why did the author feel the app’s discount system was unfair?
Correct: Premium members received discounts before non-premium customers, even if they weren’t loyal.
Distractors:
1. Non-premium users had to pay extra fees for deliveries. (Wrong: The text doesn’t mention fees.)
2. The app crashed when non-premium users tried to order. (Wrong: The "remote checkout glitch" is unrelated to discounts.)
3. Premium users got free goods, while others paid full price. (Wrong: The text says "discounts," not free goods.)
Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases "premium members get discounts before others." Distractor 1 introduces unrelated fees; 2 confuses checkout glitches with discounts; 3 exaggerates ("free goods").
---
Q4: What does the phrase "my skin turned pale" suggest about the author’s reaction?
Correct: The author was shocked or upset by the unfair discount policy.
Distractors:
1. The author was scared because the app hacked their account. (Wrong: No mention of hacking.)
2. The author was excited to see the new features. (Wrong: "Pale" implies a negative reaction.)
3. The author felt sick from looking at the screen too long. (Wrong: Context links "pale" to the unfair policy, not screen strain.)
Explanation:
The correct answer connects "pale" to the author’s emotional response to the "unfair" policy. Distractor 1 introduces hacking (irrelevant); 2 contradicts the negative tone; 3 misinterprets the cause of the reaction.



Topic: a story about finding a magical stone that gives you one wish

Text:
One afternoon, Leo was exploring the forest when he spotted a silly-looking stone half-buried in the dirt. It wasn’t originally there—he was sure of it. The type of rock was unusual, glowing faintly in the sunlight. At first, he thought it was imaginary, just a trick of the light. But when he knocked it gently, a warm breeze whispered, "Make a wish."

Leo hesitated. His attitude was curious but cautious. He counted to three, closed his eyes, and wished for his favorite book. To his pleasure, the book suddenly appeared in his hands! The benefit was obvious: the stone worked. But when he looked back, it had vanished. Had it been real, or just another imaginary dream?

Questions:
Q1: Why did Leo initially think the stone might be imaginary?
Correct: Because it looked unusual and glowed faintly, making him doubt its reality.
Distractors:
1. Because it was glowing faintly and looked strange.
2. Because his friend told him stones like that didn’t exist.
3. Because he had wished for something before and nothing happened.
Explanation:
- Correct: Directly matches "silly-looking... glowing faintly" (B1 detail).
- Distractor 1: Too vague—text specifies shape + glow.
- Distractor 2: Unsupported (no mention of friends).
- Distractor 3: Contradicts the text (wish did work).
---
Q2: How does the text show Leo’s attitude toward the stone changed?
Correct: He started skeptical ("silly-looking") but became excited ("pleasure") when the wish worked.
Distractors:
1. He went from excited to disappointed.
2. He stopped believing in magic completely.
3. He laughed at the stone at first but then took it seriously.
Explanation:
- Correct: Captures "curious but cautious" → "pleasure" (B1 emotional shift).
- Distractor 1: Reversed order (text shows doubt → excitement).
- Distractor 2: Overstated (text doesn’t say he stopped believing).
- Distractor 3: Partially true but misses hesitation.
---
Q3: What does the "warm breeze" suggest about the stone’s magic?
Correct: It suggests the stone’s magic was subtle but real, not just an illusion.
Distractors:
1. That the stone’s magic was loud and scary.
2. That the stone needed to be touched to work.
3. That the magic was gentle and subtle.
Explanation:
- Correct: Inferred from "warm breeze whispered" (B1 subtle detail).
- Distractor 1: Contradicts ("warm breeze" implies gentleness).
- Distractor 2: Unsupported (no touching required).
- Distractor 3: Correct but less precise (text doesn’t specify gentle).
---
Q4: What might have happened if Leo had wished for something silly?
Correct: The wish would have been granted, but Leo might have laughed it off as a joke.
Distractors:
1. The wish wouldn’t have worked.
2. The stone would have disappeared immediately.
3. Leo would have wished for something serious instead.
Explanation:
- Correct: Logical prediction from text (stone grants wishes).
- Distractor 1: Contradicts (text shows it does work).
- Distractor 2: Unsupported (stone vanished after the serious wish).
- Distractor 3: Unlikely (text doesn’t suggest he’d change his mind).



Topic: a story about a ghost who lives in an old library

Text:
In the quiet corners of Old Oak Library, whispers of a ghost linger like forgotten books. The building, once a place of award-winning literary events, now suffers from poverty—peeling walls and dusty shelves. Possibly, the ghost is the spirit of a librarian who vanished decades ago, still aware of her charge: protecting the books. Locals say she glides along the path between shelves, leaving faint footprints. When new librarian Clara arrived, she noticed improvement in the library’s atmosphere—books rearranged, dust vanished. One night, Clara felt a cold breath on her neck. A voice, barely audible, urged her to "contact" the town historian. The mystery deepened when Clara discovered records of a lost connection between the librarian and a famous author. Now, Clara works to restore the library, hoping the ghost will find peace.

Topic: a story about a time I saved money for a long time to buy something special

Text:
When I was 16, I dreamed of buying an original Leica camera—unable to justify the price at first. My passion for photography made the wait worthwhile, especially since it was perfect for outdoor adventures. I compared prices online, but the release of the limited edition made me act fast.

My attitude was simple: no shortcuts. I bit my tongue every time I wanted to splurge on snacks, saving every cent. My discipline felt as hard as iron, but I reminded myself that patience would pay off. Even when friends bought cheaper cameras, I remained focused.

Six months later, the camera was mine. A little money remained—enough for a backup lens. It wasn’t just a purchase; it was proof that hard work and the right attitude could turn dreams into reality.

2. Their friends told them not to buy it. / Wrong: No mention of disapproval.
3. The camera was out of stock. / Wrong: Issue was affordability, not availability.
Explanation:
The correct answer matches the text’s explicit reason (unable to afford). Distractors 1 and 2 misread original or friends as negative cues. Distractor 3 confuses release (new edition) with stock issues.
---
Q2: How did the narrator resist spending money impulsively?
Correct: They avoided buying unnecessary things, like snacks, and saved every cent.

1. They sold old clothes to earn extra money. / Wrong: No mention of selling items.
2. They asked their parents for a loan. / Wrong: Focus is on self-discipline, not borrowing.
3. They worked a part-time job. / Wrong: Text emphasizes cutting expenses, not earning more.

Q3: What did the narrator do with the money that remained after the purchase?
Correct: They bought an additional lens for the camera.
Distractors:

2. They gave it to a friend. / Wrong: No generosity mentioned.
3. They used it to buy a new phone case. / Wrong: Purchase was camera-related.

Topic: a story about a small step that made a big difference in my life

Text:
Approximately ten years ago, I visited a small town where a statue of a local hero stood in the square. The rope holding its broken arm was frayed, and no one seemed to care. I wasn’t very educated about history then, but I decided to fix it myself. As I tied the rope tightly, an old woman mentioned a local reference to the statue—a ghost was said to ring the bell at midnight if the hero’s arm wasn’t secure. Though I laughed at the idea, the bell did ring that night, and the town’s development seemed to improve after my small act. Today, I often reference this story to remind myself that even tiny efforts can create valuable change. The lesson was simple: courage isn’t always loud; sometimes, it’s just a rope, a bell, and a belief.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the narrator decide to fix the statue’s rope, even though they weren’t educated about its history?
Correct: They felt a personal responsibility to act, possibly because the statue represented something meaningful to the community.
Distractors:

Topic: an adventure story about discovering a twin

Text:

Liam laughed. "That’s impossible." But the man pulled out a camera and said, "This is mine—see these photos?" He showed Liam a picture of two children playing. "We were separated at birth," he explained. Skeptical, Liam searched his bag and found a torn piece of cloth—a jacket with both their names stitched inside.

The man’s performance grew more dramatic. "If you don’t believe me, look at this!" He rolled up his sleeve, revealing a scar on his wrist. "I got it when we were five—you must have one too." Liam checked his own arm and gasped. There it was.

Glad to have answers, Liam asked how he could prove he wasn’t a hallucination. The man grinned and handed him the camera. "Take a look at this immediate photo—I took it yesterday." On the screen, Liam saw himself standing next to the man, both wearing the same jacket.

The man’s final tip sealed it: "Check your birth certificate. We’re both listed as twins." Liam realized his living brother had been searching for him for years.

Topic: a letter to my future self

Text:
Dear Future Me,
Surely, when you read this, you’ll laugh at how worried I am about tiny things—like that old key I keep on my desk. It’s not just a key; it’s a weapon against forgetting. One day, you’ll understand how careless I was with water—not the kind you drink, but the kind that flows through your heart. Don’t let it spill over without reason.

I hope you’re doing well. Remember the knock on your door last winter? That wasn’t just a stranger—it was a lesson. Life will require more patience than you think, so treat people with kindness, even when it’s hard. The book located on your shelf, The Quiet Years, is a reference to this moment. Flip through it sometimes; it’ll remind you of who you were.

Take care of yourself. I’m proud of you, even if you don’t believe me yet.
—Past You

Questions:

2. The key unlocks hidden secrets.

Explanation:
- Correct: The text says the key is "a weapon against forgetting," not a tool or object.
- Distractor 1: Misreads weapon literally; the context is metaphorical.

- Distractor 3: Tiny contrasts with weapon’s importance, not weakness.

Q2: What does "careless with water" symbolize?
Correct: Not controlling emotions properly.
Distractors:
1. It means not drinking enough water daily.
2. It refers to wasting money carelessly.
3. It means ignoring physical dehydration.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text clarifies "not the kind you drink" and links water to the heart (emotions).
- Distractor 1: Literalizes water; the text specifies it’s metaphorical.
- Distractor 2: No financial context is given.
- Distractor 3: The text links water to emotions, not the body.
---

Correct: An unexpected but meaningful experience.
Distractors:
1. It was just a random visitor with no significance.
2. It was a warning about potential danger.
3. It was a sign to leave the house immediately.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text says "it wasn’t just a stranger—it was a lesson," implying growth.
- Distractor 1: Ignores the implied lesson in the text.
- Distractor 2: No threat is mentioned; the tone is neutral/positive.
- Distractor 3: The text doesn’t mention urgency or leaving.
---
Q4: How does the book The Quiet Years serve as a "reference"?
Correct: It helps future me remember their past self.
Distractors:
1. It’s a manual for solving future problems.
2. It’s placed on the shelf only for decoration.
3. It contains predictions about future events.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text says "it’ll remind you of who you were," not instruct or predict.
- Distractor 1: "Reference" here is nostalgic, not instructional.
- Distractor 2: The location is mentioned, but the purpose is reminder.
- Distractor 3: The book is a reference to the past, not the future.



Topic: a story about a person who inspired me

Text:
When I was ten, I wrote a story about a dragon, but my teacher called it ‘childish stuff.’ I felt embarrassed and stopped writing for years. Then, in high school, I met Ms. Carter, an editor for our school paper. She noticed my talent when I accidentally submitted a draft to her desk. ‘This has heart,’ she said, and that simple comment caused a shift in how I saw my writing. She pushed me to join a creative writing program, where I learned to organize my ideas better. At first, my stories were rejected, but I was determined to improve. Even after my official debut in a local magazine, I still remember the poison of self-doubt she helped me overcome. Today, I’m grateful for her guidance—without her, I might never have turned my ‘childish stuff’ into a career.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the narrator stop writing after age ten?
Correct: Their teacher’s criticism made them feel too embarrassed to continue.
Distractors:
1. Their parents discouraged creative writing.
2. They found writing too boring and switched to sports.
3. Their first story was too short to be taken seriously.
Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases the text’s mention of "childish stuff" and embarrassment. Distractor 1 is wrong because parents aren’t mentioned; 2 is wrong because the text focuses on writing, not sports; 3 is wrong because the issue was perception (embarrassment), not length.
---
Q2: How did Ms. Carter help the narrator overcome their self-doubt?
Correct: She gave them confidence in their talent and encouraged them to keep improving.
Distractors:
1. She paid for them to attend a writing workshop abroad.
2. She told them to stop caring about what others thought.
3. She edited all their drafts to make them perfect.
Explanation:
The correct answer captures "This has heart" (confidence) and "pushed me to join a program" (encouragement). Distractor 1 is wrong (no mention of workshops); 2 is wrong (she didn’t advise ignoring criticism); 3 is wrong (she noticed talent but didn’t edit their work).
---
Q3: What does the phrase "the poison of self-doubt" refer to?
Correct: The narrator’s lingering insecurity about their writing skills, even after achieving success.
Distractors:
1. The fear of being publicly humiliated by critics.
2. The habit of constantly rewriting stories until they were flawless.
3. The difficulty of choosing topics for new stories.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects the text’s metaphor for internal doubt ("even after my official debut"). Distractor 1 is wrong (doubt is internal, not public); 2 is wrong (no mention of obsessive rewriting); 3 is wrong (doubt isn’t about topic selection).
---
Q4: What was the result of the narrator joining the writing program?
Correct: They developed their skills and eventually published their work in a magazine.
Distractors:
1. They decided to become a teacher instead of a writer.
2. They gave up writing because it was too competitive.
3. They started their own publishing company.
Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with "official debut in a local magazine." Distractor 1 is wrong (no career shift is mentioned); 2 is wrong (they were determined to improve); 3 is wrong (no business venture is implied).



Topic: a story about a lottery winner who donated all the money to charity

Text:
When Mark, a 22-year-old college student, won $5 million in the lottery, his first view was one of shock. Unlike others who might claim wealth changes people for the worse, Mark felt innocent of greed. His university’s policy on charitable donations inspired him: "Why keep money when it can deliver happiness to others?"

At first, he entertained the idea of splurging, but the similarity between his youth and the underprivileged youth his charity helped solidified his choice. He donated everything. Critics called his decision reckless, but Mark argued, "A loss of money is better than a loss of purpose." The charity used the funds to build schools, and Mark became a symbol of generosity.

Some even said he was innocent of the usual lottery temptations. Though he missed the excitement of spending, he found greater joy in seeing smiles on children’s faces. His story proved that wealth’s true value isn’t in owning it, but in what you deliver with it.

Questions:

Correct: He considered spending it first but realized donating would help others like him when he was younger.
Distractors:
1. He was afraid of losing the money. / He didn’t trust the charity’s policy. / His family advised him not to donate.
Explanation:
- Correct matches the text’s explanation of his similarity to the youth helped.
- Distractor 1 misinterprets "loss" as fear; the text shows he chose to donate.
- Distractor 2 contradicts the text (he admired the policy).
- Distractor 3 is unsupported (no family mentioned).
---
Q2: How did the charity use the money Mark donated?
Correct: They used it to construct schools for disadvantaged children.
Distractors:
1. They gave it directly to poor families. / They invested it to grow more funds. / They used it to entertain communities with events.
Explanation:
- Correct aligns with "build schools."
- Distractor 1 generalizes "aid" but misses the specific use.
- Distractor 2 contradicts the immediate delivery of aid.
- Distractor 3 confuses Mark’s entertaining the idea with the charity’s actions.
---
Q3: What does the phrase "a loss of money is better than a loss of purpose" suggest about Mark’s priorities?
Correct: He valued helping others more than keeping wealth.
Distractors:
1. He regretted winning the lottery. / He thought keeping money would make him worse off. / He wanted to punish himself for winning.
Explanation:
- Correct reflects his moral choice over materialism.
- Distractor 1 misreads "loss" as regret; the text shows pride.
- Distractor 2 inverts the comparison (he saw donating as better).
- Distractor 3 adds unsupported motivation.

Q4: Why might critics have called Mark’s decision "reckless"?
Correct: Because most lottery winners spend their money instead of donating it all.
Distractors:

Explanation:
- Correct ties to the text’s contrast with typical winners.
- Distractor 1 assumes poor planning; the text focuses on social norms.
- Distractor 2 contradicts the text (he researched the charity).
- Distractor 3 ignores the text’s positive framing of his youth.



Topic: a story about a very noisy neighbor and how I solved the problem

Text:
Last summer, my neighbor’s loud music became a nightmare. Every evening, the bass pulled through my walls, making me feel frozen in place. I tried to reflect on how to handle it—yelling wouldn’t help, and I didn’t want to ruin our spirit of community.

One day, I saw a headline in the local paper: "Neighbors Should Communicate Before Complaining." That gave me an idea. I knocked on their door and explained how the noise affected my work. They apologized, saying they decorated their place with speakers and didn’t realize how loud they were—their apartment had a great view of the park, so they often left the windows open.

I suggested they use earplugs or lower the volume after 10 PM. At first, they resisted, but I reminded them how I’d breathe easier if they did. Slowly, my patience sank, but I stayed calm. Finally, they agreed, and the next week, the music stopped by 10 PM. Now, we even chat sometimes, and the noise is just a memory.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the narrator initially avoid yelling at the neighbor?
Correct: To maintain the community spirit.
Distractors:
1. "They were afraid of legal consequences." / Wrong: Text emphasizes community, not laws.
2. "The neighbor was violent." / Wrong: Neighbor apologized; no aggression.
3. "They didn’t want to disturb the neighbor’s view." / Wrong: View was the neighbor’s issue, not the narrator’s.

Explanation:
The correct answer fits because the text states "didn’t want to ruin our spirit of community." Distractors 1 and 2 contradict the peaceful tone; distractor 3 misattributes the view as a conflict point.
---
Q2: How did the newspaper headline inspire the narrator’s solution?
Correct: It reminded them that communication was better than complaining.
Distractors:
1. "It warned about noise laws." / Wrong: Headline is about communication, not laws.
2. "It offered a discount on earplugs." / Wrong: Earplugs were the narrator’s idea.
3. "It described a similar neighbor problem." / Wrong: Headline is general advice.

Explanation:
The correct answer matches "Neighbors Should Communicate Before Complaining." Distractors 1 and 3 misrepresent the headline’s purpose; distractor 2 adds irrelevant details.
---
Q3: What two practical suggestions did the narrator give to the neighbor?
Correct: Use earplugs and lower the volume after 10 PM.
Distractors:
1. "Close the windows and turn off the music." / Wrong: Windows were open for the view; music wasn’t banned.
2. "Move the speakers to another room." / Wrong: No mention of speaker location.
3. "Pay for soundproofing." / Wrong: No financial solution proposed.

Explanation:
The correct answer is directly quoted ("use earplugs or lower the volume after 10 PM"). Distractors 1 and 2 overstep the text’s suggestions; distractor 3 invents a cost-based fix.
---
Q4: What changed in the narrator’s relationship with the neighbor after the problem was solved?
Correct: They started chatting and the tension disappeared.
Distractors:
1. "The neighbor moved out." / Wrong: No mention of moving.
2. "They became best friends." / Wrong: "Chat sometimes" is too vague for this.
3. "The narrator installed soundproofing." / Wrong: No action by narrator.

Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases "Now, we even chat sometimes, and the noise is just a memory." Distractors 1 and 3 contradict the text; distractor 2 overinterprets "chat."



Topic: a story about sending a message to a wrong person

Text:
Liam hurriedly wrote a note to his friend about a concert at the stadium, but he accidentally sent it to a shy gardener named Clara. When he checked his phone, he saw the wrong version of the message—his words had mixed with a complex gardening app. Panicked, he took a deep breath and tried to delete it, but the board (a glitchy forum) froze. The battle with technology lasted hours. Meanwhile, Clara, confused by the soil-related emojis, thought Liam was inviting her to a gardening battle at the stadium. Feeling unfairly blamed, she ignored his calls. Too shy to explain, she just smiled awkwardly when he finally met her in person.

Q1: Why did Liam’s message confuse Clara?
Correct: Because the message mixed with a gardening app, adding soil-related emojis that made it seem like an invitation to a gardening event.
Distractors:
1. She misunderstood the stadium name. (Wrong) – The text doesn’t mention confusion about the name; it’s about the content (emojis/app mix).
2. She thought it was a joke about dirt. (Wrong) – The text says she was confused, not amused.
3. Liam sent the message to the wrong number. (Wrong) – The error was sending to the wrong person, not a wrong number.

Explanation:
- Correct matches the text’s detail about the app mix and soil emojis.
- Distractors 1/2 ignore the technical glitch (app mix) and focus on unrelated ideas.
- Distractor 3 misreads "wrong person" as "wrong number."

Q2: How did Liam react when he realized his mistake?
Correct: He panicked, took a deep breath, and tried to delete the message, but the forum froze.
Distractors:
1. He immediately called Clara to apologize. (Wrong) – The text says he tried to delete it first.
2. He laughed it off and sent another message. (Wrong) – No laughter or quick fix is mentioned.
3. He blamed his phone for the error. (Wrong) – The text shows his action (panicking), not blaming the phone.

Explanation:
- Correct reflects the sequence of actions (panic → breath → deletion attempt).
- Distractors 1/2/3 misrepresent his inactive responses (no call, no laughter, no blame).

Q3: Why didn’t Clara correct Liam’s misunderstanding?
Correct: She was too shy to explain the confusion.
Distractors:

2. Liam never contacted her again. (Wrong) – He did meet her later.
3. She thought it was a test of his patience. (Wrong) – No evidence of this in the text.

- Distractors 1/2/3 introduce unmentioned motives or events.

Correct: It suggests he struggled for a long time to fix the mistake because the forum was glitchy.
Distractors:
1. He enjoyed troubleshooting the problem. (Wrong) – "Battle" implies struggle, not enjoyment.
2. The gardening app was fighting back. (Wrong) – The text describes a technical glitch, not a sentient app.
3. He gave up after one attempt. (Wrong) – The text says the battle lasted hours, implying persistence.

Explanation:
- Correct aligns with the duration ("lasted hours") and frustration ("battle").
- Distractors 1/2/3 misread the metaphor (struggle vs. enjoyment/fantasy).



Topic: a story about how I use modern technologies to learn English

Text:
When I was a child, I ignored most technology—my parents even called me a "bookworm." But after moving abroad, I realized I couldn’t ignore English anymore. Now, I use modern tools apart from textbooks. For example, I watch helicopter videos on YouTube to learn technical vocabulary, and the shiny interface of apps like Duolingo keeps me motivated.

I also follow English-speaking influencers on social media, which helps me understand slang. My favorite trick is using a language-exchange account on Tandem, where I chat with natives. Sometimes, I fasten my learning by listening to podcasts while commuting. Even my doctor recommended this treatment—learning through immersion!

Looking back, my childhood was simple, but today’s net of digital resources makes English feel absolutely achievable. Without these tools, I’d still be stuck in beginner’s classes!

3. They saw a helicopter video that changed their mind. / Wrong: Helicopter videos are just one example of how they use tech, not the reason they started.

Explanation: The correct answer paraphrases "I realized I couldn’t ignore English anymore" (line 2). Distractors 1–3 misread causal relationships or confuse examples with causes.

3. A fast internet connection they use to stream content. / Wrong: While fast internet helps, the text emphasizes resources, not speed.

Explanation: "Net" is used figuratively for digital tools (line 10). Distractors 1–3 ignore the metaphor or overfocus on unrelated details.

2. They fasten their seatbelts before starting a lesson. / Wrong: "Fasten" refers to speeding up learning, not seatbelts.
3. They ignore their phone to focus on speaking practice. / Wrong: The text says they use tech, not ignore it.

Explanation: The correct answer rephrases "I fasten my learning by listening to podcasts while commuting" (line 7). Distractors 1–3 confuse examples or misread verbs.



Topic: a story about a time I felt truly grateful

One afternoon, I watched my sports coach struggle during practice. His pale face and slow movements showed how serious his illness was. Later, he thanked me, saying I’d set an example of gratitude. That moment taught me that true appreciation isn’t about grand gestures but recognizing the people who shape our lives.

Explanation: The correct answer matches the dual observation (pain + kindness), while distractors misrepresent the coach’s behavior.

2. He did it to avoid feeling guilty. / Wrong: Text shows proactive gratitude.
3. The coach demanded it as payment. / Wrong: No transactional motive is implied.
Explanation: The correct answer reflects personal meaning, while distractors assume negative or transactional motives.

Distractors:

2. He wants to become a role model in sports. / Wrong: No career shift is mentioned.
3. He feels superior to others for his kindness. / Wrong: Tone is humble, not arrogant.

Topic: a story about an unusual person I met while travelling

Text:
"Last summer in Marrakech, I met a man who was anything but ordinary. His name was Hassan, and from the moment he greeted me with a smile that seemed to flow effortlessly, I knew he was different. He wore an original patchwork jacket and carried a carved stick, which he used to point out the ‘true’ spices in the market—substances he claimed were ‘lost to modern life.’ Hassan visited the souk regularly, always stopping to chat with vendors, even voting for the best tea stall each week. One afternoon, we wandered near the river, where he insisted on teaching me to swim. I nearly sank, but he laughed and pulled me up, saying, ‘Even the bravest are victims of the current sometimes.’ Personally, I was amazed by his confidence. Later, I learned he’d spent years traveling alone, sticking to traditions most tourists ignore. By the time I left, I’d become just as mad for adventure as he was."

CEFR B1 Features Demonstrated:
- Lexical Range: High-frequency words (e.g., "different," "insisted") + target words integrated naturally.
- Grammar:
  - Past simple/continuous for narrative ("met," "was visiting").
  - Passive voice ("were lost") for cultural explanations.
  - Modal verbs ("could," "would") for hypotheticals ("I could have sunk").
  - Complex sentences with relative clauses ("which he used to point out").
- Discourse:
  - Logical progression (description → action → reflection).
  - Connectors ("from the moment," "by the time") for cohesion.
  - Informal tone ("mad for adventure") to match B1 conversational style.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the narrator almost drown in the river?
Correct: Hassan taught the narrator to swim, but the current was stronger than expected.
Distractors:
1. The narrator refused to use Hassan’s stick for support. (Wrong: Text says Hassan used the stick to point out spices, not for swimming.)
2. Hassan pushed the narrator into the water as a joke. (Wrong: Text describes Hassan pulling the narrator up, not pushing.)
3. The river was unusually shallow, causing the narrator to trip. (Wrong: Text implies the current was the issue, not depth.)
Explanation: The correct answer paraphrases the text’s cause ("current was stronger") without copying verbatim. Distractors exploit misreadings: (1) confuses the stick’s purpose, (2) misinterprets Hassan’s actions, (3) invents a detail about depth.
---
Q2: What does “substances” refer to in the text?
Correct: Spices or ingredients Hassan believed were no longer used in modern cooking.
Distractors:
1. Illegal drugs sold in the market. (Wrong: Text describes spices, not drugs, and Hassan’s tone is positive.)
2. Herbal remedies Hassan used for healing. (Wrong: Text mentions "spices," not remedies.)
3. The water in the river that nearly made the narrator sink. (Wrong: "Substances" refers to market items, not the river.)
Explanation: The correct answer rephrases "spices/ingredients" as "no longer used." Distractors (1) and (2) assume negative connotations or shift contexts; (3) misplaces the reference.
---
Q3: How did Hassan’s actions show he was “mad”?
Correct: He ignored modern tourist habits, followed old traditions, and took risks like teaching swimming to strangers.
Distractors:
1. He was mentally unstable and acted unpredictably. (Wrong: Text describes eccentricity, not instability.)
2. He voted for every tea stall to win money. (Wrong: Text says he "voted for the best," implying judgment, not greed.)
3. He refused to let the narrator try local food. (Wrong: Text implies Hassan was adventurous, not restrictive.)
Explanation: The correct answer combines Hassan’s traits ("ignored modern habits," "took risks"). Distractors (1) misinterprets "mad" as mental illness; (2) and (3) contradict the text’s positive portrayal.
---
Q4: Why did Hassan use a carved stick?
Correct: To point out authentic spices in the market that most people overlooked.
Distractors:
1. He needed it to walk because he had a disability. (Wrong: Text doesn’t mention disability; the stick is for pointing.)
2. It was a gift from a local vendor he trusted. (Wrong: Text doesn’t explain its origin, only its use.)
3. He used it to stir the tea he voted for each week. (Wrong: Text separates the stick’s use (pointing) from voting.)
Explanation: The correct answer paraphrases "point out spices." Distractors (1) adds unsupported detail, (2) invents context, (3) mixes unrelated actions.

Topic: a story about how I found my career path

Text:
When I was 16, my fear of public speaking almost stopped me from joining the school debate club. But my teacher, an immigrant from Mexico, encouraged me to try. My first assignment was to present a short speech—something I found embarrassing at first. However, I realized that my passion for storytelling could be a resource for others.

Years later, while studying film at university, I worked on a student album of short films. One project involved shooting a documentary about local immigrants. I noticed how their stories were rarely written about in mainstream media. This made me think: What if I could represent their voices? Now, I work as a documentary filmmaker, focusing on underheard stories. My early spending on a cheap camera was the best investment I ever made—it led me to my career.

3. Their parents forbade them. (Wrong—no mention of parental restrictions.)
Explanation: The text states "my fear of public speaking almost stopped me," so the correct answer directly matches this. Distractors 1–3 misrepresent the text’s focus on personal fear vs. external influences.

Distractors:
1. It made them quit university. (Wrong—they continued studying film.)
2. They won an award for it. (Wrong—no awards are mentioned.)
3. It taught them how to edit professionally. (Wrong—the focus was on storytelling, not editing.)

Distractors:

Topic: a story about a time I realized I was wrong about something

Text:
Last summer, I was set on the idea that Eastern cultures always copied Western fashion. My prediction seemed logical—after all, what was fashionable in Tokyo or Seoul was often a delayed version of Paris or New York. Determined to prove my point, I spent weeks researching trends, even trying to cheat the system by predicting next year’s colors. But when I visited Kyoto, I realized my aim was flawed.

The Eastern artisans I met took pride in their separate traditions, not imitation. Their effort wasn’t about following global trends but preserving centuries-old techniques. There was a plot twist: their "outdated" designs became the most sought-after. This experience taught me the connection between respect and creativity—I had been wrong, and my initial assumptions were shallow.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the narrator initially believe Eastern fashion followed Western trends?
Correct: They assumed global trends spread uniformly, with Eastern styles lagging behind Western ones.
Distractors:
1. They had read a study proving Eastern designers copied Western styles.
2. They saw evidence in social media trends.
3. They thought imitation was a sign of cultural inferiority.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects the narrator’s unexamined assumption. Distractor 3 is tempting but not explicitly stated—the text implies arrogance, not explicit inferiority claims.
---
Q2: How did the narrator’s visit to Kyoto change their perspective?
Correct: They learned that Eastern traditions valued originality over global trends, contradicting their earlier belief.
Distractors:
1. They discovered Eastern fashion was actually more fashionable than Western.
2. The artisans admitted they secretly copied Western designs.
3. They realized fashion trends were irrelevant in Eastern culture.
Explanation:
The correct answer captures the shift from imitation to originality. Distractor 3 is misleading—text doesn’t claim trends were irrelevant, just that Eastern values were misunderstood.
---
Q3: What does the phrase "cheat the system" suggest about the narrator’s attitude?
Correct: They were frustrated by their inability to prove their prediction and resorted to manipulative research methods.
Distractors:
1. They wanted to deceive the artisans into confirming their bias.
2. They used unfair advantages to win a fashion competition.
3. They felt the fashion industry was rigged against independent thinkers.
Explanation:
The correct answer shows self-deception. Distractor 3 is a stretch—text focuses on personal effort, not systemic critique.
---
Q4: What lesson did the narrator learn from the artisans?
Correct: True creativity requires respect for cultural roots, not blind imitation of global trends.
Distractors:
1. Fashion trends are meaningless without commercial success.
2. Eastern cultures are naturally more creative than Western ones.
3. The narrator should have trusted their initial instincts more.
Explanation:
The correct answer summarizes the humility lesson. Distractor 3 is a common misreading—readers might assume the narrator’s bias was partially correct, but the text rejects it entirely.

Topic: a story about a time capsule

Years later, his grandchildren pulled it out during a family reunion. However, they couldn’t agree on who should open it first, and the argument grew so serious that they took it to court. The judge ruled that the capsule belonged to the family, but the local museum’s management offered to help personally by organizing an exhibition. Mr. Thompson, now 85, smiled and said, “I never expected this much drama from a simple time capsule!”

Topic: a story about an unusual reading club

Text:
In a small town, a group of friends started an unusual reading club. Their central idea? Reading books backwards—from the last page to the first! "Books often have hidden meanings," he explains. However, some members suffer from confusion. "It’s not proper," complains Tom, but others argue it’s ours to decide.

The group debates whether their method is valid. "If you read backwards, you’ll see the ending first," says Lisa. "It changes how you appreciate the story!" Although some struggle, they persist. Their unique approach has even led them to rediscover old books others ignore. "We don’t care about tradition," laughs Maria. "As long as we’re honest about our thoughts, it’s worth it."

- Distractor 1: Misinterprets suffer as a goal (speed) rather than a challenge.
- Distractor 2: Overlooks the group’s collaborative decision-making.

- Correct: Reflects guides them directly without strict rules.

- Distractor 2: Confuses coach with a storyteller (no evidence).

Explanation:
- Correct: Paraphrases changes how you appreciate the story.

The first task required participants to bury a cursed artifact deep underground. A photographer from the school newspaper stood nearby, snapping pictures of the spellcasting. "Each spell is worth ten points," announced Professor Vexley. Luna’s incantation shimmered as she buried the artifact, but her rival, Malachi, used a diamond-encrusted staff to cheat.

In the final round, Luna’s spell blurred the line between magic and reality, making the judges vanish. The crowd gasped as the diamond trophy glowed in her hands. Though Malachi protested, the total was clear: Luna won by 20 points.

At first, Liam hesitated, but Mr. Carter stayed calm and patient. Slowly, Liam’s frightened expression softened. By the exhibition, he was grateful, even calling his photos a feature of the event.
