Topic: a detective story about deciphering a letter

Text:
Detective Carter received a letter with a strange label during the mayor’s re-election campaign. Generally, the mayor was honest, but the envelope’s fresh ink made Carter suspicious. At the letter’s arrival, he noticed a torn page—someone had tried to hide a name.

Three people could have participated in the forgery: the mayor’s assistant, his rival, or a journalist. Carter didn’t want to bother the mayor, but the clues were too important. He revised his theories after finding a partial signature. "Was this a trick," he muttered, "or a warning?"

His effective questioning led to the assistant’s confession. "I didn’t do it!" she cried, but Carter had proof. The letter wasn’t about guilt—it was blackmail. The mayor had been forced to pay, but the assistant had guilty knowledge. The campaign scandal ended before it began.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Detective Carter suspect the letter was fake?
Correct: The ink was fresh, and the torn page suggested someone had tried to hide part of the letter.
Distractors:
1. The mayor had a reputation for lying. / Wrong: The text says he was generally honest.
2. The handwriting matched the journalist’s. / Wrong: Handwriting isn’t mentioned.
3. The letter arrived during a political rally. / Wrong: The text only mentions a campaign, not a specific event.

Explanation:
The correct answer matches the fresh ink and torn page clues. Distractor 1 contradicts "generally honest." Distractor 2 introduces unsupported detail. Distractor 3 misinterprets "campaign" as an event.
---
Q2: How did the torn page change Carter’s investigation?
Correct: It showed someone had tried to hide a name, making him reconsider his suspects.
Distractors:
1. It proved the mayor was guilty. / Wrong: The letter was blackmail, not proof of guilt.
2. He realized the assistant was the only suspect. / Wrong: Three people were possible participants.
3. The page contained a coded message. / Wrong: It hid a name, not a code.

Explanation:
The correct answer links the torn page to hiding a name. Distractor 1 misinterprets "guilty" as legal guilt. Distractor 2 ignores the three suspects. Distractor 3 assumes "torn" = code.
---
Q3: What was the assistant’s role in the blackmail plot?
Correct: She knew about the blackmail but claimed she wasn’t involved.
Distractors:
1. She was the mastermind behind the blackmail. / Wrong: She had knowledge, not control.
2. She destroyed the letter to protect the mayor. / Wrong: The text implies she was forced to participate.
3. She participated in writing the letter. / Wrong: She knew about it but didn’t write it.

Explanation:
The correct answer reflects "guilty knowledge" + her denial. Distractor 1 overstates her role. Distractor 2 contradicts "forced." Distractor 3 misreads "participated."
---
Q4: Why did the mayor’s campaign end early?
Correct: A blackmail scandal involving him was exposed.
Distractors:
1. The detective publicly accused him of a crime. / Wrong: It was blackmail, not a crime.
2. His rival leaked the letter to the press. / Wrong: The assistant confessed, not a leak.
3. The torn page made voters distrust him. / Wrong: The text doesn’t link the page to public distrust.

Explanation:
The correct answer cites blackmail. Distractor 1 confuses blackmail with crime. Distractor 2 ignores the assistant’s role. Distractor 3 assumes visual clues caused distrust.



Topic: a story about unusual trends in interior design

Text:
Unusual Trends in Interior Design
Recently, a headline in Design Magazine declared that interior designers are pushing boundaries with highly unconventional materials. One of the most talked-about trends is the use of leather for walls and furniture, which creates a bold, rough texture. In comparison to traditional fabrics, leather represents durability and luxury, though it requires careful maintenance.

Another surprising choice is incorporating soil into decor—some designers mix it with resin to create unique, earthy surfaces. As one designer quoted in the article said, "We’re bringing nature indoors in ways people never expected." Even more unusual are homes featuring helicopters as coffee tables or rough, industrial metal sculptures. Technology now assists in making these ideas a reality, with 3D printing and custom fabrication tools.

These trends represent a shift toward personal expression in home design. While not everyone will adopt a leather-clad living room or a soil-infused bathroom, they prove that creativity knows no limits. If you want to stand out, why not experiment with something highly original?

Questions:
Q1: Why does the text mention leather as a "bold" material?
Correct: Its rough texture and association with durability and luxury make it stand out.
Distractors:
1. Because it is cheaper than traditional fabrics. / Wrong: Text implies luxury, not cost.
2. Because it is easier to clean. / Wrong: No mention of maintenance ease.
3. Because it is only used in modern cities. / Wrong: No geographic limitation stated.
Explanation:
The correct answer references the text’s description of leather’s "rough texture" and its symbolic link to durability/luxury. Distractor 1 misinterprets "bold" as "cheap"; distractor 2 ignores the focus on texture; distractor 3 overgeneralizes from "unusual trends."
---
Q2: How does technology help designers create unusual trends?
Correct: It assists with 3D printing and custom fabrication.
Distractors:
1. It provides free design software. / Wrong: Text specifies fabrication tools.
2. It reduces the cost of materials. / Wrong: No mention of cost.
3. It allows designers to work remotely. / Wrong: Focus is on physical creation.
Explanation:
The correct answer matches the text’s explicit mention of "3D printing and custom fabrication." Distractor 1 confuses technology with software; distractor 2 misreads "assists" as financial aid; distractor 3 shifts focus to location.
---
Q3: What does the quotation suggest about modern interior design?
Correct: It suggests designers are bringing nature indoors in unexpected ways.
Distractors:
1. It suggests that soil is the most popular material. / Wrong: Quotation is about nature broadly.
2. It implies that traditional designs are disappearing. / Wrong: No claim about disappearance.
3. It means designers should avoid using leather. / Wrong: Leather is mentioned separately.
Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases the designer’s quote about "nature indoors." Distractor 1 narrows the quotation to soil; distractor 2 overinterprets; distractor 3 misconnects examples.
---
Q4: According to the text, what is one reason someone might choose an unconventional design?
Correct: To stand out or express personal creativity.
Distractors:
1. Because it is required by law. / Wrong: No legal mention.
2. Because it is always cheaper. / Wrong: Text implies luxury.
3. Because traditional designs are banned. / Wrong: No ban mentioned.
Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with the text’s conclusion about "personal expression." Distractor 1 introduces regulations; distractor 2 contradicts "luxury"; distractor 3 distorts the creative theme.



Topic: an adventure story about a servant who overhears her master's secret

Text:
Lydia had worked for Lord Harroway for five years, but she had never seen his impressive wealth up close—until the night she overheard his western business partner. The two men spoke in hushed tones about a bank deposit in Switzerland, a deal so effective it would ruin their rivals totally. Lydia, talented at listening but frozen with fear, pressed herself against the wall.

The next morning, she measured the risk: if she told anyone, she’d be fired. But if she stayed silent, she’d be complicit. Determined to understand, she began educating herself in secret, borrowing books from the neighbourhood’s small library. Weeks later, she confronted Harroway. “You’re not just rich,” she said. “You’re dangerous.” His smile didn’t waver—he had totally underestimated her.

Questions:
Q1: Why was Lydia frozen with fear when she overheard the conversation?
Correct: She realized the deal was illegal and would harm innocent people.
Distractors:
1. She was scared because Harroway was shouting at her. / Wrong: They spoke in hushed tones.
2. She thought Harroway would fire her immediately. / Wrong: She feared being fired if she told anyone, not that he’d act then.
3. She recognized the business partner as a criminal. / Wrong: Fear came from the deal’s nature, not recognition.

Explanation:
The correct answer captures the text’s focus on the illegal deal’s consequences. Distractor 1 ignores hushed tones; distractor 2 misreads the conditional fear; distractor 3 invents a detail about recognition.
---
Q2: How did Lydia educate herself about Lord Harroway’s secret?
Correct: She borrowed books from the neighbourhood library to learn about the law.
Distractors:
1. She asked Harroway to explain the deal to her. / Wrong: She acted in secret.
2. She hired a lawyer to investigate. / Wrong: No mention of hiring anyone.
3. She overheard more conversations from other servants. / Wrong: The text specifies books.

Explanation:
The correct answer matches borrowing books. Distractor 1 assumes direct confrontation; distractor 2 adds unsupported detail; distractor 3 misremembers the method.
---
Q3: What made Lord Harroway’s business deal effective but also dangerous?
Correct: It would make him wealthy but harm his rivals, risking exposure.
Distractors:
1. It was effective because it was legal but dangerous because it was risky. / Wrong: The text implies it was illegal.
2. It was effective because it was secret but dangerous because it involved a bank. / Wrong: The bank is a tool; danger is the illegal act.
3. It was effective because it was fast but dangerous because it was complicated. / Wrong: No mention of speed or complexity.

Explanation:
The correct answer links wealth and ruining rivals. Distractor 1 contradicts illegal; distractor 2 misidentifies the danger; distractor 3 invents details.
---
Q4: Why did Lydia decide to confront Harroway instead of staying silent?
Correct: She couldn’t live with keeping a secret that made her an accomplice.
Distractors:
1. She wanted to blackmail him for money. / Wrong: Her motive was moral, not financial.
2. She was ordered to by her family. / Wrong: No family is mentioned.
3. She thought he would reward her for her loyalty. / Wrong: His smile suggests he saw her as a threat.

Explanation:
The correct answer reflects complicit. Distractor 1 assumes greed; distractor 2 adds unsupported context; distractor 3 misinterprets his reaction.



Topic: a story about a deep-sea biologist who discovered a new ecosystem

Text:
Dr. Elena Vasquez, a qualified deep-sea biologist, had always dreamed of discovering a new ecosystem. After years of planning, she finally set sail on the Maris Profundus, a research vessel equipped with cutting-edge technology. While analyzing sonar data, she noticed an unusual plot of light reflections at 3,000 meters deep—something no one had documented before.

Curious, Elena sent a remotely operated vehicle (ROV) down. To her shock, the ROV’s cameras revealed a thriving community of bioluminescent jellyfish, freeze-resistant fish, and a kind of coral never seen before. Excited, she called her team, but her excitement turned embarrassing when she accidentally played her old rugby highlights instead of the footage—she had pressed the wrong button!

Realizing her mistake, Elena took responsibility and determined to correct it. She worked backwards through the data, confirming the discovery. Though the initial mix-up was a setback, her persistence paid off. The new ecosystem, now named Vasquez’s Abyss, became a landmark in marine science.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Dr. Vasquez initially feel embarrassed during her discovery?
Correct: She accidentally played her rugby highlights instead of the ROV footage.
Distractors:
1. She forgot to charge the ROV’s batteries. (Wrong: Text mentions no battery issue.)
2. Her team laughed at her for wearing an old lab coat. (Wrong: No mention of clothing.)
3. The ecosystem turned out to be a hoax. (Wrong: The discovery was real.)
Explanation:
The correct answer is supported by "accidentally played her old rugby highlights" (direct evidence). Distractor 1 is plausible (common tech issues) but unsupported. Distractor 2 is a red herring (clothing details are irrelevant). Distractor 3 contradicts the text’s confirmation of the ecosystem.
---
Q2: How did Dr. Vasquez confirm her discovery after the mistake?
Correct: She reanalyzed the data by working backwards through the recordings.
Distractors:
1. She sent the ROV down again with a new camera. (Wrong: Text says she worked with existing data.)
2. Her team voted to trust her initial observations. (Wrong: No team vote is mentioned.)
3. She consulted a senior colleague for a second opinion. (Wrong: No consultation is described.)
Explanation:
The correct answer matches "worked backwards through the data" (paraphrased from "worked backwards"). Distractor 1 misinterprets the method (reanalysis vs. resending). Distractor 2 adds unsupported collaboration. Distractor 3 introduces irrelevant steps.
---
Q3: What does the term "plot" refer to in the sentence "an unusual plot of light reflections"?
Correct: A pattern or area of unusual data on the sonar scan.
Distractors:
1. A secret plan to hide the discovery. (Wrong: "Plot" here means data, not deception.)
2. A map of the ship’s route. (Wrong: Route maps aren’t called "plots" in sonar.)
3. A group of scientists’ research goals. (Wrong: Context is technical, not organizational.)
Explanation:
The correct answer explains "plot" as a technical term (B1-level inference). Distractor 1 confuses "plot" with narrative plotting. Distractor 2 misapplies nautical terms. Distractor 3 ignores the scientific context.
---
Q4: Why was the new ecosystem named after Dr. Vasquez?
Correct: Because she was the first to discover and document it.
Distractors:
1. Her university required all discoveries to be named after researchers. (Wrong: No policy is stated.)
2. The jellyfish in it resembled her rugby ball. (Wrong: No connection to rugby is made.)
3. She donated money to fund the research vessel. (Wrong: No funding is mentioned.)
Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases "landmark in marine science" (implied by her discovery). Distractor 1 adds unsupported bureaucracy. Distractor 2 humorously misreads the text. Distractor 3 introduces irrelevant financial details.



Topic: a story of a person who turned their hobby into a successful business

Text:
Maria loved baking cakes as a hobby, but turning it into a business seemed frightening. One day, she decided to sell her creations at a local market. To her surprise, the idea spread quickly—people loved her unique flavors. Local authority figures even praised her work, which gave her confidence. She defined her niche: handmade cakes for celebrations like weddings and birthdays.

Within a year, her small stall became popular. Customers were always grateful for her attention to detail, and word of mouth helped her grow. Plus, she enjoyed the creativity of designing new recipes. By 2023, her business had approximately 500 regular customers. Maria realized that her success came from combining her passion with hard work. Today, she’s proud to say her hobby turned into a dream—one she never expected to celebrate so big.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Maria initially find turning her hobby into a business frightening?
Correct: She was unsure if her hobby could become a profitable business.
Distractors:
1. She didn’t have enough money to start. / Wrong: Text doesn’t mention money.
2. Her family discouraged her from baking. / Wrong: No family mentioned.
3. She was afraid of failing at baking. / Wrong: Fear was about business, not skill.
Explanation: The text states she found turning it into a business frightening, not her baking ability or finances. Distractors misread "frightening" as financial/skill-based fear.
---
Q2: How did local authority figures help Maria’s business grow?
Correct: They praised her work, which boosted her confidence.
Distractors:
1. They gave her a loan to expand. / Wrong: No mention of loans.
2. They helped her find suppliers. / Wrong: Suppliers aren’t discussed.
3. They advertised her business for free. / Wrong: Text implies moral support, not promotion.
Explanation: The text specifies they "praised her work," not provided resources. Distractors assume authority figures act like investors or marketers.
---
Q3: What does the word "plus" refer to in the text?
Correct: She enjoyed the creative process of designing new recipes.
Distractors:
1. She made a lot of profit. / Wrong: Profit isn’t mentioned.
2. She received awards for her cakes. / Wrong: No awards are referenced.
3. Her customers were very loyal. / Wrong: Loyalty isn’t the focus of "plus."
Explanation: "Plus" directly follows "she enjoyed the creativity," linking to her design process. Distractors confuse "plus" with outcomes (profit/awards) or traits (loyalty).
---
Q4: According to the text, what two things combined to make Maria’s business successful?
Correct: Her passion for baking and hard work.
Distractors:
1. Hard work and marketing skills. / Wrong: Marketing isn’t mentioned.
2. Creativity and customer feedback. / Wrong: Feedback isn’t paired with creativity.
3. Local support and unique flavors. / Wrong: Support is secondary; text emphasizes passion/work.
Explanation: The text explicitly states "combining her passion with hard work." Distractors replace "passion" with unrelated factors (marketing, feedback) or overemphasize support/flavors.



Topic: a review of a museum exhibition

Text:
The "Lost Rivers" exhibition gives history a much-needed kick by showcasing a daring 1920s expedition into the Amazon. While today’s fashion changes with each season, the tools and maps on display reveal timeless engineering skills. The team’s goal was to escape the jungle’s dangers while documenting ancient ruins. Many artifacts, including a priest’s robe imported from a distant temple, were beautifully preserved.

A life-sized statue of a priest left visitors with a strong impression, though some might ignore the intricate details of the team’s engineering feats. The exhibition’s curators arranged the displays carefully: maps hang above the artifacts, while interactive screens explain the expedition’s challenges. It’s a fascinating blend of adventure and science, proving that history isn’t just about the past—it’s about the stories we choose to remember.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the author mention modern fashion in the review?
Correct: To contrast the permanence of ancient tools with today’s fast-changing trends.
Distractors:
1. "To criticize people who wear outdated clothes."
2. "To explain how the expedition team dressed."
3. "To show that engineering was more fashionable in the past."
Explanation:
- Correct aligns with the text’s comparison of timeless engineering vs. fleeting fashion.
- Distractor 1 misinterprets the contrast as a personal judgment.
- Distractor 2 ignores the focus on tools/maps.
- Distractor 3 reverses the text’s meaning (fashion changes, engineering doesn’t).
---
Q2: What was the main challenge the expedition team faced?
Correct: Navigating and surviving the dangers of the Amazon jungle.
Distractors:
1. "Finding the priest’s temple."
2. "Avoiding modern poachers."
3. "Transporting the artifacts back safely."
Explanation:
- Correct matches the text’s emphasis on "escape the jungle’s dangers."
- Distractor 1 confuses the goal (documenting ruins) with the challenge.
- Distractor 2 introduces irrelevant modern issues.
- Distractor 3 shifts focus to logistics, not the expedition’s journey.
---
Q3: How did the curators arrange the maps in the exhibition?
Correct: They hung the maps above the artifacts.
Distractors:
1. "They placed the maps on tables near the artifacts."
2. "The maps were rolled up in glass cases."
3. "Visitors could take the maps home as souvenirs."
Explanation:
- Correct directly quotes the text’s description.
- Distractor 1 misreads "hang" as "place on tables."
- Distractor 2 adds unnecessary details.
- Distractor 3 invents a policy not mentioned.
---
Q4: What does the priest’s statue symbolize according to the text?
Correct: The religious and cultural importance of the ancient civilization.
Distractors:
1. "The team’s failure to protect the statue."
2. "A warning about the dangers of religious artifacts."
3. "The fashion trends of ancient priests."
Explanation:
- Correct reflects the text’s praise for the statue’s preservation and cultural role.
- Distractor 1 misinterprets "preserved" as neglected.
- Distractor 2 adds a negative tone not present.
- Distractor 3 confuses the priest’s role with modern fashion.



Topic: an adventure story about a person who survived on an uninhabited island

Text:
After a shipwreck, Alex found himself on a tiny, uninhabited island in the eastern Pacific. Unlike the poverty of his childhood, this place offered a strange kind of freedom—no taxes, no property laws, just survival. At first, he struggled, but he quickly learned effective ways to collect rainwater and build shelter. The island’s isolation had a similarity to stories he’d read, but nothing prepared him for the reality. When his hunting skills failed, he had to switch to fishing, which became his main source of food. Surprisingly, he even participated in a loose ‘community’ with other castaways, sharing tools and knowledge. Compared to the standard life he knew, this existence was both harsh and liberating.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Alex initially struggle on the island despite its "freedom"?
Correct: He lacked experience with the island’s harsh conditions and resources.
Distractors:
1. He refused to participate in the castaway community.
2. The eastern Pacific had strict property laws.
3. He was too lazy to adapt his hunting skills.
Explanation: The correct answer aligns with the text’s contrast between freedom and harsh reality. Distractors 1 and 2 contradict explicit details; distractor 3 misinterprets his failure as laziness.
---
Q2: What does the phrase "no taxes, no property laws" suggest about the island’s society?
Correct: It highlights the absence of organized government or economic systems.
Distractors:
1. The islanders lived in extreme poverty.
2. Taxes were replaced by a barter system.
3. Property was shared equally among all survivors.
Explanation: The correct answer captures the text’s implication of anarchy. Distractors 1 and 3 misread "no property" as poverty/equality; distractor 2 invents a system not mentioned.
---
Q3: How did Alex’s survival strategy change after his hunting failed?
Correct: He switched to fishing as his primary food source.
Distractors:
1. He relied on rainwater collection exclusively.
2. He participated in a fishing cooperative with other castaways.
3. He gave up eating meat entirely.
Explanation: The correct answer matches the text’s explicit action (switch to fishing). Distractors 1 and 3 overgeneralize; distractor 2 misrepresents community dynamics.
---
Q4: What similarity did Alex notice between his island experience and other stories?
Correct: The island’s isolation mirrored survival stories he had read.
Distractors:
1. The island’s climate was similar to other uninhabited locations.
2. Other stories also featured castaways who refused to participate in communities.
3. The lack of taxes was a common theme in adventure literature.
Explanation: The correct answer reflects the text’s direct comparison (similarity to stories). Distractors 1 and 3 introduce irrelevant details; distractor 2 contradicts the text’s positive portrayal of community.



Topic: an article about a person who collects unusual things

Text:
The Man Who Collects the Unseen
Meet Elias Carter, a collector of musical oddities—broken instruments, sheet music with unfairly low values, and even a 19th-century music bank teller’s whistle. He rarely buys items; most arrive as donations or through trades. "Upon examining something," he says, "I often find hidden stories." For example, once he nearly purchased a rare violin upon seeing its beauty—until he spotted a clause in the contract stating it had been stolen decades ago.

Elias documents every piece in a leather-bound journal, noting provenance and quirks. A local historian does him a favour by verifying authenticity, though he insists on storing items properly in climate-controlled rooms. His collection is so vast that he divides duplicates between museums and private buyers. "It’s not about owning," he explains. "It’s about preserving what others might overlook."

Though some call his hobby impractical, Elias argues that "true value isn’t in price, but in the tales objects carry." His latest obsession? A collection of musical advertisements from the 1920s—rarely seen, and rarely appreciated.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Elias almost buy the stolen violin?
Correct: He was immediately drawn to its appearance and didn’t check the contract carefully at first.
Distractors:
1. He trusted the seller’s reputation without verifying the item’s history.
2. The violin was priced much lower than similar items at auctions.
3. He recognized the violin’s previous owner from a photo in his journal.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text says he acted "upon seeing its beauty" before noticing the clause.
- Distractor 1: Wrong because the text doesn’t mention trusting the seller’s reputation.
- Distractor 2: Wrong; price isn’t the focus for the violin (though unfair pricing is mentioned elsewhere).
- Distractor 3: Wrong; no photo or personal recognition is described.
---
Q2: How does Elias decide which items to keep and which to share?
Correct: He keeps items with unique historical value and shares duplicates with institutions or collectors.
Distractors:
1. He keeps only items that are still functional as musical tools.
2. He uses a lottery system to randomly decide which items to donate.
3. He lets curators from museums select items for their collections.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text states he divides duplicates and keeps "what others might overlook" (rare pieces).
- Distractor 1: Wrong; he collects broken instruments and non-functional items.
- Distractor 2: Wrong; no lottery system is mentioned.
- Distractor 3: Wrong; the text says he decides to divide items, not that museums choose.
---
Q3: What does Elias believe is the true value of his collection?
Correct: He thinks the most important aspect is the historical stories objects represent.
Distractors:
1. He believes items are valuable only if they can be sold for high prices at auctions.
2. He prioritizes items based on how well-preserved they are physically.
3. He cares more about the number of items he owns than their individual histories.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text quotes him: "true value isn’t in price, but in the tales objects carry."
- Distractor 1: Wrong; he criticizes unfair auction pricing.
- Distractor 2: Wrong; he stores items properly but emphasizes stories, not condition.
- Distractor 3: Wrong; he divides items and focuses on uniqueness, not quantity.
---
Q4: What is one example of an unusual item Elias collects?
Correct: A 19th-century music bank teller’s whistle or vintage musical advertisements from the 1920s.
Distractors:
1. A set of ancient Greek musical scrolls.
2. A collection of modern electric guitars from the 1980s.
3. Rare first-edition sheet music by famous composers.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text lists the whistle and 1920s ads as examples of his unusual focus.
- Distractor 1: Wrong; no Greek scrolls are mentioned.
- Distractor 2: Wrong; modern guitars aren’t described as part of his collection.
- Distractor 3: Wrong; he collects musical items (instruments, ads), not just sheet music.



Topic: a story about how I proposed a creative idea at work

Text:
At work, we whenever had brainstorming sessions in our central studio, but my idea stood out. It was a luxury experience for clients—a diamond-themed event at the mall. Some colleagues doubted it at first, saying it was too extravagant, but I still believed in it. The day of the presentation arrived, and everything went perfectly. The client loved the pleasant atmosphere, and the team was amazed by how well it worked. My proposal passed without hesitation, and now it’s our company’s new signature event!

Questions:
Q1: Why did the author’s colleagues initially react negatively to the idea?
Correct: They thought it was too extravagant.
Distractors:
1. They disliked the mall location.
2. The idea was too simple.
3. They preferred a budget-friendly approach.
Explanation: The text explicitly states colleagues doubted the idea because it was "too extravagant." Distractors introduce unrelated concerns (location, simplicity, budget) that aren’t mentioned.
---
Q2: What was the client’s reaction to the event?
Correct: The client loved the pleasant atmosphere.
Distractors:
1. The client was confused by the diamond theme.
2. The client requested changes to the design.
3. The client canceled the event.
Explanation: The text clearly says the client "loved" the atmosphere. Distractors assume negative or uncertain reactions (confusion, changes, cancellation) that contradict the positive outcome.
---
Q3: How did the team respond after the presentation?
Correct: They were amazed by how well the idea worked.
Distractors:
1. They criticized the author’s lack of preparation.
2. They ignored the author’s contribution.
3. They suggested a cheaper alternative.
Explanation: The text describes the team as "amazed." Distractors imply criticism, indifference, or cost concerns, which are unsupported by the text.
---
Q4: What does the word "diamond" symbolize in the text?
Correct: It represents the brilliance or high-quality nature of the idea.
Distractors:
1. It refers to actual diamonds used in decorations.
2. It was the name of the client’s company.
3. It symbolized the event’s budget limit.
Explanation: The text uses "diamond" metaphorically to describe a "luxury experience." Distractors misinterpret it as literal (decorations), a company name, or a budget constraint, none of which are supported.



Topic: a story about my dream house

Text:
Since childhood, I’ve dreamed of a house that feels like a hug. I want a sort of cottage with a cut straight from a fairy tale—white walls, a shiny wooden porch, and a lipstick-red door to match my love of bold colors. It must be a place where my relations can gather for holidays, where every corner reflects my identity. My parents went mad when I first described it, but I convinced them by showing sketches. The project is due to start next year, and age doesn’t matter; this home will last forever. Some say it’s too extravagant, but I don’t care. It’s not just a house; it’s my story.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the author say their dream house "reflects my identity"?
Correct: Because it incorporates their personal tastes and values, making it uniquely theirs.
Distractors:
1. Because it’s old and has historical value.
2. Because it’s made of expensive materials.
3. Because it’s the same as their parents’ house.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches "every corner reflects my identity" (personalization).
- 1: "Age doesn’t matter" contradicts historical value focus.
- 2: No mention of cost; "shiny floors" could be affordable.
- 3: Text emphasizes uniqueness ("my story").
---
Q2: How did the author persuade their parents to support the dream house?
Correct: By showing them detailed sketches of the design.
Distractors:
1. By promising to build it quickly.
2. By arguing that it’s practical.
3. By showing them a model house.
Explanation:
- Correct: "I convinced them by showing sketches."
- 1: "Due next year" = timeline, not speed promise.
- 2: Parents were "mad"—implied impracticality, not logic.
- 3: "Sketches" ≠ physical model.
---
Q3: What two specific design features does the author mention for their dream house?
Correct: A shiny wooden porch and a lipstick-red door.
Distractors:
1. A garden and a swimming pool.
2. White walls and a black roof.
3. A library and a fireplace.
Explanation:
- Correct: Explicitly stated ("shiny wooden porch... lipstick-red door").
- 1: "Fairy tale" doesn’t specify outdoor features.
- 2: Only "white walls" mentioned; roof color omitted.
- 3: No interior details provided.
---
Q4: What does the phrase "the project is due next year" suggest about the author’s plans?
Correct: They plan to begin construction or planning next year.
Distractors:
1. The author is worried about delays.
2. The house is already half-built.

Topic: an article about one of the most unusual cities in the world

Text:
Timbuctu, the legendary "city of gold," lies in the Sahara Desert, where the warm sun beats down on sand dunes. Its complex history as a trade crossroads dates back centuries, but today, it’s also a symbol of resilience. Founded in the 11th century, Timbuctu indeed lived up to its fame—scholars from across Africa and the Middle East gathered here, and caravans carried salt, gold, and books for decades. However, by the 20th century, war and neglect threatened its treasures.

In recent years, efforts to update Timbuctu have begun. UNESCO declared its old town a World Heritage Site, and tourists now visit to see the rows of mud-brick houses and the hand-written manuscripts, some over 500 years old. Scientists have confirmed that these texts contain knowledge lost elsewhere. Yet, preserving them is no easy treatment—sandstorms and poverty still endanger the city.

Timbuctu remains unusual: a place where ancient traditions meet modern challenges. If its heritage is carefully managed, it could inspire the world for another millennium.

Governments must act quickly to protect fragile ecosystems before irreversible damage occurs. While some areas become uninhabitable, others, like deserts, remain silent witnesses to humanity’s struggle against extreme weather.

This basis of respect contrasts with Denmark’s tradition of smashing plates on doors to afford good luck in the new year.

Scotland’s First-Footing is slow but meaningful: the first guest brings coal, bread, or whisky. Brazil takes a symbolic approach, burying a small box with written wishes or even loneliness to leave behind. These customs range from noisy to quiet, but all share deep cultural roots.

Topic: an article about the principles of healthy eating

Text:
Many people believe that healthy eating is about strict diets or unpleasant restrictions. The truth is far simpler: a balanced diet leads to long-term well-being. For example, the lie that skipping meals helps you lose weight faster has been debunked by countless experiments. One study found that processed foods, often marketed as convenient, actually increase fatigue and weaken immunity.

In some cultures, fresh, whole foods take center place in daily life, while in others, quick fixes like cotton candy diets promise results without effort. These versions of nutrition advice are empty promises. The horror of realizing your favorite snack contains as much sugar as three apples might shock you—but it’s a wake-up call. Sugar might not murder you overnight, but it slowly erodes health over time.

The key to successfully eating well is to focus on truth, not trends. Replace processed foods with whole grains, lean proteins, and vegetables. Your body will thank you.

Workers rarely receipt their injuries immediately, meaning long-term health problems often go unnoticed. Following safety protocols reduces risks, but fatigue and poor training still cause many accidents. Despite advances in technology, the unpredictability of these jobs ensures that hazards remain a daily concern.

Teams often gather in winter for competitions, where speed and teamwork are key. Experts recommend starting on calm lakes to practice steering. To summarize, ice sailing blends adventure, skill, and the beauty of winter landscapes. Whether you’re a thrill-seeker or a curious sailor, this sport promises an unforgettable experience.

Topic: a story about the most unusual parks and gardens in the world

One of the world’s most unusual parks is the Bride’s Garden in Prague, where a tragic love story unfolded. The park’s winding paths resemble rows of old files, and its remote setting adds to its mystery.

Topic: an adventure story about a round-the-world journey

Text:

In a remote village, locals protested when they tried to trade, and one man even shot at them. To avoid conflict, Elias exchanged spices for colorful cloth and listened to the villagers’ stories. He realized they needed to prove their peaceful intentions to gain the villagers’ respect.

Later, they encountered fishermen who shared plenty of fresh food. Elias wrote about these adventures in his account, noting how cultural exchange taught him humility. By the end of the trip, he had learned that risk and respect were key to survival. His journey proved that even in danger, kindness could open doors.

- Distractor 1: Incorrect because the cloth was colorful (aesthetic), not for repairs.
- Distractor 2: Incorrect; villagers protested, not demanded payment.
- Distractor 3: Incorrect; Elias’s goal was peace, not impressing.

Distractors:
1. Respect was only important when trading goods.
2. Elias proved respect by sailing through storms bravely.

- Correct: Matches the text’s link between respect and positive outcomes (kindness opened doors).
- Distractor 1: Incorrect; respect helped with fishermen (no trade) and avoided violence.
- Distractor 2: Incorrect; storms were about risk, not respect.
- Distractor 3: Incorrect; the text ends with kindness, not distrust.



Topic: a story about a time I bought something valuable without realizing it

Text:
"I’ve always loved photography, so when I heard about a local volunteer fair for amateur photographers, I decided to go. While browsing the stalls, an honest gentleman waved at me and handed me a small, dusty cover of an old book. ‘This might interest you,’ he said. The design felt familiar, but I couldn’t place it. My attention was divided between the fair and the cover, so I only glanced at it before putting it in my bag. Later, I showed it to a retired coach who collects antiques. ‘This is a first edition!’ he exclaimed. ‘You’ve got a rare book cover worth hundreds!’ I realized it was better than I thought—all because I’d bought it without even noticing!"

Topic: an article about simple ways to reduce stress in daily life

Long-term habits matter too. Eating whole grains and nuts provides steady energy, while creating a peaceful setting—like dim lighting or soft music—helps your brain unwind. Switching to a relaxing channel on TV or listening to nature sounds can also face the problem head-on by shifting your focus. The key is consistency: small, daily changes reduce stress more than occasional drastic measures.

Topic: an article about the advantages and disadvantages of online shopping

Text:
Online shopping has become equally popular as traditional shopping, but it has both benefits and drawbacks. Many people are amazed by how convenient it is—no need to wait in long queues at the till or search for items on crowded shelves. However, some customers get upset when orders arrive late or products don’t match descriptions. Retailers often intend to improve service, but delays can still happen.

Another issue is that online shopping can be annoying when websites crash or following complicated return policies. On the other hand, businesses have successfully reduced costs by cutting staff numbers, passing savings to customers. While online shopping offers speed and variety, balancing its pros and cons is key for both shoppers and stores.

(Word count: 148)

- Correct: The text states "customers get upset when orders arrive late or products don’t match descriptions."
- Distractor 1: Wrong—delivery fees aren’t mentioned.
- Distractor 2: Wrong—website usability isn’t discussed.
- Distractor 3: Wrong—"shelves" refers to physical stores, not online inventory.

A weak reader might pick Distractor 1 if they assume "upset" implies hidden costs, or Distractor 3 if they confuse online/offline shopping.

Distractors:
1. They now offer 24/7 customer service.

Explanation:
- Correct: The text says "businesses have successfully reduced costs by cutting staff numbers."
- Distractor 1: Wrong—customer service hours aren’t mentioned.
- Distractor 2: Wrong—no mention of digital-only sales.
- Distractor 3: Wrong—free shipping isn’t discussed.

A weak reader might choose Distractor 1 if they associate "service" with customer support, or Distractor 3 if they assume "savings" = free perks.

Q3: Why might following an online store’s return policy be frustrating?

Distractors:

A weak reader might pick Distractor 1 if they infer delays from "annoying," or Distractor 3 if they assume policies include fees.
---
Q4: What does the author suggest about balancing online shopping’s advantages and disadvantages?
Correct: Both shoppers and stores need to consider the trade-offs carefully.
Distractors:
1. Online shopping is always better than traditional shopping.
2. Stores should eliminate all disadvantages to compete.
3. Shoppers should avoid online stores entirely.
Explanation:

Topic: an article about the growing popularity of second-hand shopping

Second-hand shopping is booming this spring. Many people are leaving behind their unpleasant old habits of buying new whatever they saw in stores. Instead, they’re choosing thrift shops, online markets, and flea markets where prices are slightly lower.

The appeal goes beyond savings. Shoppers love the thrill of finding unique items—a vintage dress or even a helicopter-themed lamp among a host of treasures. What was once considered odd is now celebrated. Ideas that once seemed far-fetched are now surfaceing in mainstream culture. So this spring, whether you’re bargain-hunting or just curious, second-hand shopping might be the trend worth trying.

Topic: a personal story about an unusual, but effective way to plan a budget

Text:
When I tried to save money using my bank account, it felt like chasing a shadow. Numbers on a screen didn’t touch me—generally, people avoid facing their finances this way. So, I decided to take my budgeting outdoors.

First, I investigated my spending by sorting through coins and bills. Seeing physical money made me realize how quickly I wasted it—like a dog chasing its tail. I started with a single coin in a jar, labeling it “Emergency Fund.” Every time I spent, I moved a coin from one jar to another. It was slow, but it worked.

Before, I was stuck in a cycle forever, but this method leading to real changes. I stopped overspending because I touched my money daily. My account balance grew, not just on paper but in my hands.

Most people think this is silly, but generally, what works is what sticks. My unusual system broke the old habits.

Outdoors, life adapts dramatically. In polar day, viewers—whether tourists or scientists—marvel at the endless light, but the cold bites harder. Survival requires practical solutions, like insulated clothing. Conversely, polar nights demand secondary light sources, such as lanterns. Scientists profit from studying these extremes, uncovering clues about climate change.

Judging the diagram’s accuracy helps visualize the tilt’s impact. While polar conditions seem harsh, they offer unique opportunities for research and adventure.

Studies in literature show a similarity in migration timing among species, but urban lights create a disadvantage for nocturnal migrants. Conservationists run campaigns to protect migration paths, though farmers often annoy geese landing in their fields. Surprisingly, even small birds like hummingbirds migrate alone, while larger species travel in groups. This highlights how migration is both a survival strategy and a responsible act of adaptation.

Topic: an article about ancient navigation methods

Text:
Before modern technology, sailors relied on ancient navigation methods to cross oceans. They volunteered crew members to act as lookouts, scanning the horizon for landmarks. Skilled navigators, often hired by wealthy traders, studied the stars to locate their position. If the sun’s rise was obscured by clouds, sailors might injure themselves trying to adjust sails in rough seas. Captains who failed to reach their destination were sometimes considered guilty of poor planning and could face arrest.

To navigate, sailors weighed the height of stars against known patterns. They were aware of tides and currents, which could drastically change their course. Knowledge of these techniques was carefully exchanged between cultures—Polynesian wayfinders, for example, shared methods with European explorers. Though risky, these methods proved remarkably accurate, laying the foundation for modern navigation.

Topic: an article about how the continents formed

Text:
How Continents Formed: Earth’s Ancient "Script"
The continents we see today were not always located where they are. Millions of years ago, the Earth’s crust was like a giant puzzle, constantly shifting due to tectonic forces. Scientists treat these movements as a slow dance, where plates collide, separate, or scrape past each other, leaving behind mountains, valleys, and even entire oceans.

Evidence of this drama comes from remote regions, where ancient rocks and fossils tell the story. For example, a single leaf from a prehistoric plant, preserved in sedimentary layers, can reveal the climate of a long-lost forest. Over time, wind and water turned organic matter into dust, which later formed new land. These layers create a kind of impression—like a fingerprint of Earth’s past.

A photographer documenting these sites might capture the beauty of canyons carved by rivers, but the real magic lies in the hidden script written in the rocks. Unfortunately, some people treat this history as a mystery to exploit, even engaging in illegal activities like smuggling fossils. Therefore, protecting these sites is crucial to understanding our planet’s evolution.

Questions:

Distractors:
1. Wind directly creates mountains by pushing tectonic plates.
2. Wind prevents fossils from forming by scattering them.
3. Wind is irrelevant to land formation; only water matters.
Explanation:
- The text states wind turns organic matter into dust (not moves plates or destroys fossils). Distractors 1 and 2 misrepresent wind’s role, while 3 ignores the explicit mention of wind in the process.
---
Q2: Why does the author mention a "script" when describing Earth’s history?

Distractors:

2. The "script" is a metaphor for illegal fossil trading.
3. The "script" explains how continents move like actors in a play.

- The text uses "script" to describe natural records, not human writing (1), crime (2), or movement (3). Distractors confuse metaphorical vs. literal meanings.

Explanation:

Topic: an adventure story about finding a way out of a maze

Text:
Lena, a photographer, had always dreamed of exploring ancient ruins. When she heard rumors of a highly dangerous maze hidden in the mountains, she was absolutely determined to enter. The guard at the entrance warned her not to go in—"No one returns from there!"—but Lena ignored him.

Inside, the maze was warm, lit by a strange golden glow. She had to balance carefully on narrow beams, her heart pounding. Suddenly, she spotted a costume half-buried in the dirt: a wing from some mythical bird. "This must be a clue," she thought. Then she saw a note pinned to a wall: "Bury the key under the third stone." Digging carefully, she found a rusted key.

With the key in hand, Lena followed a path that led to a door. She pushed it open and stepped into the sunlight, glad to be free. As she turned to leave, the guard appeared, smiling. "You passed the test," he said. Lena realized the maze wasn’t a trap—it was a trial.

Correct: She was absolutely determined to explore the maze.
Distractors:
1. She didn’t believe the guard was serious. / Wrong: The text says she ignored his warning despite its seriousness.
2. Her photographer’s instincts told her the maze was safe. / Wrong: The maze is described as highly dangerous.
3. She had a map that proved the maze was safe. / Wrong: No map is mentioned.

Explanation:
The correct answer matches Lena’s determination ("absolutely determined"). Distractor 1 misinterprets the guard’s tone; distractors 2–3 assume unsupported details (safety, map).

2. A photograph of the maze’s creator. / Wrong: The note mentioned a key, not a photograph.
3. A compass that showed her the way out. / Wrong: The text specifies a key.

Explanation:
The correct answer cites the key from the note. Distractor 1 confuses the wing costume with the buried object; distractors 2–3 introduce unrelated items.
---
Q3: How did the maze’s atmosphere make Lena feel, and why?
Correct: The warm, glowing maze made her feel curious but uneasy because it was highly dangerous.
Distractors:
1. She felt safe because the maze was brightly lit. / Wrong: The maze was dangerous despite the light.
2. She was scared because it was too cold inside. / Wrong: The text says it was warm.

Correct: He was testing Lena, not trying to stop her.

3. He was lost in the maze and needed her help. / Wrong: He warned her and knew the maze.

Explanation:

Topic: an adventure story about exploring a haunted house

Text:
Last weekend, Jake and I decided to explore the abandoned house on Maple Street. Our operation was risky, but we were curious. Inside, the air smelled damp, and I could barely breathe. A net dangled from the ceiling, swaying as if someone had just climbed it. ‘Smart move,’ Jake whispered, but I wasn’t sure. We followed a narrow hallway to the second level, where a rusted metal tube ran along the wall. Suddenly, the old house groaned, and the sail of the attic door creaked open. A cold wind rushed in—definite proof the house was haunted! I almost quit, but Jake grabbed my arm. ‘We came this far,’ he said. We climbed higher, past whispering voices, and successfully reached the attic. There, we saw a shadowy figure—but before we could react, the floor collapsed beneath us. We fell into a hidden room and escaped through a back window. We’ll never go back!

Financially, things weren’t as smooth as I’d hoped. My scholarship covered most of my tuition, but I still had to pay extra tax on my student visa. Back home, heating would have been a luxury, but in my new country, it was standard in every building. I learned to budget carefully, following strict rules to avoid debt.

The most memorable emotion came during graduation. Standing on stage, I realized how much I’d grown. Following the ceremony, I visited my old laboratory—now just a memory—and smiled at the tape recordings of my first experiments. This year abroad changed me in ways I couldn’t have expected.

The tower was approximately 30 meters tall, with a spiral staircase that creaked underfoot. Naturally, I missed the noise of the city, but I adapted quickly. Each morning, I burned toast, dropping crumbs into the pan, while my father checked the latest weather reports on the crackling radio. The local authority provided supplies monthly, but we had to be careful not to waste anything. One storm snapped off a branch from the old oak nearby, and I tried to cheat the darkness by leaving lights on at night. Though lonely, it was a life filled with quiet beauty—and one I’ll never forget.

Topic: a news article about an ecological project

Text:
For years, the murky waters of the Black Creek, flowing through the heart of downtown, were a literally offensive eyesore. Local settlers, many of whom had lived by the river for decades, grew tired of the stagnant conditions. In 2022, they launched an ecological project to restore the creek, partnering with scientists to monitor water quality and remove pollutants.

A crucial component of the initiative was a public appeal for community involvement. Residents were asked to report illegal dumping, while volunteers collected trash along the banks. Initially, some criticized the project as too ambitious, but its appeal grew as visible changes emerged. By 2023, water levels became stable, and fish species long absent from the creek began to return.

The project’s success wasn’t just ecological—it was emotional. "Seeing the river literally cleaner after years of neglect makes me satisfied," said Maria Lopez, a lifelong settler. Experts agree that the initiative’s transparency, with real-time data monitored via sensors, helped build trust. However, early concerns about the project being offensive to businesses near the creek were addressed by relocating industrial waste pipes upstream.

Today, the restored creek is a symbol of hope—a disc of green space in the downtown area where families picnic and children fish. While challenges remain, the project stands as a model for urban ecological revival, proving that even the most polluted waters can be transformed with persistence and collaboration.

Topic: an adventure story about time travel

Text:
Lena had spent years designing gaming apps, but nothing prepared her for the glitch in her latest project—a time-travel feature. One click sent her to 1889, where she accidentally erased a key historical event. Panicked, she met an accountant from the future who was entirely out of place. "You’ve altered the timeline," he stammered, clutching a ledger. Lena had to overcome her shock and pick between two options: restore the event or save an extinct species that depended on her presence.

The interpretation of history was already shifting. Locals began defending their version of events, insisting the past was unchangeable. Lena realized she couldn’t fix everything. She focused on one critical moment: preventing a war that would reshape her own era. Meanwhile, the accountant argued for a proposal to reset the timeline entirely, but Lena doubted its feasibility.

In a hidden library, she found a printed journal from 2150, detailing a failed time-travel experiment. The author had warned against gaming with history. Lena took the journal as proof—her only chance to defend her choices. With the accountant’s reluctant help, she proposed a new plan: document every change to avoid future paradoxes. As the city clock struck midnight, the timeline flickered back to normal. Lena returned to her lab, wondering if she’d made the right pick—or if history would judge her interpretation harshly.

Topic: a story about a cross-cultural misunderstanding

Text:
When Japanese executive Hiroki Tanaka met his American counterpart, Mark Carter, for a business deal, neither expected trouble. Mark, eager to secure an affordable solution for his clients, proposed a simplified contract. However, Hiroki’s face darkened. "This is not how we do things," he said firmly. Mark, unaware of the cultural nuances, attempted to explain that flexibility was key in today’s globalization-driven market.

Hiroki, however, insisted on including a clause honoring their ancestors, a tradition deeply rooted in Japanese business etiquette. Mark, whose broad interpretation of professionalism did not extend to such formalities, shrugged neutrally, assuming Hiroki was being overly rigid. Unbeknownst to Mark, Hiroki read into his silence as disrespect—a misreading that could have been avoided if Mark had researched Japanese customs beforehand.

The power dynamics shifted when Hiroki backtracked, explaining that the gesture was not about stubbornness but about accommodating centuries-old values. Mark, realizing his mistake, apologized for his lack of cultural awareness. Afterwards, they agreed that while globalization connects nations, it also demands patience and respect for differing traditions.

Topic: an adventure story about an accidental invention

Text:
Dr. Elena Vasquez had spent years at the biological research facility in Santiago, overseeing a workforce of brilliant but cautious scientists. Her latest experiment was deliberate—she aimed to stabilize a protein for medical use—but when she combined it with a rare enzyme, something unique emerged: a gel-like substance that hardened into a material stronger than steel. Though the process was accidental, its properties were immediately relevant to construction.

The workforce was skeptical at first. "This isn’t what we were trained for," muttered the lead engineer. But after fully testing the material under extreme conditions, even the skeptics were stunned. It was lightweight, self-repairing, and resistant to corrosion. "We could build anything with this," Elena exclaimed. The city’s mayor, impressed, proposed using it to restore an old monument—a crumbling 19th-century bridge—into a symbol of modern innovation.

The overall impact was dramatic. Within a year, the bridge stood reborn, its biological composite structure drawing global attention. Companies rushed to replicate the process, though none succeeded. Elena’s accidental invention hadn’t just saved a monument; it had redefined an industry.

Topic: a news article about the advantages and disadvantages of social media

Social media has revolutionized communication, but its impact remains predictable in some ways—both positive and negative. One major advantage is its role in sponsoring small businesses and influencers, giving them a platform to reach global audiences. However, this success is not without obstacles. Besides financial opportunities, social media fosters connections, but it also enables offensive behavior. Cyberbullying and hate speech are often unacceptable, yet platforms struggle to enforce penalties effectively. A recent study revealed that 60% of users admitted to making a mistake online, whether due to miscommunication or impulsive posts. While some argue that strict regulations could help, others fear censorship might become the unintended consequence.

Ultimately, social media’s dual nature—connecting people while enabling harm—makes its long-term effects predictable in complexity. Balancing innovation with responsibility is the key challenge ahead.

Topic: a personal story about overcoming a significant fear

Text:
Overcoming My Fear of Public Speaking

Public speaking terrified me—until I attended a workshop at my local community center. The instructor emphasized that fear was normal, but action would make it shrink. Skeptical, I signed up for a membership in Toastmasters, a club designed to help people like me. At first, my confidence was in a drought; every time I spoke, my voice split into nervous tremors. But the structured environment was efficient. We practiced weekly, and every session gave me feedback to refine my skills.

Progress came slowly at first, but my rate of improvement accelerated after a small victory: winning a local debate championship. The experience taught me that fear wasn’t a single monster but a puzzle I could solve piece by piece. Constructive criticism from peers helped me manufacture courage. By the time I gave my first presentation at work, I no longer saw public speaking as a threat but as a skill I’d mastered through persistence.

But when military officials demanded a challenging upgrade—testing its combat capabilities—everything changed.
