Text:
"My career expansion had me working 80-hour weeks, wearing a silk scarf to meetings while my mind raced with deadlines that governed my life. Prior to burnout, I’d never questioned the relentless pace—until I witnessed a homeless man smiling at a free meal. His gratitude, unburdened by spreadsheets, made me feel like a fool for chasing empty markers of success. Basically, I realized my life had become a race with no finish line. That night, I drew a line in the sand: no more late nights. Critics called me lazy, but I’d rather be present than perfect. Now, I volunteer at shelters, where the real measure of achievement isn’t a title but the quiet joy of giving back."

Questions:
Q1: What specific event caused the narrator to question their work-life balance?
Correct: Seeing a homeless man’s gratitude for a free meal.
Distractors:
1. A colleague criticized their work ethic. (Wrong: Criticism came after the realization, not before.)
2. Their silk scarf reminded them of materialism. (Wrong: The scarf symbolized status, but the turning point was the homeless man’s reaction.)
3. They hit a personal burnout milestone. (Wrong: Burnout was the result, not the trigger.)

Explanation:
The correct answer is directly stated ("until I witnessed..."). Distractors 1 and 3 misplace causality, while 2 confuses symbolism with the event. A weak reader might pick 3 because burnout is mentioned, but the text clarifies it was after the encounter.
---
Q2: How does the narrator describe their prior attitude toward work?
Correct: They never questioned the relentless pace of their career.
Distractors:
1. They believed work was the only path to happiness. (Wrong: The text doesn’t claim this; it critiques chasing success without happiness.)
2. They resented colleagues who criticized their efforts. (Wrong: Criticism is mentioned but not as a prior attitude.)
3. They saw deadlines as a necessary evil. (Wrong: The text implies deadlines governed their life, not that they viewed them neutrally.)

Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases "never questioned the relentless pace." Distractor 1 overgeneralizes; 2 and 3 introduce unsupported ideas. A weak reader might choose 1 because it aligns with common workaholic stereotypes.
---
Q3: What does the phrase "markers of success" refer to in the text?
Correct: External achievements like titles or long hours.
Distractors:
1. Financial wealth accumulated through hard work. (Wrong: The text doesn’t mention money explicitly.)
2. Personal milestones like learning new skills. (Wrong: The narrator rejects external markers, not personal growth.)
3. The ability to govern one’s time effectively. (Wrong: This is the opposite of what they realized was missing.)

Explanation:
The correct answer reflects the contrasted values (titles vs. giving back). Distractor 3 is a misreading of "governed by deadlines" as a positive trait. A weak reader might pick 1 because "success" often equates to money.
---
Q4: Why does the narrator say they "felt like a fool"?
Correct: Because they realized they’d been chasing meaningless achievements.
Distractors:
1. They had ignored their family’s advice. (Wrong: Family isn’t mentioned.)
2. Their silk scarf was a symbol of hypocrisy. (Wrong: The scarf is a detail, not the reason for shame.)
3. They had failed to meet a critical deadline. (Wrong: Burnout was due to too many deadlines, not one failure.)

Explanation:
The correct answer ties to "empty markers of success." Distractor 2 misinterprets the scarf’s role. A weak reader might pick 3 because deadlines are emphasized, but the text links shame to systemic overwork, not a single event.
---
Q5: What is the real measure of achievement for the narrator now?
Correct: The quiet joy of giving back to others.
Distractors:
1. A balanced schedule with no overtime. (Wrong: While they drew a line, the text focuses on purpose, not hours.)
2. Proving critics wrong about their choices. (Wrong: Critics are mentioned but not the goal.)
3. Earning a promotion despite their changes. (Wrong: The text rejects titles as markers.)

Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases "the quiet joy of giving back." Distractor 1 is a partial truth but misses the emotional shift. A weak reader might pick 2 because critics are mentioned, but the text frames their change as internal, not retaliatory.



Topic: a personal story about what I learned from mentoring someone

Text:
When I agreed to mentor Jamie, I expected to teach her coding skills. What I didn’t anticipate was how awkward and controversial her decisions would become. Early on, she made a weird request to skip quality checks, calling it a "team-building exercise." I felt like a rat in a maze, unsure whether to intervene or let her resign from the project herself. The situation grew worse when her bunch of shortcuts led to a major bug, and suddenly, I was surrounded by colleagues questioning my judgment.

The breaking point came when Jamie resigned from the project, leaving me to clean up the mess. In the aftermath, I realized her actions weren’t just incompetence—they reflected a questionable ethic. She’d been taught that speed mattered more than integrity, and I’d been too passive. It was like getting lost in a bush without a compass. The experience forced me to confront my own role as a mentor. I’d assumed teaching technical skills was enough, but the real lesson was about ethics: how to guide someone through moral ambiguity, even when it’s awkward or controversial.

Now, I see mentoring as more than passing down knowledge—it’s about shaping so-called "professionals" into people who prioritize principles over shortcuts.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the narrator feel like "a rat in a maze"?
Correct: The narrator was morally uncertain about whether to challenge Jamie’s unethical shortcuts, creating a confusing dilemma.
Distractors:
1. Jamie physically trapped the narrator in a small space.
2. The narrator was lost in the office building while looking for Jamie.
3. Jamie’s coding errors made the narrator’s work impossible to debug.
Explanation:
The correct answer captures the metaphorical confusion (ethical dilemma). Distractor 1 misreads "maze" literally; 2 confuses metaphors; 3 ignores the narrator’s emotional state.
---
Q2: What controversial decision did Jamie make, and how did it affect the team?
Correct: Jamie skipped quality checks, calling it a "team-building exercise," which caused a major bug and led to criticism of the narrator’s mentoring.
Distractors:
1. Jamie resigned from the project after arguing with the team.
2. Jamie publicly criticized the narrator’s leadership style.
3. Jamie demanded a raise without contributing extra work.
Explanation:
The correct answer ties Jamie’s shortcuts to the bug and criticism. Distractor 1 misorders events; 2 introduces unrelated conflict; 3 is unsupported by the text.
---
Q3: How did the narrator’s initial assumptions about mentoring change?
Correct: The narrator realized mentoring involves guiding ethical behavior, not just technical skills.
Distractors:
1. The narrator decided mentoring was too stressful and quit.
2. The narrator learned to avoid controversial mentees in the future.
3. The narrator adopted Jamie’s "team-building" approach to speed up projects.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects the growth in the conclusion. Distractor 1 misattributes "resign" to the narrator; 2 oversimplifies the lesson; 3 misrepresents the narrator’s stance.
---
Q4: What does "lost in the bush" symbolize?
Correct: It represents the narrator’s confusion about navigating Jamie’s lack of clear ethical principles.
Distractors:
1. It describes the narrator’s difficulty finding Jamie in the office.
2. It refers to the narrator’s inability to understand technical coding errors.
3. It symbolizes the narrator’s fear of being blamed for Jamie’s mistakes.
Explanation:
The correct answer links the metaphor to ethical ambiguity. Distractor 1 misreads symbolism; 2 confuses themes; 3 partially correct but oversimplifies.
---
Q5: Why does the narrator describe Jamie’s ethic as "questionable"? Provide two reasons.
Correct:
1. Jamie prioritized speed over integrity (e.g., skipping checks).
2. She was taught that shortcuts were acceptable ("team-building exercise").
Distractors:
1. Jamie refused to work with the narrator after the bug was found.
2. Jamie had a history of lying to previous mentors.
3. The narrator personally disliked Jamie’s communication style.
Explanation:
The correct answers cite shortcuts and misleading framing. Distractor 1 introduces unrelated conflict; 2 is unsupported; 3 misattributes the critique.



Topic: a personal account of a day I spent completely disconnected from technology

Text:
"I almost hesitated when my friend suggested a day without technology. The wind rustled through my hair as I stepped outside, and for the first time in months, I felt truly present. My phone stayed in my pocket, unchecked—a rare luxury. By midday, I found myself in a quiet café, a social institution where people gathered without screens. The venue hummed with low conversation, and I watched a curious child sketching on a napkin, her focus undistracted. Later, I wandered through a hospital corridor, where a nurse smiled and said, ‘You look like you’ve escaped something.’ I had. My inner referee—usually debating notifications—was silent. As evening fell, I sat on a bench, honouring the day’s simplicity. The experience changed me significantly; I realized how much I’d been missing."

Questions:
Q1: Why did the narrator initially hesitate about the day without technology?
Correct: They were unsure about disconnecting from their phone and constant connectivity.
Distractors:
1. They feared missing urgent messages from work. / They had never tried a tech-free day before. / They worried they wouldn’t stay productive.
Explanation: The correct answer matches the text’s focus on general discomfort ("almost hesitated"). Distractors introduce unsupported reasons (work, inexperience, productivity).
---
Q2: How did the café serve as a "social institution"?
Correct: It was a neutral space where people interacted naturally without relying on technology.
Distractors:
1. It enforced a strict no-phones policy. / It was a venue for organized events like lectures. / It provided free Wi-Fi for customers.
Explanation: The correct answer aligns with the text’s description of the café as a casual gathering spot. Distractors assume rules or activities not mentioned.
---
Q3: What did the nurse’s comment imply about the narrator’s demeanor?
Correct: The nurse perceived the narrator as relaxed or liberated from stress.
Distractors:
1. The nurse thought the narrator was lost. / The nurse assumed the narrator was a patient. / The narrator seemed unusually cheerful.
Explanation: The correct answer reflects the metaphor of "escaping" something. Distractors misread the tone (stress vs. cheer) or context (lost vs. patient).
---
Q4: Why was the child’s sketching notable to the narrator?
Correct: It demonstrated how children could concentrate deeply without digital interruptions.
Distractors:
1. The child’s drawing was exceptionally detailed. / The narrator admired the child’s artistic talent. / The child was the only one not using a device.
Explanation: The correct answer focuses on focus, not quality or uniqueness. Distractors add unsupported details.
---
Q5: What role did the "inner referee" play during the day?
Correct: It symbolized the narrator’s usual habit of overthinking or checking technology, which was absent that day.
Distractors:
1. It was a voice urging the narrator to check their phone. / It was a real person giving advice. / It helped the narrator decide where to go.
Explanation: The correct answer explains the metaphorical silence of the referee. Distractors literalize it or reverse its function.



Topic: a sci-fi story about a society where human memory can be digitally stored and replayed

Text:
In the year 2147, humanity had mastered the art of storing memories digitally, allowing people to replay their pasts like holographic recordings. The launch of the Mnemosyne Project revolutionized society, though not without controversy. Critics argued that the make-up of human identity was being altered—what did it mean to be "you" if your memories could be edited or shared?

Lena, a former ballet dancer, relied on memory sessions to relive her prime performances. But when she accessed a suppressed memory—a wound from a failed audition—she realized the system wasn’t just preserving the past; it was rewriting it. The depth of her emotional turmoil forced her to question whether she was living in reality or a curated illusion.

Meanwhile, the government maintained national archives of collective memories, ensuring historical continuity. Yet, underground "memory masters" like Kael sold black-market replays, allowing clients to stand witness to lives they never lived. Some emerged with new skills; others dissolved into paranoia, unable to distinguish their own thoughts from borrowed ones.

As Lena watched a crowd at a public memory replay, she wondered: if everyone could access the past, would the future even matter?

Questions:
Q1: Why did Lena feel her identity was being altered by the memory system?
Correct: The system’s ability to edit or share memories blurred the boundaries of her authentic self.
Distractors:
1. She feared her ballet skills would degrade from overuse of replays.
2. The government erased personal memories to enforce conformity.
3. Her memories were secretly implanted by memory masters.
Explanation: The correct answer aligns with the text’s focus on "make-up of identity" and "edited or shared" memories. Distractors introduce unsupported fears (skill loss, government erasure, implants).
---
Q2: How did Kael’s black-market memory replays impact clients?
Correct: Clients either gained new identities or became confused about their own thoughts.
Distractors:
1. All clients developed permanent memory loss.
2. The replays only contained historical events, not personal lives.
3. Clients were forced to relive traumatic memories against their will.
Explanation: The correct answer reflects the text’s dual outcomes ("new skills"/"paranoia"). Distractors misrepresent the scope (universal amnesia, historical-only replays, coercion).
---
Q3: What was the significance of ballet in Lena’s memory sessions?
Correct: It represented a preserved cultural experience she used to relive her career, but also exposed the system’s flaws when she accessed a suppressed memory.
Distractors:
1. Ballet was banned as a "fake" memory type.
2. Lena used ballet replays to suppress her emotional wounds.
3. Citizens were legally required to memorize ballet as a national memory.
Explanation: The correct answer ties ballet to Lena’s personal use and the system’s ethical issues. Distractors misrepresent ballet’s role (bans, forced memorization, therapeutic use).
---
Q4: Why might public memory replays make the future seem irrelevant?
Correct: If the past could be perfectly relived, people might lose motivation to innovate or improve the future.
Distractors:
1. The replays caused a time paradox that erased future events.
2. The government used replays to manipulate citizens into submission.
3. People preferred reliving the past to experiencing the present.
Explanation: The correct answer echoes Lena’s rhetorical question about the future’s value. Distractors introduce unsupported sci-fi elements (paradoxes, brainwashing, preference).
---
Q5: What did the Mnemosyne Project achieve, and what was a major ethical concern?
Correct: It enabled digital memory storage and replay, but raised concerns about altered identities and exploitation by black-market dealers.
Distractors:
1. It cured mental illness by permanently erasing bad memories.
2. The project caused people to forget their names.
3. It allowed immortality by replaying lives indefinitely.
Explanation: The correct answer summarizes the text’s focus on revolution/ethics. Distractors oversimplify (cures, immortality) or misrepresent (name loss).



Topic: an analysis of how food delivery apps have altered the social rituals of eating

Text:
Food delivery apps have redefined the classic ritual of eating together, replacing it with a depressing trend: solo meals in front of screens. Their primary function is convenience, but at the cost of the goodness once found in shared tables. There’s a clear tendency to order instead of cook, fueled by greater exposure to global cuisines—though often with acid remarks about the quality. While apps offer variety, they also introduce hidden fees that erode savings, forcing strict budgeting even for casual diners. Some see delivery as a badge of culinary adventure, but the social erosion is undeniable. The shift reflects broader changes: technology prioritizes efficiency over tradition, and the goodness of communal dining fades into nostalgia.

Questions:
Q1: How do food delivery apps challenge the "classic" dining experience?
Correct: They replace shared meals with solo, screen-based eating.
Distractors:
1. "They make food taste worse."
2. "They encourage people to cook more."
3. "They increase restaurant profits."
Explanation:
The text contrasts classic (shared) dining with modern solo meals. Distractor 1 misinterprets acid remarks as literal taste changes; 2/3 ignore the social focus.
---
Q2: What negative social consequence of delivery apps is described as "depressing"?
Correct: The trend of eating alone in front of screens.
Distractors:
1. "The high cost of delivery fees."
2. "The loss of traditional recipes."
3. "The environmental impact of packaging."
Explanation:
Depressing refers to social isolation, not cost or recipes. Distractor 1 conflates fee critique; 2/3 are tangential.
---
Q3: Why might customers make "acid" remarks about delivery food?
Correct: Because the quality often doesn’t match expectations.
Distractors:
1. "Because the food arrives too slowly."
2. "Because delivery drivers are rude."
3. "Because the apps are too complicated."
Explanation:
Acid remarks target quality, not speed, drivers, or app design. Distractors project unrelated complaints.
---
Q4: How do hidden fees affect consumers’ eating habits?
Correct: They force consumers to budget strictly, reducing casual spending.
Distractors:
1. "They make people eat healthier."
2. "They lead to more restaurant visits."
3. "They encourage family meals."
Explanation:
Strict budgeting reduces spending; distractors misalign with erosion of shared meals.
---
Q5: What does the author imply about the "badge" of using delivery apps?
Correct: It symbolizes modernity, but at the expense of traditional social dining.
Distractors:
1. "It shows people are lazy."
2. "It proves delivery is better than cooking."
3. "It represents a return to tradition."
Explanation:
Badge is symbolic of modernity, not laziness or value judgments. Distractors overinterpret or reverse the text’s tone.



Topic: a story about a long-standing rivalry that transforms into mutual respect

Text:
Captain Elias Voss and Dr. Mira Chen had been rivals since their first mission together. The first indication of their conflict came when Chen ignored Voss’s request to reroute power, calling his conduct "reckless." By the time they reached Mars, their rivalry had become a major obstacle, with the rest of the crew walking on eggshells. The affair of the broken oxygen filter—initially blamed on Chen—only deepened the darkness between them.

Then, disaster struck. A sudden spike in radiation threatened the habitat. Voss and Chen were forced to collaborate, their hands trembling as they worked side by side. The tension in the air began to relieve as they realized the mission’s survival depended on mutual trust. Chen admitted her earlier mistake; Voss acknowledged his overconfidence. The crew, watching silently, could see the shift in their regard for each other.

By the time the radiation levels stabilized, their rivalry had softened into cautious respect. It was probable that, had they not faced this crisis, they might have failed the mission—and each other. As they reviewed the data together, Voss even joked, "Guess we’re not such an obstacle anymore." Chen smirked. The darkness of their past had given way to the light of shared purpose.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the text describe the rivalry as an "obstacle"?
Correct: Their rivalry hindered teamwork and nearly jeopardized the mission’s success.
Distractors:
1. It was a physical barrier in the spaceship. / Wrong: The text describes it as a teamwork issue, not a literal obstacle.
2. Chen and Voss were afraid to speak to each other. / Wrong: The text mentions tension but not fear of speaking; it’s about ignored requests and clashing conduct.
3. The crew disliked both of them equally. / Wrong: The crew is described as walking on eggshells, implying they’re affected by the rivalry but not necessarily disliking both.
---
Q2: What specific event forced Voss and Chen to work together?
Correct: A sudden increase in radiation levels required immediate cooperation.
Distractors:
1. The oxygen filter broke. / Wrong: The filter was part of the affair (conflict), not the crisis that forced cooperation.
2. They realized they were running out of food. / Wrong: The text specifies radiation, not food shortages.
3. Voss apologized first. / Wrong: Both admitted faults, but the trigger was the radiation, not an apology.
---
Q3: How does the word "relieve" change its meaning from the beginning to the end of the text?
Correct: Initially, it referred to easing physical tension (e.g., shaking hands), but later it symbolized resolving emotional conflict between them.
Distractors:
1. It first meant "to lift a burden," then "to make someone happy." / Wrong: "Relieve" in the text refers to tension (emotional), not happiness.
2. It described physical exhaustion at first, then mental exhaustion. / Wrong: The text links it to tension (teamwork), not exhaustion.
3. It started as a medical term, then became a metaphor. / Wrong: "Relieve" is used figuratively throughout (tension), not medically.
---
Q4: What might have happened if the radiation leak had occurred earlier in the mission?
Correct: The mission would likely have failed due to their unresolved conflict.
Distractors:
1. They would have blamed each other and abandoned the mission. / Wrong: The text implies failure, not abandonment.
2. The crew would have fixed it without their help. / Wrong: The text suggests Voss and Chen were the only ones who could resolve it.
3. The mission would have been delayed by weeks. / Wrong: The text doesn’t mention delays, only failure.
---
Q5: How does the crew’s role in the story reflect the theme of transformation?
Correct: They act as silent witnesses to the rivals’ change, showing that external observers recognize growth even when the rivals themselves don’t immediately acknowledge it.
Distractors:
1. They helped Voss and Chen resolve their conflict. / Wrong: The crew is observers, not active mediators.
2. They were too scared to interfere. / Wrong: The text doesn’t state fear; it’s about neutrality.
3. They replaced Voss and Chen with new astronauts. / Wrong: No mention of replacements; the focus is on Voss and Chen’s change.



Topic: a story about a person who moves to a new country every few years and loves it

Text:
For as long as I can remember, moving every few years hasn’t just been a routine—it’s been a revision of who I am. There’s a hidden thrill in packing up and not knowing what’s next, though my parents blame it on my restless childhood. I’ve never missed a "home" because I’ve never had one. My origin is a cultural tapestry, stitched together by languages and landscapes. By my twenties, I’d embraced the nomadic lifestyle wholeheartedly. Wherever life took me—Tokyo’s neon alleys, a village in Morocco, or a session at a Berlin café—I saw it as a phase, not a punishment. The linguistic hurdles? A challenge, not a barrier. I’d strike up conversations in broken Spanish or French, laughing at my mistakes. Even data analysts would envy my ability to adapt. If I’d stayed put, I’d never have learned that the chaos was a gift. Moving taught me resilience, curiosity, and that routine was a myth I’d outgrown.

Q1: Why does the narrator enjoy moving frequently?
Correct: They view it as a way to embrace new identities and experiences, finding joy in the unpredictability of change.
Distractors:
1. They enjoy the thrill of outsmarting others who resist change. / Wrong: The text frames moving as personal growth, not competition.
2. Their parents encouraged them to travel to escape responsibility. / Wrong: The narrator says "no one to blame," implying autonomy.
3. They believe moving is the only way to achieve true happiness. / Wrong: The text doesn’t claim moving guarantees happiness, only enrichment.

Explanation:
The correct answer captures the "revision of who I am" and "hidden thrill" without repetition. Distractor 1 misreads the text as competitive; distractor 2 contradicts the narrator’s agency; distractor 3 overgeneralizes.

Q2: What does the narrator mean by "routine was a myth"?
Correct: They argue that the idea of a stable, unchanging daily life is an illusion they’ve deliberately rejected.

3. They regret not knowing their ancestors’ stories. / Wrong: The text doesn’t mention regret or loss.

Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases "cultural tapestry" and "no single origin." Distractor 1 misinterprets "tapestry" as exclusion; distractor 2 contradicts the plural description; distractor 3 is unsupported.

Q4: What challenge does the narrator face when moving, and how do they handle it?
Correct: Language barriers, which they navigate by embracing mistakes and engaging with locals despite imperfect communication.

2. They avoid speaking new languages to protect their dignity. / Wrong: They "strike up conversations" despite errors.

Explanation:
The correct answer reflects "linguistic hurdles" and "laughing at my mistakes." Distractor 1 introduces an unsupported theme; distractor 2 contradicts their proactive approach; distractor 3 is factually incorrect.
---
Q5: What does the narrator imply about people who stay in one place?
Correct: They may miss opportunities to develop adaptability and resilience that come from frequent change.
Distractors:
1. They are likely to be more materially successful. / Wrong: The text doesn’t link stability to success.
2. They have stronger family ties. / Wrong: The text doesn’t mention family dynamics.
3. They are less open-minded than travelers. / Wrong: The text doesn’t compare mindsets directly.

Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases the conditional "If I’d stayed put, I’d never have learned..." Distractor 1 introduces an unrelated topic; distractor 2 is unsupported; distractor 3 is speculative.



Topic: a personal account of a time I had to make a split-second decision

Text:
One evening, I received an email about a high-risk investment opportunity—guaranteed returns in days. The pace of the correspondence was relentless; the sender claimed to be a verified expert. Trusting my own judgment, I nearly transferred money before pausing. Then, I noticed inconsistencies in the licence details attached. My anger flared when I realized it was a fraud—a common scam targeting distracted individuals.

In that split-second transition, I had to act. My mind raced through the zone of danger: if I hesitated, I’d lose thousands. Worse, I’d forgotten to take my medication that morning, clouding my focus. Through the lens of hindsight, the warning signs were obvious, but the shocking speed of the scam had blinded me. I canceled the transfer just in time, avoiding disaster.

The experience taught me to slow down. Scammers exploit urgency, and my initial trust was misplaced. Now, I double-check every detail—especially when my medication or stress might impair my judgment. That night, I learned the hard way: in high-stakes moments, split-second decisions can mean the difference between security and ruin.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the narrator nearly fall victim to the fraud?
Correct: The scammer’s messages arrived at an overwhelming pace, preventing careful scrutiny.
Distractors:
1. The narrator was tricked because they ignored their medication.
2. The investment was too risky, so the narrator panicked.
3. The scammer threatened legal action if the narrator didn’t comply.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches the text’s focus on pace and urgency.
- Distractor 1: Incorrect cause (medication clouded focus, didn’t cause the fraud).
- Distractor 2: "Panicked" isn’t mentioned; risk was secondary to speed.
- Distractor 3: No threats are described.
---
Q2: How did the narrator’s medication affect their decision-making?
Correct: It impaired their focus, making them more susceptible to the scam’s urgency.
Distractors:
1. The medication made them more alert to the scam.
2. They forgot to take it because they were too busy investing.
3. The medication caused them to act angrily toward the scammer.
Explanation:
- Correct: Directly paraphrases "clouding my focus."
- Distractor 1: Opposite meaning ("impaired" vs. "alert").
- Distractor 2: Mislinks medication to investing (text doesn’t specify).
- Distractor 3: Anger stemmed from realizing the fraud, not medication.
---
Q3: What specific detail in the email raised suspicions about the investment?
Correct: The inconsistencies in the attached licence details.
Distractors:
1. The email used unprofessional language.
2. The sender claimed to be a government official.
3. The investment promised returns faster than legally possible.
Explanation:
- Correct: Explicit in the text ("inconsistencies in the licence details").
- Distractor 1: Not mentioned; text focuses on licence, not language.
- Distractor 2: No government claim is made.
- Distractor 3: "Guaranteed returns" is mentioned, but licence is the red flag.
---
Q4: What might have happened if the narrator had hesitated longer?
Correct: They would likely have lost thousands of dollars and faced financial ruin.
Distractors:
1. They would have lost their driving licence.
2. The scammer would have contacted their bank directly.
3. They would have realized it was a scam in time.
Explanation:
- Correct: Paraphrases "lose thousands" and "disaster."
- Distractor 1: Licence was part of the scam, not a consequence.
- Distractor 2: No mention of direct bank contact.
- Distractor 3: Contradicts the text’s urgency ("split-second").
---
Q5: How does the narrator now approach high-pressure situations differently?
Correct: They verify details carefully, especially when distracted by stress or medication.
Distractors:
1. They avoid all investments to prevent stress.
2. They take their medication immediately before decisions.
3. They rely on others to verify details for them.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches "double-check every detail" and "when medication or stress impair judgment."
- Distractor 1: Overgeneralizes (text doesn’t say they avoid investments entirely).
- Distractor 2: Misinterprets "forgot" as a new habit.
- Distractor 3: Text specifies self-verification, not delegation.



Topic: a story about an experience that completely changed my perspective on life

Text:
Before that fateful day, my life had been predictable—a routine I followed without question. I was content, partly because I believed I understood the world. But my assumptions were built on speculation, not truth. Then, during a trip to a remote village, I witnessed an elderly man document his life story in a handwritten journal. His words were raw, unfiltered, and bound by no one’s expectations. That moment shattered my complacency.

At first, I dismissed it as coincidence, but over time, I realized my old views were only partly correct. The man’s story gave me an incentive to question my own path. What had once seemed like a boost of motivation turned into a deeper calling. I extended my stay in the village, no longer a mere spectator but an active participant in his community.

The experience was more profound than I’d imagined. I elected to quit my stable job and travel, though many called me reckless. Their speculation about my choices didn’t matter—I was bound by the lesson I’d learned: life isn’t about following scripts but embracing the unpredictable.

Q1: Why did the narrator initially dismiss the elderly man’s story as coincidence?

3. They believed the story was fictional. / Wrong: The journal was documented (real), and the narrator’s issue was with their own assumptions.

Topic: a story about a time I failed spectacularly and what I learned

Text:
One summer, I entered a national essay competition hosted in a housing project near my town. Overconfident, I skipped the extensive rules review and misread a question—my mistake cost me the chance to win. The confusion was immediate; I froze mid-exam, my gesture of surrender (a dramatic sigh) only making things worse. As I left, the tune of my embarrassment played in my head for weeks.

Later, I analyzed my error plus the discount I’d given my own preparation. The failure wasn’t just about the competition; it was a classic example of assuming I knew more than I did. To recover, I conducted extensive research on competitive writing, even joining a local club. The experience taught me that pride blinds us to details—like that national exam question I’d overlooked.

Now, I laugh about it. The discount I got? On my ego.

Topic: a story about reconnecting with someone after many years of silence

Text:
The publication of a regional newspaper article about the restoration of Lord Whitmore’s estate was an invasion into my past. Twenty years had passed since I last saw him, yet the memory of our childhood—our grade-school pranks, the bargain we made to share secrets—lingered like an unsolved mystery. I had always assumed our silence was mutual, but the article suggested otherwise: Lord Whitmore, now a relic of his own history, had been searching for me too.

Our reunion was risky. He lived in a world of regional traditions, where time moved slower, while I had built a life in the city, prioritizing efficiency over nostalgia. At first, we struggled to incorporate the past into our conversations, like two ships passing in the night. Yet, the bargain we struck—meeting once a month to share old photographs—seemed to satisfy us both. The distribution of our memories, however, was uneven. His estate was a museum of the past, while my apartment held only modern comforts.

As we walked through his gardens, I realized the reunion wasn’t about closing old wounds but acknowledging their existence. The lord of my past and the man before me were two different people, yet the connection remained. Perhaps that was the point: some things, like a well-negotiated bargain, are worth preserving, even if they can’t be fully satisfied.

Topic: a story about a time I stood up for something I believed in despite opposition

Text:
Standing Up for What Matters

There was this time I had to defend a belief that felt like a punk rock ethos in a world obsessed with tradition. My team wanted to scrap a project because "it was too risky," but I saw its potential. They dismissed my ideas with all that stuff about "playing it safe," and honestly, I hesitated. The investment in time and resources seemed overwhelming, and my expertise was questioned—"Who are you to challenge the way we’ve always done things?"

Although I knew it might backfire, I decided to defend my position. I gathered additional evidence, highlighting how similar projects had succeeded elsewhere. When they argued it was my fault for pushing too hard, I reminded them that the bigger issue was fairness—not just to the team, but to the project’s potential to inspire innovation. Taking the first step was terrifying, but once I spoke up, others started questioning their own assumptions.

In the end, the project launched successfully, and my initial doubts faded. It wasn’t just about winning the argument; it was about proving that standing up for what you believe in—even when it’s unpopular—can have ripple effects. The experience taught me that principles are worth the investment, even if others see only fault.

Topic: a personal account of a time I witnessed an act of injustice and my reaction

Text:
Last summer, I stood on the deck of a ferry watching the sunset when I witnessed something that left me in shock. A street vendor, taking advantage of tourists, charged an elderly woman triple the price for a scarf. Around me, speculation spread—some whispered he was in debt, others assumed he was greedy. The crowd grew loose with murmurs, but no one intervened.

It was clearly the wrong thing to do, yet no one spoke up. I hesitated, but I couldn’t just leave it. Stepping forward, I asked the vendor to lower the price. At first, he refused, but the unity of the crowd—now watching—forced him to comply. The woman left with her scarf, and the tension dissolved.

In this phase, I realized injustice thrives in silence. We needed a sustainable change, not just a temporary fix. The next day, I reported the vendor to the local authorities. Though small, this act reminded me that standing against wrong requires more than shock—it demands action.

Questions:

3. They believed the vendor’s excuse about being in debt was valid. / Wrong: The crowd was passive due to hesitation, not belief.

Distractors:
1. The vendor called the police, escalating the conflict. / Wrong: The vendor complied, not escalated.

Distractors:

2. Silence is necessary to protect the innocent. / Wrong: Silence enables injustice.
3. Only authorities can stop injustice. / Wrong: Author took personal action first.

Distractors:

3. It made the author feel less guilty about confronting the vendor. / Wrong: Focus was on collective action, not guilt.

Topic: a story about the day I decided to quit something that was no longer serving me

The guilt hurt, but the truth was clearer. I could no longer justify the time or emotional energy. Moreover, the hours I spent exceeded what I could realistically sustain without burning out.

The decision to quit wasn’t easy. I feared judgment, but the relief was instant. Within weeks, I replaced the project with something aligned with my values. Looking back, the offence I’d felt was a red flag—I’d been clinging to something that no longer challenged or inspired me. Now, I understand that quitting isn’t failure; it’s critical self-preservation.

Topic: a story about learning to appreciate a form of art I previously dismissed

My parents, wealthy art collectors, always proposed visiting galleries, but I’d roll my eyes at their "pretentious" tastes. That changed one evening when a leaflet for a local mural festival landed on my doorstep. Curious, I attended, expecting chaos, but instead, I saw conscious effort behind every brushstroke.

The artist, a former protester, explained how his work addressed pollution, including damage to human lungs. He described how nowadays, street art is widely recognized in museums, even if its delivery is often controversial. I realized my dismissal was ignorant. The next day, I bought one of his pieces—not as a trend, but as a reminder of how art can challenge perceptions.

Topic: a story about a time I judged someone prematurely and was proven completely wrong

Text:
Last year, I judged a colleague prematurely, and the experience taught me a lesson I’ll never forget. At an elementary level, I assumed his ideas were naive because he spoke softly and his presentation pitch lacked confidence. His quiet presence made me underestimate him, and I was entirely dependent on my first impressions. During a team meeting, I gave him feedback that was more critical than constructive, focusing on what I saw as flaws rather than potential.

Later, I realized our views differed far more than I’d expected. His project, though initially dismissed, turned out to be revolutionary in its approach. The principle I’d violated—that first impressions are often misleading—became clear when I saw the respect he earned from others. His honour was never in question; it was my hasty judgment that had failed him.

Hopefully, this experience will prevent me from repeating the mistake. I learned that presence isn’t always about volume, and confidence isn’t always about pitch. The revolution in my thinking started with admitting I’d been wrong.

Q1: Why did the narrator initially dismiss the colleague’s ideas?
Correct: The narrator assumed his ideas were unsophisticated due to his soft-spoken tone and unconfident delivery.

3. The narrator was jealous of the colleague’s popularity.

The turning point was when I forced myself to adopt a neutral mindset. Instead of debating every angle, I focused on execution. Even now, I catch myself overthinking, but I remind myself: the worst enemy isn’t failure—it’s hesitation.

Text:

At the border with the old world, a troop of rebels opposed the Truth Laws, hiding in the ruins of a bar where patrons dared not joke. Inside, Elias sipped a litre of water, his fingers trembling. Across the table, Mara’s smile didn’t reach her eyes. He could infer she was hiding something—her pupils dilated, a telltale sign. Truth-seekers, experts who specialized in detecting micro-expressions, patrolled the streets, their badges gleaming.

Elias’s hand clenched. "You’re lying," he accused. Mara’s skin mottled pink. "I—I was going to tell you," she stammered. The bar fell silent. The cost of old habits was clear: in this world, even the smallest fib could destroy you.

Topic: an essay on the psychological impact of doomscrolling and the need for a new digital literacy

Text:
The psychological impact of doomscrolling—endlessly consuming purely negative news—is evident in rising anxiety and depression. Algorithms exploit our brains like an aircraft’s autopilot, feeding us content that spoils our mental well-being. The feedback loops of likes and shares reinforce this cycle, making it harder to break free. While some argue that doomscrolling is harmless, research shows it spoils our ability to focus, leaving us emotionally drained.

A sustainable solution requires more than individual willpower. Schools and workplaces must teach adequate digital literacy, helping people recognize harmful patterns. Those struggling should consult a therapist to develop healthier habits. Yet, opponents of this change—often tech companies—resist regulation, prioritizing engagement over well-being. The irony? We’re not just passive consumers; we’re complicit in our own suffering, scrolling light as a feather but heavy in consequence.

Topic: an analysis of the psychological principles behind effective user interface design

Text:
User interfaces (UIs) function like a cognitive organ—a system that processes information efficiently. Designers rely on psychological principles to create intuitive material that users interact with seamlessly. One key principle is the limitation of short-term memory, which holds only about seven items at once. To mitigate this, designers use chunking, grouping related elements (e.g., phone numbers as 123-456-7890). Another critical factor is proportion: studies show buttons in a 1:1.5 size ratio improve click accuracy, aligning with Fitts’s Law.

A thesis supported by multiple surveys of senior designers argues that top-tier apps belong to a league of their own due to consistent visual hierarchy. For instance, a finding from Nielsen Norman Group revealed that users experience shock—frustration—when navigation feels inconsistent. If users struggle to locate elements, it causes cognitive shock, disrupting workflow.

Research demonstrates that psychology-driven UI reduces errors and improves retention. By applying these principles, designers transform raw material into functional tools that align with human cognition.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the text compare UIs to a "cognitive organ"?
Correct: To emphasize that UIs process information like a biological organ, highlighting their role in cognitive efficiency.
Distractors:
1. To suggest that UIs should be designed like biological organs physically.
2. Because organs are visually appealing, making UIs more attractive.
3. To argue that UIs must be complex like human organs.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects the text’s metaphorical use of "organ" for function, not physical design. Distractor 1 misinterprets it literally; 2 confuses aesthetics with purpose; 3 contradicts the simplicity implied by chunking.
---
Q2: How does Fitts’s Law relate to the proportion of UI elements?
Correct: It suggests that buttons in a 1:1.5 size ratio improve accuracy by reducing movement time.
Distractors:
1. It claims smaller buttons are easier to click.
2. It requires all UI elements to be equal in size.
3. It proves that color contrast overrides button size.
Explanation:
The correct answer matches the proportion (1:1.5) mentioned. Distractor 1 reverses the ratio; 2 ignores the ratio entirely; 3 introduces an unrelated factor (color).
---
Q3: What finding from Nielsen Norman Group is mentioned, and how does it affect users?
Correct: Users experience frustration (shock) when navigation is inconsistent.
Distractors:
1. Users prefer apps with minimal colors.
2. Inconsistent UIs improve user retention.
3. Nielsen Norman Group found that larger buttons reduce errors.
Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases the shock caused by inconsistency. Distractor 1 misattributes the finding to color; 2 reverses the effect; 3 confuses Fitts’s Law with the survey.
---
Q4: How do senior designers contribute to the thesis about UI design?
Correct: Their surveys support the claim that top apps use consistent visual hierarchy.
Distractors:
1. They argue that UIs should avoid psychological principles.
2. Their work shows that short-term memory is irrelevant.
3. They design UIs based solely on personal preference.
Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with the text’s mention of surveys and league of top apps. Distractor 1 contradicts the supportive role; 2 ignores memory principles; 3 dismisses evidence-based design.
---
Q5: What happens if a UI ignores the principles of short-term memory?
Correct: Users may struggle to retain or locate information, leading to errors.
Distractors:
1. Users will remember more details.
2. The UI will look more professional.
3. Users will prefer the UI despite confusion.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects the text’s warning about overload. Distractor 1 reverses the effect; 2 confuses form with function; 3 misinterprets user reaction (shock implies dislike).

Topic: a humorous classification of different types of people in a public library or co-working space

Text:
Public libraries and co-working spaces are fundamentally microcosms of humanity—where every personality type finds its niche. To illustrate, consider three remarkably distinct categories.

First, the Treasure Hoarder: They treat books, headphones, or even the printer as a treasure, hoarding them like dragons guarding gold. Their visible distress if you touch their "spot" is legendary. Second, the Strict Enforcer, who sees silence as a necessity and shushing as a public service. They enforce rules with the wind-like efficiency of a librarian’s glare—no excuse (even "I was just stretching") will save you.

Then there’s the Excuse Artist, who slips into the space under false pretenses—"Oh, I’m researching!"—while scrolling memes. Fundamentally, all three types share one thing: they’ve mastered the art of avoiding real work. Yet remarkably, their presence makes the space visible as a sanctuary from the chaos outside.

So next time you see someone glaring at you for humming, remember: you’re not being strict—you’re part of the performance.

Questions:
Q1: What does the text imply about the Treasure Hoarder’s relationship with library items?

1. "They believe items are public property." / Wrong: Text uses "treasure" metaphor, implying possessiveness.
2. "They lend items freely to others." / Wrong: Hoarding suggests reluctance to share.
3. "They see items as tools, not treasures." / Wrong: Contradicts the "treasure" description.

Topic: a reflective essay on the experience of impostor syndrome in a new professional or social role

Text:
When I first sat in the chair of my new role as a project lead, I was delighted—until a voice whispered that I didn’t belong. Like a bat in headlights, I froze, realizing impostor syndrome was a component of this edition of me. The tragedy wasn’t the doubt itself, but how deeply it rooted in my foundation of self-worth.

My early failures had shaped this narrative: if I hadn’t been "good enough" then, how could I be now? Furthermore, the irony wasn’t lost on me—I’d spent years preparing for this grand opportunity, only to question whether I’d faked my way in. The foundation of my confidence crumbled under the weight of "What if they find out?"

Yet, over time, I learned to bat away the doubt like an annoying insect. The edition of me that once trembled now laughs at the memory. Impostor syndrome, it turns out, is just another layer of the human experience—messy, universal, and ultimately, part of what makes growth possible.

Explanation:
The correct answer captures the emotional paralysis implied by the metaphor. Distractor 1 misreads the metaphor literally; 2 overgeneralizes the fear; 3 reverses the direction (hiding vs. being seen).

Topic: a story about a portrait painter who realizes they are capturing the subject's soul

Text:
When Elias Voss first began painting portraits, he believed his skill lay only in capturing faces—stunning in their detail but lifeless. His newly opened studio in the high attic of an old mansion was dim, the lighting unreliable, yet he refused to complain. "A true artist doesn’t dare settle for mediocrity," he’d tell his skeptical clients. But after years of painting the same hollow expressions, Elias grew restless. His latest commission—a portrait of a reclusive musician—felt incorrect. The subject’s eyes, though closed, seemed to organize the very air around them, as if his soul lingered in the brushstrokes.

One evening, Elias worked late, adjusting the canvas by candlelight. The musician’s hands, usually still, trembled slightly—unique in their vulnerability. Elias hesitated, then painted them satisfyingly, as if the man’s essence had seeped into the pigments. The next morning, the client arrived, stared at the portrait, and whispered, "You’ve done more than paint me. You’ve resign me to eternity." Elias realized then that he hadn’t just captured a likeness; he’d stolen a soul.

Topic: a psychological mystery about a person who finds a photograph of themselves from a school they never attended

The healthcare system’s slow distribution of resources left her waiting months for proper support. Friends dismissed her distress as paranoia, but Lena knew the photo was real. The independence she’d fought for now felt fragile, as if the image had exposed a hidden layer of her identity.

Worse, the photo’s fabulous detail—a faded school emblem—suggested it wasn’t just a coincidence. Was it a hoax? A message? Lena’s obsession grew, blurring the line between mystery and madness.

Topic: an adventure story about teenagers who find themselves inside a computer game

Trees lay chopped down in a forest they’d never seen before, and their wrist devices (worn like smartwatches) displayed status updates about a global mortgage crisis, though their parents dismissed it as a conservative conspiracy theory.

“This isn’t just a game,” Aisha whispered as she scanned the horizon. The dense jungle surrounded them, but the sky flickered between pixelated and clear. Liam’s fingers trembled as he adjusted his device. “What if we’re not in Kansas anymore?” His parents, conservative skeptics, had laughed when he mentioned the glitch, calling it a phase. But the overseas server logs hinted at a deeper truth—one that might explain why the game’s physics defied logic.

A rustling in the bushes made them freeze. A portal shimmered between the trees, pulsing with energy. “Should we go?” Aisha asked. Liam hesitated. If this was real, their mortgage-paying parents might never find them. But if it wasn’t? The alternative was scarier: they were trapped in a simulation with no exit.

If I had stayed a child, I wouldn’t have had to write a thesis, or fix my own problems, or accept that the dairy cartons of my youth were now replaced by coffee cups. The confusion dissolved into clarity—I was growing up, and that was the creation of a new me.

Topic: a personal essay on the biggest misconception people have about me

Text:
"The biggest misconception about me is that I’m too serious. People assume I lack humor, but nothing could be further from the truth. Once, during a cave tour, my group’s guide told a joke so hilarious that we all laughed—even the usually stoic geologist. That moment proved I can embrace spontaneity, though I often overthink things. My leadership style thrives on organic unity; I believe teams perform best when they collaborate naturally, not rigidly. Some say I’m overly prepared, but I’d argue I’m sufficiently equipped to handle challenges. For years, I borrowed traits from others (like my sister’s patience), almost like a loan, to refine my approach. The misconception persists, though, so I offer an apology for any past moments when my seriousness seemed unwarranted. Over time, I’ve focused on expansion—growing beyond my initial self—while ensuring my team feels united. After all, unity isn’t about uniformity; it’s about harmony."

Feeling like an outsider isn’t about fitting in; it’s about knowing what’s appropriate for you—not others.

Topic: a personal story about a conversation that fundamentally altered a key relationship

Text:

My friend Clara, usually so composed, snapped, ‘You always take the cheaper option!’ I bristled, but beneath her offence lay something dull and festering: years of unspoken desire for me to acknowledge her struggles. Her thesis on emotional agency had always fascinated me, but I’d never applied its lessons. That night, I took agency, admitting I’d avoided tough talks to keep the peace. Clara’s eyes widened—not with anger, but relief. ‘I’ve wanted you to see me,’ she confessed. The restore of our bond felt like turning a globe: suddenly, the world of our friendship wasn’t just my placement in her life, but hers in mine. The shift was beneficial, though messy. Now, we tackle conflicts head-on, our relationship richer for the honesty.

Topic: a story about a time I discovered a secret that changed my perception of my family

Text:
When I was twelve, I discovered a secret that reshaped my understanding of my family. It began during a visit to my senior uncle’s farm, where he extensively grew vegetables and kept chickens. One evening, while helping him prepare dinner, I noticed steam rising from a hidden compartment in the kitchen. Curious, I peeked inside and found jars of preserved meat—not just ordinary meat, but protein-rich game he’d hunted himself. My uncle, sensing my confusion, assured me it was a tradition, but I later learned it was also a way to assure financial stability during hard times.

Moreover, the discovery revealed a tendency in our family to keep struggles private. My parents, who always seemed so composed, had once faced bankruptcy. My uncle’s assistance in those years had been crucial, yet no one had ever mentioned it. The timing of my discovery—just before my parents’ anniversary—made the revelation even more poignant. I realized they had bet on secrecy to protect us, but their silence had also created a gap in trust. That night, I asked my father about it, and his response changed everything: "Some burdens are lighter when shared." The secret didn’t erase our past, but it deepened my respect for their resilience.

Correct: Because it was protein-rich game that their uncle had hunted himself, which was not part of their usual diet.
Distractors:
1. It was spoiled and emitting an odd smell. (Wrong) – The text describes steam (neutral) and implies freshness ("protein-rich").
2. His uncle had secretly imported it from another country. (Wrong) – The text states it was hunted locally.
3. The family had never eaten meat before that discovery. (Wrong) – The focus is on the type (game) and method (hunting), not dietary habits.

Explanation:
The correct answer highlights the specificity of the meat (game, hunted) and its nutritional emphasis (protein-rich), which contrasts with the narrator’s prior assumptions. Distractors 1 and 2 misread sensory details (steam ≠ spoilage) and geographical context (no mention of imports). Distractor 3 ignores the text’s implication that meat was eaten but not this specific kind.

Distractors:
1. It made the narrator resent their parents for keeping secrets. (Wrong) – The text states the secret "deepened respect," not resentment.
2. It confirmed the narrator’s suspicion that the family was wealthy. (Wrong) – The secret was about struggle, not wealth.
3. It led the narrator to question their uncle’s hunting skills. (Wrong) – The focus is on family dynamics, not hunting competence.

Explanation:
The correct answer captures the dual emotional shift: respect (resilience) and critique (gap in trust). Distractor 1 misinterprets "poignant" as negative; distractors 2–3 ignore the text’s emphasis on financial hardship and shared burdens.
---

Correct: He provided critical assistance during their parents’ bankruptcy, though his help was never openly discussed.
Distractors:
1. He was the one who caused the family’s financial problems. (Wrong) – The text implies he helped, not harmed.
2. He refused to help and left the family during hard times. (Wrong) – The text says his assistance was "crucial."
3. He secretly stole money from the family to survive. (Wrong) – No evidence of theft; the focus is on hunting/game preservation.

Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with "crucial assistance" and "no one had ever mentioned it." Distractors 1–3 contradict the text’s positive framing of his role and lack of evidence for malice.
---

Correct: It occurred just before the parents’ anniversary, which made the revelation about their past struggles more emotionally charged.
Distractors:

2. The timing was random, with no emotional significance. (Wrong) – The text explicitly links it to poignancy.
3. It coincided with a family argument, revealing deeper conflicts. (Wrong) – No argument is mentioned; the focus is on silence and trust.

Explanation:
The correct answer ties timing to the anniversary’s symbolic weight. Distractors 1–3 ignore the text’s emphasis on emotional resonance and misattribute context (birthday/argument).
---

Correct: It suggests that the family’s tendency to keep struggles private may have unintentionally isolated them, and openness could have eased their emotional load.
Distractors:
1. The family should have shared their problems with outsiders, like neighbors or friends. (Wrong) – The text implies family sharing, not external help.
2. Secrets are always beneficial, as they protect family unity. (Wrong) – The text critiques secrecy ("gap in trust").
3. The narrator’s discovery was a burden, not a relief. (Wrong) – The phrase is the parents’ reflection, not the narrator’s.

Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases the text’s critique of secrecy ("gap in trust") and the implied benefit of sharing ("burdens are lighter"). Distractors 1–3 misrepresent the text’s focus on internal family dynamics and the negative connotation of secrecy.

Topic: a story about a time I had to live with a difficult person and what it taught me

Text:
When I moved in with my uncle last year, I expected a pure living arrangement—help with rent, shared meals, and a quiet space to study. Instead, I found myself in a puzzle of conflicting personalities. My uncle, a retired trader, had strong opinions, and I often found myself opposing his views, especially about money. At first, I speculated that he was just stubborn, but his humorous way of arguing—mixing sarcasm with logic—made it hard to stay annoyed.

The biggest challenge was adjusting to his expectations. He assumed I’d take on full-time chores, like cooking and cleaning, without discussing it first. When I pointed out that my workload was already heavy, he dismissed my concerns, saying, “You’ll adjust or leave.” That wasn’t adequate for me, but I stayed, determined to turn the experience into a lesson.

Over time, I realized his strictness wasn’t personal—it was his way of teaching responsibility. Even his trading metaphors (“Life’s like the stock market—you’ve got to take risks”) became oddly insightful. By the end, I’d learned that difficult people often have pure intentions, even if their methods are flawed. The lesson? Patience and communication can turn a frustrating situation into growth.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the narrator initially assume their uncle’s strictness was personal, and how did this assumption change over time?
Correct: At first, the narrator thought their uncle’s behavior was simply stubbornness, but later realized his strictness was a way to teach responsibility, not a personal attack.
Distractors:
1. The narrator believed their uncle was trying to control them financially. (Wrong—the text mentions trading metaphors, not financial control.)
2. Their uncle’s humor made the narrator think he was joking about his demands. (Wrong—the humor was part of his argument style, not a cover for demands.)
3. The narrator assumed their uncle was lazy and avoided chores himself. (Wrong—the uncle expected full-time chores from the narrator, not the opposite.)
Explanation: The correct answer captures the shift from speculating about stubbornness to recognizing pure intentions. Distractor 1 misinterprets the trading context; 2 ignores the uncle’s opposing views; 3 contradicts the uncle’s expectations of the narrator.

Explanation: The correct answer reflects the uncle’s humorous style and its adjusting effect on the narrator’s emotions. Distractor 1 misreads “hard to stay annoyed” as increased anger; 2 ignores the narrator’s later appreciation; 3 contradicts the uncle’s direct opposing of the narrator’s views.

Topic: a fantasy story about a librarian who discovers the books can rewrite themselves

Or something far older? Despite the chaos, she remained optimistic, convinced the answers lay in tracing the source. "Perhaps the library protects itself," Marcus muttered, but Eleanor shook her head. "No—it’s acquiring knowledge, rewriting history to fit its own rules."

As dawn broke, the books stilled, leaving no trace of their transformation. Yet Eleanor knew the truth: the library was alive, and she had only begun to understand its power.

Topic: a reflective essay on the concept of home in an increasingly globalized and mobile society

Text:
The concept of home has evolved considerably in our globalized world. For early settlers, home was a fixed plot of land; today, it’s a fluid idea shaped by work and circumstance. Economic pressures, such as the 2008 recession, forced mass migrations, thus challenging traditional notions of belonging. A front door, once a clear marker of stability, now symbolizes impermanence for many.

Routines, once the backbone of domestic life, are disrupted by constant relocation. The bond between people and place weakens when borders blur, yet a smile on the cheek might hide the loneliness beneath. Childhood homes feel golden in memory, but for the displaced, they become distant echoes. The loss of home is apparent in statistics—millions live without fixed addresses—but its emotional weight is harder to measure.

Thus, home is no longer just a physical space but a state of mind. It’s found in shared meals, laughter, or even a temporary rental. The apparent instability of modern life has redefined what it means to belong.

1. "The recession made people buy more houses to feel secure."
2. "Home became more expensive after the recession."
3. "People stopped moving because the recession made home unaffordable."
Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with the text’s claim that the recession forced migrations, redefining home as flexible. Distractor 1 misrepresents the focus (security vs. mobility); 2 ignores the conceptual shift; 3 reverses the cause-effect.

Topic: an article exploring the ethics of digital legacies and social media profiles after death

Text:
The Ethics of Digital Legacies
Broadly speaking, the rise of digital legacies raises ethical questions we’re only beginning to address. When someone dies, their social media profiles—often a mate’s last connection—become a battleground. Families may possess emotional ties to posts, but platforms control access, creating conflicts. For instance, acid comments on a memorial page can’t be erased by martial laws, which don’t apply online. Grief is hard enough to overcome without legal ambiguity.

A wise approach involves planning ahead, like setting profiles to "memorial mode," but this isn’t always possible. Laws lag behind technology, and emotional needs often exceed legal protections. Some argue that reasonably private profiles should be honored, while others fear exploitation. The digital emission of personal data—even after death—demands accountability. Platforms must balance transparency with respect, ensuring families aren’t left powerless.

Ultimately, society must collaborate to address this. While technology moves fast, ethical frameworks should keep pace. The key lies in treating digital legacies with the same care as physical ones—because in death, as in life, our connections matter most.

Topic: a reflective essay on the value of solitude in a hyper-connected world

Text:
In today’s hyper-connected world, solitude often feels like a lost art. Many of us are creatures of habit, constantly bombarded by notifications and the pursuit of instant convenience. Yet, research from a leading analyst firm suggests that even short periods of withdrawal from digital noise can significantly boost productivity and creativity. The problem? Most people struggle to disconnect, believing that constant coverage of tasks and social interactions is essential for success.

A recent study examined how solitude affects output—not just in terms of quantity, but quality. Participants who spent just 30 minutes in silence reported clearer thinking and deeper focus. One operator of a tech company admitted that his most innovative ideas emerged during moments of solitude, not in meetings or brainstorming sessions. However, the modern workplace often treats solitude as a luxury, fragmenting our time into micro-tasks that leave little room for reflection.

The irony is that while technology was meant to simplify life, it has created a paradox: we have more tools at our disposal, yet feel more disconnected. The key, perhaps, lies in balancing connection with intentional solitude—recognizing that even in a world obsessed with output, the quiet moments are where true insight is born.

Topic: an article about the paradox of choice - why more options often make us less happy

Text:
We live in an era where volume of choices dominates every affair of daily life—from streaming services to grocery aisles. While we assume more options will make us happier, studies show the opposite. Psychologists call this the "paradox of choice": the more alternatives we face, the more we resist committing to any. Stuck in a circuit of overthinking, we hesitate, second-guess, and often regret decisions before we even draft one.

Our brains aren’t wired to handle infinite possibilities. When confronted with a volume of options—say, 20 types of cereal—we fear missing the "perfect" choice. This anxiety chops at our confidence. Even brands exploit this: their logos promise simplicity, yet their websites offer endless customizations. In rural areas, where fewer choices exist, people report higher satisfaction. There, decisions feel lighter, like immigration to a simpler life.

The solution? Stop treating choices as affairs of the heart. Instead of endlessly comparing, draft a decision and commit. If you resist, you remain stuck in the circuit. As the philosopher Barry Schwartz argues, "Freedom of choice is only valuable when we use it wisely." Next time you’re paralyzed by options, ask: Is this really necessary, or am I just afraid to chop through the noise?

Questions:
Q1: Why does the author compare decision-making to "immigration"?
Correct: The author uses "immigration" to illustrate how our brains hesitate to adopt new options, creating resistance to change.
Distractors:
1. "The author suggests that making decisions is like physically relocating." / Wrong: Text implies mental resistance, not physical movement.
2. "Immigration symbolizes the emotional weight of changing habits." / Wrong: Focuses on resisting options, not habits.
3. "The comparison highlights how choices are permanent like immigration." / Wrong: Text suggests temporary resistance, not permanence.
---
Q2: How does the "volume" of choices affect our ability to make decisions?
Correct: The volume of choices creates overwhelm and hesitation, making it harder to commit to any single option.
Distractors:
1. "More options lead to quicker decisions." / Wrong: Text states we hesitate.
2. "The volume of choices reduces anxiety." / Wrong: Text says it increases fear of missing the perfect choice.
3. "It’s only problematic in rural areas." / Wrong: Text contrasts urban/rural but focuses on urban overload.
---
Q3: What metaphor does the author use to describe the mental state of overthinking?
Correct: The author describes overthinking as being "stuck in a circuit" of repetitive indecision.
Distractors:
1. "The author compares it to a maze." / Wrong: Text uses "circuit" (looping, not maze).
2. "Overthinking is like a storm in the mind." / Wrong: No mention of storm; metaphor is circuit.
3. "It’s described as a prison of indecision." / Wrong: Text says "circuit" (suggests repetition, not confinement).
---
Q4: According to the text, why do brands with many customization options use simple logos?
Correct: Brands use simple logos to create an illusion of simplicity, masking the complexity of their choices.
Distractors:
1. "To hide the complexity of their products." / Wrong: Text says "promise simplicity" (not hide).
2. "Because simple logos are easier to trademark." / Wrong: No mention of trademarks.
3. "To attract customers who dislike decision fatigue." / Wrong: Text doesn’t link logos to decision fatigue.
---
Q5: What does the author suggest as a practical way to overcome the paradox of choice?
Correct: The author advises drafting a decision and committing to it instead of endlessly comparing options.
Distractors:
1. "Avoid making any decisions at all." / Wrong: Text says "commit" (opposite).
2. "Rely on others to make choices for you." / Wrong: No mention of delegation.
3. "Accept that some decisions will be wrong." / Wrong: Focuses on committing, not accepting mistakes.



Topic: an essay about the advantages and disadvantages of remote work

Text:
Remote work, with its origin in tech startups, has become a global phenomenon. One of its key advantages is the ability to specialize in fields that require niche expertise, as companies can hire talent regardless of coverage area. Employees often feel more motivated when they control their schedules, leading to higher productivity. Additionally, remote work eliminates commute costs, saving individuals hundreds per month on transport and office account fees. Faster project delivery is another perk, as teams collaborate across time zones without delays.

However, remote work isn’t without flaws. Without clear boundaries, employees may ruin their work-life balance, blurring the lines between professional and personal time. The lack of in-person interaction can also remove essential cues for teamwork, leaving some feeling isolated. Even those who thrive in remote settings might struggle to "stay in their lane" without guidance, leading to misaligned priorities. For creatives, like songwriters, the absence of a shared space can stifle inspiration—imagine trying to craft a lyric without brainstorming sessions!

Ultimately, remote work’s success hinges on adaptability. Companies must provide structure to prevent chaos, while employees should set limits to avoid burnout.

Topic: a case study on the use of gamification in advertising

Text:
Gamification in Advertising: A Case Study

Traditional advertising risks starving brands of engagement by failing to interact with audiences. Gamification—integrating game mechanics into ads—has emerged as a solution, but its success depends on execution. A poorly designed campaign can bomb, leaving users frustrated. For example, a fast-food chain’s app-based scavenger hunt grabbed attention but starved interest when rewards were unclear.

The victory of gamification lies in its ability to motivate. Discounts and badges act as incentives, while real-time feedback loops enhance user retention. A study found that brands using gamified ads saw a 40% increase in saving marketing budgets by reducing paid promotions. However, overuse can be harmful. If users feel manipulated—like they’re being tricked into engagement—they’ll disengage.

Funding remains a hurdle, especially for startups testing gamified ads. Without proper resources, even well-intentioned campaigns may fail. The key is balance: creative mechanics paired with ethical design. Brands must ensure feedback feels rewarding, not exploitative, to avoid backlash.

In conclusion, gamification can revolutionize advertising, but it requires careful planning. When done right, it turns passive viewers into active participants—saving costs while enhancing brand loyalty.

Questions:
Q1: Why might a gamified ad campaign "bomb"?
Correct: Poor design frustrates users, causing disengagement.
Distractors:
1. Because it requires too much funding to develop. / Wrong: Funding is a separate issue.
2. Because users find it too easy to complete. / Wrong: Bombing implies failure, not ease.
3. Because it lacks visual appeal. / Wrong: Text focuses on mechanics, not aesthetics.
Explanation: The correct answer aligns with the text’s emphasis on execution. Distractor 1 confuses funding (a hurdle) with failure. Distractor 2 misinterprets "bomb" as success. Distractor 3 ignores the focus on interactivity.
---

2. It makes ads more boring. / Wrong: Feedback increases engagement.

And so, they met—not as strangers, but as two people who had already built something extraordinary. The exhibit became their neutral ground, and the city, their gorgeous stage.

Topic: a suspenseful story about an elevator in a skyscraper that occasionally stops at a floor not in the building's plans

Text:
The lively skyscraper of GlassCorp was the ideal workplace for ambitious professionals, but its elevators had a reputation. Every few months, one would stop between floors—never on any registered level. Employees joked about it, but no one investigated.

One evening, as the city hummed below, junior analyst Daniel took the last elevator up. The doors slid shut, and the car descended smoothly—until it halted. The display read Floor X, a number that didn’t exist. The lights flickered. Daniel stepped out into a narrow corridor, its walls lined with exposed pipes and construction debris. At the end lay a package, wrapped in plastic and labeled "Property of GlassCorp."

Before Daniel could touch it, the elevator dinged. A representative from maintenance stepped out, his face pale. "You shouldn’t have come," he muttered. "This floor isn’t affordable to visit." He urged Daniel to withdraw, but the young man noticed the man’s hands trembling. "Why does this keep happening?" Daniel asked. The representative didn’t answer. Instead, he pressed a button, and the elevator lurched upward—only to stop again, this time at Floor X during a storm. "It’s not random," Daniel realized. "It’s an advance toward something."

Back in his apartment, Daniel found a hidden logbook in the elevator’s maintenance closet. The entries matched the stops: always during full moons, always at Floor X. The city below was too lively to ignore the spice of fear now tangled in his thoughts.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the representative urge Daniel to withdraw from the building?
Correct: The representative is terrified of the unplanned floor’s hidden dangers, which may include supernatural elements or corporate secrets.
Distractors:
1. "The representative is testing Daniel’s loyalty to the company."
2. "The floor is structurally unsafe due to poor construction."
3. "Daniel accidentally damaged the elevator, and the representative is angry."
Explanation:
- Correct: The text describes the representative’s fear ("face pale," "hands trembling") and vague warnings ("not affordable to visit"), implying something beyond physical danger.

- Distractor 2: Incorrect because the text hints at mystery, not structural failure. "Construction debris" could mislead readers to focus on safety.
- Distractor 3: Incorrect because there’s no evidence Daniel caused the stop. A careless reader might blame him for the elevator’s behavior.

Topic: a fantasy story about finding an "impossible object" in an ancient fossil layer

Text:
In a remote coastal harbour, geologist Dr. Elara Voss uncovered an astonishing artifact buried deep within a fossil layer. The object—a smooth, metallic line etched with symbols—defied all known materials. Its surface emitted a faint, pulsating glow, unlike any electronics ever studied. "This isn’t from Earth," Elara muttered, her voice trembling. The discovery seemed a sheer coincidence, yet the plot thickened when she realized the fossil layer dated back 500 million years—long before human civilization.

The artifact was an exception among fossils, its composition external to Earth’s natural elements. Hence, its origin remained a mystery. Some whispered it was a fragment of an ancient spaceship, others claimed it was a relic from heaven. Elara’s team dismissed the theories, but the object’s allure was undeniable. As she held it, the symbols rearranged into a map—one that led not to a place, but to a story yet untold.
