Topic: a review of different architectural approaches to building in earthquake-prone zones

Text:
Architectural Approaches to Earthquake-Resistant Building

Earthquake-prone regions demand innovative architectural solutions. Some cultures possess traditional knowledge, such as Japan’s use of flexible wood frames, which document resilience over centuries. Modern engineers, however, devote resources to advanced systems like base isolation, where buildings defend themselves against tremors by decoupling from foundations. Critics cite the 2011 Japan earthquake as proof of its effectiveness, though costs remain high.

Materials must be appropriate for local conditions—steel in urban zones, reinforced concrete in rural areas. Strong wind forces also influence design, particularly in coastal regions. A local institute in Chile, for instance, developed a hybrid system combining damping devices with traditional adobe techniques, showcasing architectural diversity.

Yet challenges persist. Some argue that over-reliance on high-tech solutions neglects cultural practices. Further enquiry reveals that communities often reject imported designs, preferring adaptations of their own methods. Governments must balance innovation with accessibility, ensuring seismic codes are both rigorous and practical.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the text mention Japan’s flexible wood frames alongside modern base-isolation systems?
Correct: To demonstrate how both traditional and modern approaches contribute to earthquake resilience.
Distractors:
1. "The text implies traditional methods are inferior to modern ones."
   - Wrong: The text does not rank them; it shows complementary roles.
2. "Japan’s wood frames are highlighted because they are the most cost-effective option."
   - Wrong: Cost is not the focus; the emphasis is on diversity of solutions.
3. "The comparison proves that base isolation is the only reliable method."
   - Wrong: The text does not exclude traditional methods; it contrasts them.

Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with the text’s contrast of approaches (traditional vs. modern). Distractor 1 misinterprets the neutral tone; Distractor 2 confuses implied cost (later) with the stated purpose (diversity); Distractor 3 ignores the plurality of solutions presented.

Topic: a story about the experience of returning to work after a long-term absence and feeling like a stranger

The agenda for my first meeting assumed I knew everyone’s projects, but their observations that I was "different" stung. The workload hit hardest. Tasks that once felt natural now demanded a portion of the old me I’d lost. My team had moved on; their failed attempts to include me in small talk only highlighted the gap. Worse, the office culture had shifted from manufacturing pragmatism to tech-speak jargon. By week three, I accepted I wasn’t just rusty—I was a stranger in my own story.

Topic: an adventure story about finding a hidden image under a painting

Text:
The mysterious oil painting, "Whispers of the Past," had hung in the museum for a century, its consumption by time leaving only faint traces of its original brilliance. When the gallery announced a contest to uncover its secrets, art historian Dr. Elena Vasquez became one of its most eager observers. Though initially met with disappointment—the painting’s surface revealed nothing unusual—she refused to abandon her search. Working efficiently, she examined every round inch of the frame, convinced the answer lay beneath the visible layers.

After weeks of scrutiny, Elena noticed a slight irregularity near the bottom round. Using ultraviolet light, she confirmed her suspicion: the painting’s underside held another image, efficiently concealed by the artist. With trembling hands, she peeled back the canvas to reveal a hidden depiction of a bear, its eyes glowing with an almost positive energy. The bear, now revealed, carried a message in its paws: a date matching the painting’s creation. The completion of her discovery was met with awe—she had solved the puzzle before the contest’s final round.

As the crowd gathered, Elena explained how the bear’s position aligned with the frame’s round edges, a clue the artist had left for future observers. Though some expressed disappointment at the mystery’s resolution, most agreed it was a stroke of genius. The painting, once forgotten, now stood as a testament to hidden artistry and the rewards of persistence.

Topic: a critical analysis of the wellness industry

Text:
The wellness industry often markets itself through lyric language, painting self-care as a poetic journey rather than a business. Critics argue that it preserves the illusion of holistic health while ignoring systemic issues like healthcare access. Even surgeons have spoken out against unproven treatments sold as "natural" cures, especially in rural counties where wellness retreats remain a luxury. Activists oppose the industry’s expansion, claiming it exploits vulnerable consumers under the guise of self-empowerment. These "wellness zones"—often exclusive retreats—reinforce inequality, leaving many as mere spectators in their own health.

The industry’s influence extends to daily life, dictating a rigid schedule of paid meditation apps and overpriced juices. Yet, do these practices adequately address mental health crises? Research suggests that while mindfulness can help, it’s no substitute for therapy or social support. The problem isn’t self-care itself but its commercialization, which turns personal well-being into another consumer product. To move forward, we need evidence-based alternatives that prioritize real health over aesthetic trends.

Questions:
Q1: How does the text describe the wellness industry’s use of language, and what does this imply about its motives?
Correct: The industry employs poetic, emotional (lyric) language to frame self-care as transformative, suggesting its motives are more about profit than genuine health improvement.
Distractors:
1. It uses technical jargon to appeal to scientists. / Why wrong? Text describes lyric (emotional) language, not jargon.
2. It implies the industry is entirely honest about its products. / Why wrong? Text critiques the illusion of honesty (preserves illusion).
3. The language is designed to confuse consumers with complexity. / Why wrong? Lyric suggests simplicity/emotion, not complexity.
---
Q2: What specific professional group is mentioned as warning against unproven wellness treatments, and why might their opinion be significant?
Correct: Surgeons are cited for their medical authority, lending credibility to critiques of the industry’s lack of regulation.
Distractors:

Topic: an essay about the advantages and disadvantages of minimalism in interior design

Text:
Minimalism in interior design emphasizes simplicity, functionality, and the removal of excess. While this style has numerous advantages, it also faces criticism. One prime benefit is its ability to reduce stress by eliminating clutter, which can feel like a bombing of unnecessary stimuli. Minimalist spaces are easier to clean and maintain, making them practical for both homes and workplaces, where focus is essential. Additionally, minimalism helps establish a sense of harmony by prioritizing essential elements.

However, minimalism is not without flaws. Some people find it confusing, particularly if they associate it with sterile environments. Critics argue that the style lacks diversity, offering limited opportunities for artistic expression. Feedback from designers and homeowners often highlights this as a drawback, with many preferring more entertaining maximalist designs that incorporate bold colors and patterns. The perception of minimalism as cold or impersonal can also deter potential adopters. While it suits certain lifestyles, its rigid principles may not appeal to everyone.

In conclusion, minimalism’s advantages—such as reduced stress and efficiency—are significant, but its disadvantages, including a lack of warmth and creativity, must be considered. Ultimately, the style’s success depends on individual perception and needs.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the author compare clutter to a "sensory bombing"?
Correct: Clutter overwhelms the mind with unnecessary stimuli, creating stress.
Distractors:
1. It suggests clutter is physically dangerous.
2. It implies clutter is visually appealing but chaotic.
3. It means clutter is difficult to clean.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects the text’s metaphor ("bombing of unnecessary stimuli"). Distractor 1 misinterprets "bombing" literally; 2 conflates sensory overload with visual appeal; 3 ignores the focus on mental stress. Weak readers might prioritize the word "bombing" over its figurative use.
---

Correct: It reduces distractions and promotes focus.
Distractors:
1. It makes workplaces more entertaining.
2. It increases social interaction.
3. It requires more time to organize.
Explanation:
The text links minimalism to focus and reduced stress, not entertainment (distractor 1) or social dynamics (2). Distractor 3 contradicts the "easier to clean" advantage. Weak readers might associate "workplace" with social or entertainment contexts.
---
Q3: What specific criticism do designers make about minimalism’s lack of diversity?
Correct: They argue it limits artistic expression and bold decoration.
Distractors:
1. It lacks color entirely.
2. It is too expensive for most people.
3. It is only popular in urban areas.
Explanation:
The text criticizes limited artistic expression, not absence of color (1). Cost-effectiveness is an advantage (2), and no geographic limits are mentioned (3). Weak readers might assume "diversity" = "color" or project external biases.
---
Q4: In what way might minimalism be perceived as confusing?
Correct: Its strict simplicity can feel cold or impersonal to those expecting warmth.
Distractors:
1. It is difficult to implement correctly.
2. It requires specialized knowledge.
3. It is only suitable for modern architecture.
Explanation:
The text describes perception of coldness (not implementation difficulty, 1) or expertise (2). No architectural links are made (3). Weak readers might focus on "confusing" as technical or stylistic.
---
Q5: According to the text, what determines whether minimalism is successful for an individual?
Correct: Personal preferences and lifestyle needs.
Distractors:
1. The size of the space.
2. The designer’s reputation.
3. The climate of the region.
Explanation:
The text emphasizes individual perception and needs, not physical space (1), designer influence (2), or climate (3). Weak readers might assume external factors like space or weather play a role.



Topic: a commentary on the modern phenomenon of over-posting a travel experience

Text:
The modern traveler’s database of Instagram posts has become a critical lens through which experiences are measured. What was once an acceptable way to share memories has erupted into an obsession with curation. As one economist noted, the rise of over-posting reflects a cultural shift where urban destinations—like Paris or Tokyo—are now judged by their photogenic potential rather than their soul. Meanwhile, rural counties remain underrepresented, their charm lost in the algorithm’s favor.

Social media acts as a referee, determining what constitutes a "good" trip. Travelers beat the system by cherry-picking moments, crafting a narrative of perfection that bears little resemblance to reality. The result? A hollowed-out version of travel, where the pursuit of likes overshadows genuine connection. Even the most breathtaking landscapes lose their soul when reduced to a grid of filtered images.

The problem isn’t just aesthetic—it’s systemic. A database of curated perfection trains us to expect flawless experiences, making anything less feel inadequate. It’s time for a critical reassessment: Are we documenting life, or performing it for an audience?

- Distractor 1: Ignores the authenticity critique; focuses on quantity, not quality.

- Distractor 3: Reverses the text’s argument (urban over-posting is worse, not more acceptable).

Topic: a personal story about the moment I realized I was perpetuating a cycle I had sworn to break

Text:
I had always prided myself on my discipline—whereas my friends struggled with procrastination, I swore I’d never repeat their mistakes. Then, one evening, I studied psychology and encountered the concept of self-sabotage. The more I read, the more I realized my "discipline" was a sophisticated trap: I’d set goals, then subsequently undermine them with excuses.

The moment of clarity hit during a meeting where my impatient team—troop, really—rushed through ideas. I’d snap like a broken ruler, then berate myself for it. "Why can’t I just be patient?" I’d think, almost calling my patience cute when it vanished. But the truth was uglier: I wasn’t just impatient; I was perpetuating a cycle. My mind had a chairman—a voice that whispered, "You’ll fail anyway, so why try?"—and I’d unknowingly elected it.

Reading about terrorists in psychology texts (metaphorically, of course), I saw my impatience as an enemy. It wasn’t just a flaw; it was a system. If I hadn’t studied psychology, I might have spent years blaming myself whereas the real issue was the cycle itself. That night, I drafted a plan: no more excuses, no more chairmen. The first step? Admitting I’d been the terrorist all along.

Explanation:
- Correct: Matches the text’s metaphor ("enemy in my mind") and the cycle’s repetitive harm.
- Distractor 1: Ignores the "metaphorically" clarification.
- Distractor 2: Contradicts the text’s focus on repetition (cycle).
- Distractor 3: Misinterprets mental disruption as physical violence.

Topic: a story about mediating a conflict between two people I care about deeply

Text:
My best friend, Mark, and his sister, Lisa, had been at each other’s throats for weeks. These two stubborn creatures of habit—he conservative to the core, she a progressive activist—couldn’t agree on how to handle their late father’s estate. The shock of their accusations during dinner silenced the room. I, caught in the middle, recalled my compulsory conflict-resolution training from nursing school: stay neutral, listen, and seek common ground.

Lisa stormed out after Mark called her ideas "impractical," while he muttered about "textbook cases" of family disputes. I nudged Mark’s elbow to lower his voice. "You’re both right in your regard for your father," I said, "but you’re unconscious of how your biases are hardening your stances." He scoffed, but Lisa paused, her anger flickering.

That night, I reread my textbook on mediation. Ethical dilemmas like theirs required patience. The next day, Lisa admitted she’d been so focused on "doing right" that she’d ignored Mark’s grief. He, in turn, softened when she said, "Maybe we’re both just creatures of fear." They didn’t resolve everything, but the tension eased.

I realized my nursing background had taught me more than sterile techniques—it had trained me to see people, not just problems. The compulsory lessons had prepared me for this: conflicts aren’t won; they’re navigated with regard for all sides.

Topic: a personal story about the moment I realized I was jealous of a friend's success

Text:
The moment I realized I was jealous of my friend Alex’s success was during a beach trip last summer. We had always shared a universal bond—until that day, when he announced he’d won a prestigious award. His status as a rising star in our field suddenly felt like a threat, though I’d never admit it. The infection of envy crept in silently, like a tide pulling me toward the shore of self-doubt.

At first, I rationalized it: his achievement was the equivalent of years of hard work, while my own projects had stalled. But deep down, I resented his participation in a networking scheme that had excluded me. My nerve failed when he joked about "beating" the competition—his confidence stung. I wondered if his success was built on evil luck or sheer talent, but the truth was simpler: I feared my own stagnation.

That evening, as we walked along the water, I confessed my feelings. Alex laughed and said, "Jealousy’s just proof you care." His words cut through the fog. I realized envy wasn’t about him—it was about my own scheme of self-sabotage. The tide receded, and for the first time, I saw his success as a challenge, not a threat.

Explanation:
The correct answer ties the metaphor to emotional states. Distractor 1 conflates universal with the metaphor. Distractor 2 mislinks the scheme to the setting. Distractor 3 misplaces the argument’s timing.



Topic: a story about how an unusual compromise resolved a conflict

Text:
The small village of Eldermere had long been dominated by the decisions of a massive technological corporation, which sought to expand its operations nearby. Out of spite, the villagers resisted, fearing job losses and environmental damage. Their situation was depressing: the village’s economy relied on outdated farming methods, while the company’s advances left them feeling obsolete. The villagers suspected the corporation of hiding its true intentions, and tensions escalated.

One evening, as the community gathered in frustration, a ten-year-old girl named Mira spoke up. Unconscious of the political weight of her words, she suggested using the company’s discarded technological equipment to modernize the village’s irrigation system. Her idea was surprisingly wise—it offered a win-win solution. The corporation, initially dismissive, saw an opening when Mira’s proposal gained traction. After negotiations, they agreed to train villagers in sustainable tech, reinforcing trust between both parties. Hence, the conflict dissolved, and Eldermere transformed from a struggling community into a model of innovation.

Topic: an essay about the advantages and disadvantages of developing tourism

Text:
Tourism has grown from a formerly modest industry into a golden economic opportunity for many countries. Conversely, responsible tourism can preserve heritage while generating revenue. A high rating from travelers might reflect not just luxury but also ethical standards, such as supporting local artisans openly rather than exploiting them. Balancing profit and sustainability is key; without it, tourism risks becoming a double-edged sword.

Topic: an essay about the benefits of playing computer games

Text:
While critics argue that gaming is a waste of time, research shows it offers distinct advantages beyond entertainment. One key benefit is cognitive development: games like puzzles or strategy titles act as "protection" against mental decline by sharpening problem-solving skills. Studies reveal that regular, weekly sessions improve memory and focus—far more than passively reading a notebook of facts.

Socially, multiplayer games foster teamwork, teaching players to communicate effectively under pressure. Completing challenging levels also brings a sense of satisfaction, reducing stress. Even if you’re sitting on your heel during a marathon session, you’re absorbing "info"—whether it’s historical facts in an adventure game or language skills in a role-playing title.

Physically, while gaming isn’t a substitute for outdoor exercise (unlike standing in the wind), active titles encourage movement. More importantly, games create a "greenhouse" of creativity: players design solutions in real time, a skill valuable in any career.

Critics claim gaming is isolating, but the positive effects on collaboration and learning outweigh the negatives. Balanced play—like any hobby—yields rewards. So next time someone dismisses your controller, remind them: gaming isn’t just fun; it’s a tool for growth.

3. "Gaming reduces screen time, leading to better focus." (Wrong: Not mentioned.)
Explanation: The correct answer aligns with the text’s focus on cognitive benefits (problem-solving/memory) and examples (puzzles/strategy). Distractors 1–3 misrepresent contrasts, metaphors, or omit evidence.

Topic: an essay on whether zoos should be banned

Text:
The subject of whether zoos should be banned is one of the most debated topics in animal welfare. Proponents argue that zoos play a crucial role in secure habitats for endangered species, ensuring their nutrition and medical care. Skilled conservationists often work in zoos to breed animals and reintroduce them to the wild, a task that would be nearly impossible without these facilities. Additionally, zoos motivate public interest in conservation, acting almost like a mosque’s role in inspiring community values.

However, critics highlight the psychological harm animals endure in captivity, often reduced to mere monsters in the eyes of visitors. The confinement can lead to stress and unnatural behaviors, raising ethical questions about whether zoos prioritize human entertainment over animal well-being. Some argue that overseas sanctuaries or digital alternatives could achieve similar educational goals without the ethical drawbacks.

To evaluate the issue fairly, we must consider both perspectives. While zoos have undeniable benefits, stricter regulations and a shift toward ethical alternatives—such as wildlife reserves—could strike a balance. The debate ultimately hinges on whether zoos can reform or if they should be phased out entirely.

3. Zoos replace the need for wildlife reserves entirely.

Topic: an essay on how virtual reality might change education in the future

Text:
Virtual reality (VR) will reshape education, though its full potential remains untapped. Traditional classrooms rely on conventions like lectures and textbooks, which fail to engage students effectively. VR, however, could stand as a revolutionary alternative by immersing learners in interactive environments. For instance, students might explore ancient Rome or dissect virtual frogs, making complex subjects more accessible. The exception to this might be subjects like art, where VR allows students to create artwork in 3D spaces, fostering creativity beyond conventional boundaries.

Beyond engagement, VR could transform examinations. Instead of written tests, students might solve problems in a simulated lab or debate in a virtual courtroom, assessing skills more holistically. Yet, risks persist. The digital nature of VR raises concerns about theft of intellectual property or insecure data breaches. To mitigate these, educational associations could collaborate with tech firms to develop encrypted platforms. While challenges exist, the beneficial outcomes—such as safer field trips or specialized training—outweigh the drawbacks. Ultimately, VR is not just a tool but a paradigm shift that could make education more dynamic and inclusive.

Topic: an essay on the responsibility of individuals versus corporations in fighting climate change

Text:
The urgency of climate change is no fairy tale; scientists observe record temperatures and melting ice caps with alarming frequency. Efforts to combat it comprise actions from individuals and corporations alike, yet neither can address the crisis entirely on their own. While individuals cope with daily choices—recycling, reducing plastic, or using public transport—these steps, though valuable, document only a fraction of global emissions. Studies show that even the most eco-conscious person contributes far less to carbon output than a single factory.

Corporations, however, produce 71% of global emissions, a statistic that implies their outsized responsibility. Some industries remain opposed to regulation, arguing that green policies will harm profits. Yet, delaying action implies irreversible damage to ecosystems. Media coverage often tells a different tale, focusing on individual heroes who bike to work or sort their trash, while corporate accountability receives far less attention. This creates a growing sense of helplessness among the public, who may feel their efforts are insignificant compared to systemic issues.

The reality is that both individuals and corporations must act. While personal changes matter, systemic reform—pushed by corporate accountability—is essential. The coverage of climate action should reflect this balance, not just the tale of personal virtue.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the author argue that media coverage of climate change often tells a misleading "tale"?
Correct: It exaggerates the impact of individual actions while minimizing corporate responsibility.
Distractors:
1. Media ignores climate action entirely, making people think nothing can be done.
2. Media only reports on corporate pollution, making people blame businesses too much.
3. Media uses fairy tales to scare people into changing their habits.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches the text’s critique of media focusing on "individual heroes" (e.g., recycling) while downplaying corporate emissions.
- Distractor 1: Incorrect because the text says media does cover action (just poorly balanced).
- Distractor 2: Opposite of the text’s claim (media underreports corporate guilt).
- Distractor 3: Misinterprets "fairy tale" as a media strategy, not a metaphor for inaction.
---
Q2: How do the statistics about emissions (71% from corporations) support the author’s claim that neither individuals nor corporations can solve climate change alone?
Correct: Corporate emissions are so high that even widespread individual changes wouldn’t offset them, while corporations alone lack public pressure to reform.

Topic: an essay on the psychology of risk-taking: why some people seek danger and others avoid it

Text:
Psychologists study why some people seek danger while others avoid it. Risk-takers and risk-avoiders represent two psychological angles on the same behavior. Those who chase thrills often feel a sense of completion when they overcome challenges, like a skydiver following the instinct to conquer fear. Their loyalty to adrenaline can blind them to risks—even when their wrist or other body parts suffer injuries. Critics, or their opponents, argue that such behavior is reckless, yet risk-takers often edit their perception of danger, convincing themselves the reward outweighs the harm.

In contrast, risk-avoiders prioritize safety. They recognize their own limits and may have physical or mental disabilities that make risk-taking dangerous. External pressures, like parents or societal norms, also discourage them from taking chances. For example, someone who grew up in a strict household might avoid extreme sports out of fear of recognition—not just from others, but from themselves for failing to meet expectations.

Ultimately, the balance between risk-taking and caution depends on personality, upbringing, and even biology. While one group thrives on danger, the other ensures survival through restraint.

Questions:
Q1: Why might risk-takers feel a sense of completion after taking a risk?
Correct: They experience fulfillment from overcoming fear or challenges.
Distractors:
1. They receive financial rewards for their actions.
2. They are praised by their opponents for their bravery.
3. They edit their perception of danger to feel safer.
Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with the text’s focus on psychological achievement ("sense of completion"). Distractor 1 is wrong because the text mentions no financial incentives. Distractor 2 is incorrect because opponents criticize risk-takers. Distractor 3 confuses the effect (completion) with the process (editing perception).
---
Q2: How do risk-avoiders’ perceptions of danger differ from those of risk-takers?
Correct: Risk-avoiders see danger as a threat to avoid, while risk-takers minimize it.
Distractors:
1. Risk-avoiders take calculated risks, unlike reckless risk-takers.
2. Risk-avoiders edit their perception of danger to feel more secure.
3. Risk-avoiders are more loyal to safety than risk-takers are to thrills.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects the text’s contrast ("risk-avoiders prioritize safety" vs. "risk-takers edit perception"). Distractor 1 is wrong because risk-avoiders avoid danger entirely. Distractor 2 misassigns the editing trait to risk-avoiders. Distractor 3 incorrectly applies "loyal" to risk-avoiders.
---
Q3: What role do external pressures play in discouraging risk-taking?
Correct: Societal norms and parental influence can make people avoid risks.
Distractors:
1. External pressures encourage risk-taking by rewarding bravery.
2. People with disabilities are more likely to take risks due to external pressures.
3. Editors of safety guidelines discourage risk-taking.
Explanation:
The correct answer matches the text’s mention of parents/societal norms discouraging risk-taking. Distractor 1 reverses the relationship. Distractor 2 mislinks disabilities with risk-seeking. Distractor 3 misinterprets "edit" as referring to guidelines.
---
Q4: How does the text suggest that biology might influence risk behavior?
Correct: Innate traits, like thrill-seeking instincts, may predispose some to risk-taking.
Distractors:
1. Biology makes risk-avoiders more cautious than risk-takers.
2. People with wrist injuries are biologically prone to risk-taking.
3. External pressures like genetics discourage risk-taking.
Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with the text’s implication that innate traits (e.g., instincts) drive risk-taking. Distractor 1 overgeneralizes biology to both groups. Distractor 2 confuses cause (injuries) with effect. Distractor 3 incorrectly equates genetics with external pressures.

Correct: Wrist injuries from extreme sports.
Distractors:
1. Mental disabilities caused by reckless behavior.
2. Failure to complete thrilling tasks.
3. Recognition from opponents for taking risks.
Explanation:
The correct answer directly cites the text’s example ("wrist injuries"). Distractor 1 reverses the relationship (disabilities are pre-existing). Distractor 2 misinterprets "completion" as failure. Distractor 3 misassigns "recognition" to physical harm.



Topic: a speculative essay on what one piece of knowledge should be passed to future generations

Text:
If we could pass one piece of knowledge to future generations, it should be the tale of resilience—the concrete lesson that survival depends not on wealth, but on adaptability. Early settlers carved highways through unknown lands, only to face frequent storms, disease, and conflict. Today, we still build highways—not just of asphalt, but of ideas—yet we often forget that progress requires flexibility.

The most valuable zone for humanity is not one of material gain, but of shared wisdom. Settlers who ignored this principle collapsed; those who learned to interpret their environment endured. Wealth alone cannot make a civilization immune to collapse. Instead, future generations need the ability to see challenges as opportunities, to treat setbacks as tales of growth, not failures.

Thus, the one knowledge worth preserving is not how to accumulate resources, but how to interpret and adapt. Without this, even the grandest highways will lead to dead ends.

Topic: an essay discussing the ethics of using animals in medical research

Text:
The ethics of using animals in medical research remains a contentious issue, with strong arguments on both sides. Proponents argue that animal testing has a flavour of necessity, as it has led to life-saving drugs like insulin and vaccines. Without such research, they claim, scientific progress would stall. However, critics highlight the suffering inflicted on animals, many of which are kept in cramped, inadequate housing before experiments.

One aspect of the debate is whether alternatives—such as computer models or human cell cultures—can fare as well. While these methods are improving, they are not yet reliable enough to replace all animal testing. A brief look at history shows that public opinion has leaned toward greater animal rights, with laws tightening in many countries. For instance, some labs now face penalties akin to jail time for mistreatment, though enforcement remains inconsistent.

Scientific referees (peer reviewers) often prioritize results over ethics, raising concerns about bias. Yet, the parade of progress in animal welfare suggests a shift. Independent oversight and transparency could balance independence in research with compassion. Ultimately, the question isn’t whether animal testing should end, but how it can be conducted more humanely.

Distractors:

2. To reduce the cost of drug development.
3. Because no alternatives exist at all.
Explanation: The correct answer directly quotes the text’s example of insulin/vaccines. Distractors 1 and 2 misinterpret "necessity" as suitability/cost, while 3 ignores the mention of improving alternatives.

Distractors:
1. Animals are now guaranteed humane housing in all labs.
2. Testing has been banned entirely in most countries.
3. Scientists must now obtain public approval for experiments.
Explanation: The correct answer reflects the text’s mention of "tightening laws" and "jail" penalties. Distractors 1 and 2 overgeneralize, while 3 introduces an unsupported requirement.

Q3: How does the text describe the reliability of alternatives to animal testing?

Distractors:
1. Alternatives are already as effective as animal testing.
2. No alternatives exist.
3. Alternatives are only used for cosmetic testing.
Explanation: The correct answer captures the text’s hedging ("not yet"). Distractors 1 and 2 misread the text’s tone, while 3 is irrelevant.

Explanation: The correct answer paraphrases the text’s call for "independent oversight." Distractors 1–3 present extreme or unsupported solutions.



Topic: an article about the placebo effect

Text:
The placebo effect—where a patient’s symptoms heal simply from believing in a treatment—has demonstrated that the mind and body operate in unity. Studies establish that even sugar pills can reduce pain or depression if administered with confidence. Yet, skeptics argue that this phenomenon is merely a litter of anecdotes, ignoring decades of rigorous trials.

At its core, the placebo effect relies on the soul of trust between doctor and patient. A password of credibility must exist: if a patient doubts the treatment, its power wanes. Neuroscientists have mapped how placebos trigger dopamine release, climbing a vertical ladder of recovery in some cases. However, pharmaceutical companies wage ethical debates—should placebos be prescribed if they lack active ingredients?

Critics warn that overuse could undermine real medicine, while proponents see potential in harnessing belief for chronic conditions. The challenge lies in balancing skepticism with the undeniable fact that, for some, the placebo isn’t just a trick—it’s a ladder to healing.

Questions:

Correct: Trust acts as a "password" of credibility.
Distractors:
1. Patients lack scientific knowledge. / Wrong: The text links failure to doubt, not ignorance.

3. Doctors prescribe placebos incorrectly. / Wrong: The text implies the issue is patient doubt, not doctor error.
---

Correct: They trigger dopamine release.

Distractors:

2. A journey with obstacles. / Wrong: Too vague; the text is specific.

Topic: an essay on the widening gap between the rich and the poor and what can be done about it

Text:
The gap between the rich and the poor has widened dramatically in recent decades, threatening social stability and economic growth. While incomes vary widely—from billionaires to workers in low-paying jobs—the root causes are clear: weak conservation of resources, unfair recruitment practices, and internal corporate policies that prioritize profits over equity.

To address this, governments must implement redistributive policies, such as progressive taxation and improved job recruitment programs for marginalized groups. Experts urge policymakers to consult economists and activists to design effective solutions. Meanwhile, chief executives should adopt ethical practices, ensuring fair wages and transparent internal audits. Without these steps, inequality will persist, exacerbating social tensions.

Globally, tracking inequality metrics is critical. Organizations like the UN must collaborate to monitor progress and adjust strategies. Conservation of wealth through sustainable investments can also help bridge the divide. Ultimately, closing this gap requires urgent action—governments, corporations, and citizens must work together to create a fairer society.

By 2020, Estonia’s GDP had rebounded, proving that even in crisis, strategic investments and adaptive governance could yield long-term gains.

Topic: an article about neurolinguistics

Text:
Neurolinguistics explores how the brain processes language, revealing that speech isn’t just a skill but a consumption of cognitive energy. Studies show that even simple conversations drain neural resources, while emotionally charged words—like a nasty insult or a memorable compliment—leave stronger traces in memory. The brain’s ability to conserve neural pathways also explains why stroke patients can sometimes recover lost language functions, though this process is dependent on the integrity of the left hemisphere.

Bilingual individuals further complicate this picture by activating multiple neural networks simultaneously. Damage to these organic structures, whether from injury or disease, can disrupt language, but the brain’s plasticity often compensates. For example, fMRI scans—using radiation-free methods—reveal how neurons "sing" in harmony like a choir, synchronizing to produce coherent speech.

Yet, despite these advances, questions remain. Could AI ever replicate the brain’s organic flexibility? While machines excel at pattern recognition, they lack the adaptive conservation of human neural pathways. The interplay between biology and language continues to fascinate scientists worldwide.

Questions:
Q1: Why do emotionally charged words (e.g., insults) leave stronger memory traces than neutral ones?
Correct: They trigger stronger neural responses, enhancing memory encoding.
Distractors:
1. "They are processed faster by the right hemisphere."
2. "The brain releases more dopamine during emotional speech."
3. "Neutral words are forgotten because they lack radiation."
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches text’s focus on emotional impact ("memorable").
- Distractor 1: Text doesn’t mention hemisphere speed; confuses lateralization with memory.
- Distractor 2: Overcomplicates with neurochemistry (text uses general "neural responses").
- Distractor 3: Radiation refers to fMRI, not memory mechanisms.

1. "Bilinguals have larger left hemispheres."

- Correct: Directly quotes "multiple" vs. "single network."

- Distractor 3: Radiation is about imaging, not processing.

Correct: Neurons work like a choir singing in harmony.
Distractors:
1. "A symphony orchestra."
2. "A computer network."
3. "A single neuron."
Explanation:
- Correct: Directly quotes "choir" metaphor.
- Distractor 1: Similar but less precise ("choir" is specified).
- Distractor 2: Confuses biological (organic) with artificial systems.
- Distractor 3: Implies individuality, not collective harmony.



Topic: an essay on the economic arguments for and against raising the minimum wage

Text:
The ongoing debate over raising the minimum wage hinges on its core economic impacts. Supporters argue that higher wages reduce poverty and boost consumer spending, while critics warn of job losses and inflation. The core argument for raises is poverty reduction, but businesses claim wage hikes reduce efficient labor allocation. Workers, however, may become more loyal if wages reflect their value. Critics argue that without productivity gains, wage increases are not equivalent to economic growth. Policymakers face pressure: supporters urge action, while small businesses fear a 'wage cliff' pushing them into insolvency. A sudden hike could deal a blow to retailers, while gradual increases might avoid tearing apart the wage-inflation balance. Despite criticism, some studies show modest job growth, but the trade-offs remain contentious.

Questions:
Q1: What is the primary economic justification given in the text for raising the minimum wage?
Correct: Reducing poverty and stimulating consumer spending.
Distractors:
1. Increasing corporate profits through higher labor costs.
2. Ensuring wages are equivalent to CEO salaries.
3. Making labor markets more efficient for employers.
Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with the text’s "poverty reduction" and "consumer spending" arguments. Distractor 1 contradicts the text’s business opposition; 2 misuses "equivalent"; 3 reverses the text’s claim that wages reduce efficiency.
---
Q2: How does the text describe the potential negative impact of sudden wage hikes on small businesses?
Correct: It could push them into insolvency by creating a "wage cliff."
Distractors:
1. Sudden hikes would make workers more loyal to employers.
2. Small businesses would benefit from higher consumer demand.
3. Wage increases would improve efficient labor management.
Explanation:
The correct answer matches "wage cliff' pushing them into insolvency" and "deal a blow to retailers." Distractor 1 confuses loyalty (a pro-wage point); 2 ignores the text’s caution about timing; 3 misinterprets "efficient."
---
Q3: According to the text, what criticism is leveled against minimum wage increases?
Correct: Critics argue they are not equivalent to economic growth without productivity gains.
Distractors:
1. Critics claim wage hikes always lead to loyal workforces.
2. The criticism focuses on wages being too efficient for businesses.
3. Studies universally show wage increases cause job losses.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects "not equivalent to economic growth" and "criticism." Distractor 1 misattributes loyalty; 2 inverts "efficient"; 3 overstates the text’s cautious tone.
---
Q4: Why might gradual wage increases be preferred over sudden hikes, according to the text?
Correct: To avoid tearing apart the balance between wages and inflation.
Distractors:
1. Gradual hikes ensure wages are equivalent to inflation rates.
2. Sudden hikes make workers more loyal to employers.
3. Gradual increases are always efficient for businesses.
Explanation:
The correct answer cites "avoid tearing apart the balance." Distractor 1 misuses "equivalent"; 2 confuses loyalty with timing; 3 contradicts the text’s business concerns.
---
Q5: What compromise does the text suggest policymakers should consider regarding minimum wage?
Correct: Nuanced policy that avoids abrupt changes while prioritizing equity.
Distractors:
1. Policymakers should urge businesses to accept sudden wage hikes.
2. The text advocates for wages to be equivalent to inflation adjustments.
3. Small businesses should be exempt from minimum wage laws.
Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with "urge nuanced policy" and contrasts sudden hikes. Distractor 1 misinterprets "urge"; 2 misuses "equivalent"; 3 is unsupported by the text.



Topic: an article about inventions predicted by science fiction writers

Text:
From Sci-Fi to Reality: Inventions Predicted by Writers

Lately, scientists have been rushing to turn science fiction into reality. Many inventions once considered impossible are now part of our lives. For example, virtual reality was a central plot in The Matrix, yet today, users can interact with 3D worlds seamlessly. Similarly, digital currencies—like those described in Cyberpunk 2077—are now a prospect for global economies.

What was once plain science fiction has become sophisticated technology. AI characters, like those in Blade Runner, can now hold conversations, raising questions about human-machine interaction. Even the idea of owning a virtual estate, as seen in Ready Player One, is becoming plausible. Companies are exploring how people might take out a mortgage in a digital world, where property exists only in code.

However, challenges remain. If virtual worlds grow, how will laws apply? Could you delete your digital past entirely, erasing every mistake? These questions highlight the ethical dilemmas of turning sci-fi dreams into reality. While the prospect of living in a virtual paradise is exciting, it also forces us to reconsider what is real—and what we’re willing to lose.

Questions:
Q1: Why have scientists recently accelerated the development of technologies inspired by science fiction?
Correct: Because recent technological progress has made once-unthinkable ideas feasible, creating urgency to innovate.
Distractors:
1. Scientists are competing for funding from governments. / Wrong: The text attributes the rush to feasibility, not funding.
2. Science fiction writers have started collaborating with engineers. / Wrong: No collaboration is mentioned; the focus is on scientific progress.
3. People are demanding more entertainment-based technologies. / Wrong: The text links the rush to capability, not consumer demand.

Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with the text’s explicit reason ("rush to turn sci-fi dreams into reality"). Distractor 1 misinterprets motivation (funding vs. progress). Distractor 2 confuses actors (writers vs. scientists). Distractor 3 shifts focus from innovation to entertainment, which the text doesn’t emphasize.
---
Q2: How does the example of The Matrix illustrate the transition from sci-fi to real-world technology?
Correct: It shows how a fictional concept—virtual reality—has become a practical, widely used technology.
Distractors:
1. It shows that sci-fi can predict future wars. / Wrong: The text focuses on VR, not warfare.
2. The film’s special effects inspired modern movie-making. / Wrong: The text emphasizes VR technology, not filmmaking.
3. It proves that AI will eventually surpass human intelligence. / Wrong: The text mentions interaction, not superiority.

Explanation:
The correct answer matches the text’s point about VR’s real-world application. Distractor 1 misrepresents the film’s theme. Distractor 2 ignores the VR focus. Distractor 3 overstates the text’s claim about AI.
---
Q3: What ethical concerns arise from the possibility of owning a virtual estate or taking out a digital mortgage?
Correct: Uncertainties about legal regulations and ownership rights in digital spaces.
Distractors:
1. People might stop buying real estate entirely. / Wrong: The text doesn’t claim this will happen.
2. Virtual currencies will replace all physical money. / Wrong: The text calls it a prospect, not a certainty.
3. Governments will ban virtual property to protect the economy. / Wrong: The text raises questions, not solutions.

Explanation:
The correct answer reflects the text’s ethical dilemmas (laws, contracts). Distractor 1 assumes a definitive outcome. Distractor 2 misinterprets "prospect" as fact. Distractor 3 invents a regulatory response not in the text.
---
Q4: In what ways might the ability to "delete" one’s digital past change society?
Correct: It could reduce personal accountability by allowing people to erase records of their actions.
Distractors:
1. Crime rates would decrease because people could hide their misdeeds. / Wrong: The text doesn’t link deletion to crime.
2. Social media platforms would become obsolete. / Wrong: The text focuses on personal data, not platforms.
3. People would live longer because they could "reset" their health data. / Wrong: The text doesn’t mention health.

Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with the text’s focus on accountability. Distractor 1 overgeneralizes. Distractor 2 confuses scope (personal vs. public data). Distractor 3 introduces unrelated content.
---
Q5: According to the text, what is one key difference between early science fiction and today’s technological advancements?
Correct: Early sci-fi was often simplistic or fantastical, whereas modern tech is highly developed and practical.
Distractors:
1. Early sci-fi was always pessimistic, while modern tech is optimistic. / Wrong: The text contrasts complexity, not tone.
2. Today’s tech is only inspired by sci-fi, whereas early works were original. / Wrong: The text implies adaptation, not originality.
3. Early sci-fi focused on space travel, while modern tech is about virtual worlds. / Wrong: The text doesn’t limit early sci-fi to space.

Explanation:
The correct answer matches the text’s contrast ("plain" vs. "sophisticated"). Distractor 1 misrepresents tone. Distractor 2 reverses causation. Distractor 3 overgeneralizes from one example (The Matrix).



Topic: a sci-fi story about a child who makes friends with a robot

Text:
Liam stumbled into the abandoned lab, its walls scorched by destruction. Among the wreckage stood a robot, its metallic frame bent but still functional. Its most characteristic feature was a smooth, matching set of tools on one arm—clearly designed for precision work. Though damaged, the robot’s sophisticated AI hummed to life as Liam approached.

Curious, Liam decided to assess its capabilities. The robot could analyze data with intellectual speed but struggled with physical tasks. "Can you help me restore the power grid?" Liam asked, attempting to negotiate a task. The robot hesitated, its optical sensors flickering. "Affirmative," it replied, "but my limbs are compromised."

Determined, Liam worked alongside the robot, teaching it to adapt. At first, the robot saw itself as a tool, but Liam refused to treat it that way. He embraced its quirks, even when it made mistakes. One day, a malfunction threatened to destroy their progress. Liam’s quick thinking and the robot’s analytical skills helped them resolve the crisis.

By the end, the robot wasn’t just a machine—it was a partner. Their bond proved that even in a world of chaos, friendship could restore hope.

Questions:
Q1: Why was the robot initially abandoned in the lab?
Correct: The lab was destroyed, leaving the robot damaged and seemingly useless.
Distractors:
1. The robot was abandoned because it malfunctioned and became dangerous.
2. The robot was too advanced for the scientists to control.
3. The robot’s creator left it behind to focus on other projects.
Explanation:
- Correct: Directly matches the text’s description of the lab’s destruction.
- Distractor 1: Incorrect—no mention of danger, only damage.
- Distractor 2: Incorrect—sophisticated implies capability, not abandonment.
- Distractor 3: Incorrect—abandoned refers to the lab’s state, not intentional neglect.
---
Q2: How did Liam assess the robot’s abilities, and what was one limitation he noticed?
Correct: Liam tested its functions and realized it excelled at data analysis but had physical limitations.
Distractors:
1. Liam assessed the robot by asking it to perform complex calculations.
2. The robot’s limitation was that it couldn’t communicate verbally.
3. Liam noticed the robot was too emotional to work efficiently.
Explanation:
- Correct: Aligns with the text’s description of intellectual strengths and physical struggles.
- Distractor 1: Partially true but misses the physical limitation focus.
- Distractor 2: Incorrect—the robot did communicate ("Affirmative").
- Distractor 3: Incorrect—the robot was logical; Liam embraced its quirks, not emotions.
---
Q3: What challenge did Liam and the robot face when trying to restore the power grid?
Correct: The robot’s damaged limbs made it difficult to perform manual repairs.
Distractors:
1. The robot’s AI was too outdated to help.
2. Liam lacked the technical knowledge to guide the robot.
3. External hackers sabotaged their efforts.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches the text’s mention of compromised limbs.
- Distractor 1: Incorrect—sophisticated implies advanced AI.
- Distractor 2: Incorrect—Liam’s quick thinking suggests competence.
- Distractor 3: Incorrect—malfunction was internal, not hacking.
---
Q4: How did Liam’s attitude toward the robot change over time?
Correct: He started seeing it as more than a tool, eventually treating it as a friend.
Distractors:
1. Liam grew frustrated with the robot’s slow responses.
2. Liam decided the robot was better off left alone.
3. Liam treated the robot like a pet rather than a partner.
Explanation:
- Correct: Reflects the shift from tool to partner.
- Distractor 1: Incorrect—Liam embraced its quirks, not rejected them.
- Distractor 2: Incorrect—they formed a bond, not abandonment.
- Distractor 3: Incorrect—partnership was emphasized, not ownership.
---
Q5: What does "a true partnership" suggest about their relationship by the end?
Correct: Their collaboration was equal, blending Liam’s creativity with the robot’s technical skills.
Distractors:
1. Their partnership was one-sided, with Liam doing most of the work.
2. The robot was still just a machine, despite their bond.
3. Their partnership was temporary and would end when the lab was fixed.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches the text’s description of equal collaboration.
- Distractor 1: Incorrect—the robot contributed analytically.
- Distractor 2: Incorrect—the text says "no longer a machine."
- Distractor 3: Incorrect—restore hope implies lasting impact.



Topic: an essay about the advantages and disadvantages of being famous

Text:
Fame is a double-edged sword that offers both opportunities and challenges. On one hand, it can aid charitable causes, as celebrities often use their platform to stream funds to worthy projects. Many famous individuals become entrepreneurs, leveraging their name for business ventures, with recruitment for high-profile collaborations becoming easier. The potential for personal growth is immense, but the first step toward fame often requires suspending privacy—like handing over a password to your personal life. The public’s gaze can feel like a relentless stream of criticism, eroding self-esteem over time. Worse, the flash of fame can quickly turn into destruction, as scandals or burnout derail careers.

Questions:
Q1: How does fame aid charitable causes according to the text?
Correct: It enables celebrities to raise funds and awareness for charitable projects.
Distractors:
1. Fame forces celebrities to donate money.
2. Famous people only help causes that benefit them personally.
3. Fame allows celebrities to avoid taxes by donating.
Explanation: The text describes fame as a platform for aid, not a coercive or self-serving act.
---
Q2: What metaphor does the author use to describe the loss of privacy for famous people?
Correct: "Handing over a password to your personal life."
Distractors:
1. A locked door.
2. A broken window.
3. A public stage.
Explanation: The "password" metaphor highlights deliberate exposure, not physical or accidental loss.
---
Q3: Why might fame lead to destruction for celebrities?
Correct: Scandals or burnout can ruin careers and mental health.
Distractors:
1. Fame always leads to financial ruin.
2. Celebrities are physically harmed by fans.
3. Fame makes people lazy.
Explanation: The text links destruction to scandals and burnout, not financial loss or violence.
---
Q4: How does the text suggest fame can be beneficial for entrepreneurs?
Correct: It attracts business opportunities and collaborations.
Distractors:
1. Fame guarantees financial success.
2. Entrepreneurs must be famous to start businesses.
3. Famous people avoid business risks.
Explanation: Fame facilitates opportunities but doesn’t eliminate risks or require fame as a prerequisite.
---
Q5: What does the phrase "the flash of fame" imply about its longevity?
Correct: It suggests fame is temporary and fleeting.
Distractors:
1. Fame is always bright and positive.
2. Fame requires constant effort to maintain.
3. Fame is only for short-term projects.
Explanation: "Flash" conveys transience, not effort or positivity.



Topic: an article about gardening without soil

Text:
Urban gardening has evolved beyond soil, with hydroponics and aeroponics gaining traction among residents seeking sustainable food sources. These methods, which integrate water and nutrients directly into plant roots, offer considerable advantages: lower costs, reduced water usage, and the ability to support wildlife in compact spaces. For example, a community garden in Berlin transformed a vacant lot into a thriving hydroponic farm, generating revenue from selling produce and hosting workshops for spectators. Critics initially cried that such systems lacked the "soul" of traditional farming, but data shows yields are often higher.

Innovations like gaming-inspired apps now let users monitor plant health in real time, making hydroponics more accessible. The democratic nature of these systems—requiring minimal space or expertise—has attracted diverse participants, from students to retirees. While challenges remain (e.g., energy costs), the environmental and social benefits are undeniable. Residents are encouraged to experiment: start small, join local groups, and witness how soil-free gardening can revolutionize urban living.

Questions:
Q1: Why do hydroponic systems attract a diverse range of participants, including students and retirees?
Correct: Because they require minimal space or expertise, making them accessible to all socioeconomic groups.
Distractors:
1. They produce higher-quality food than traditional methods.
2. They are cheaper to install than traditional gardens.
3. They are promoted by government subsidies.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches the text’s emphasis on democratic accessibility.
- Distractor 1: Confuses yield (quantity) with quality.
- Distractor 2: Overgeneralizes "lower costs" to installation.
- Distractor 3: Introduces unsupported external factors.
---
Q2: How do hydroponics contribute to wildlife conservation in urban areas?
Correct: By supporting wildlife in compact spaces through efficient resource use.
Distractors:
1. They reduce pesticide use, benefiting pollinators.
2. They create habitats for rare urban species.
3. They replace soil-based gardens, which harm ecosystems.
Explanation:
- Correct: Aligns with the text’s mention of wildlife in compact spaces.
- Distractor 1: Assumes hydroponics avoid pesticides (not stated).
- Distractor 2: Overinterprets "support" as habitat creation.
- Distractor 3: Introduces unsupported criticism of soil gardens.
---
Q3: What initial criticism did hydroponic gardening face, and how was it addressed?
Correct: Critics argued it lacked the "soul" of traditional farming, but data proved higher yields.
Distractors:
1. Farmers feared job losses due to automation.
2. Scientists doubted the nutritional value of hydroponic produce.
3. Residents complained about the smell of nutrient solutions.
Explanation:

- Distractor 1: Shifts focus to labor economics (irrelevant).
- Distractor 2: Projects modern debates onto the text.
- Distractor 3: Introduces sensory complaints (not mentioned).
---
Q4: In what ways does hydroponics generate income for communities?
Correct: Through selling produce and hosting workshops for spectators.
Distractors:
1. By selling seeds and gardening equipment.
2. Through government grants for sustainable projects.
3. By charging membership fees for community gardens.
Explanation:
- Correct: Matches the text’s examples of revenue sources.
- Distractor 1: Overlooks "workshops" detail.
- Distractor 2: Introduces unsupported funding.
- Distractor 3: Confuses revenue models.
---
Q5: How does the text suggest residents can start experimenting with soil-free gardening?
Correct: By starting small and joining local groups.
Distractors:
1. By purchasing expensive hydroponic kits.
2. Through online courses offered by universities.
3. By converting their entire backyard into a hydroponic system.
Explanation:
- Correct: Reflects the text’s advice to start small.
- Distractor 1: Misinterprets "lower costs" as requiring kits.
- Distractor 2: Projects formal education (not mentioned).
- Distractor 3: Ignores "start small" recommendation.
